My friend and I were busy talking, so for a while I did not notice the child, but when I chanced to glance at her again, I was surprised to see that she was still eating. Two or three times afterward I looked at her, and finally I turned to my friend.
“I wonder what that child is eating,” I said.
“She is not eating anything,” my friend replied. “She is chewing gum.”
Again I looked at the child. She was sitting, drooped and weary, her loose hands lying in her lap, and her feet spread around her bundle in a very awkward and difficult position. As I watched her tired face, suddenly I remembered something that had happened on the train on my trip across the continent.
“Is she sick?” I asked,
“No, I think not. Why do you ask?”
“I think I took that medicine on the train,” I replied.
“Oh, no!” my friend said, laughing. “Chewing gum is not medicine. It’s a sort of wax, just to chew.”
“Why does she do it?” I asked.
“Oh, most children of her class chew gum, more or less. It’s not an elegant thing to do. I don’t allow my children to touch it.”
I said nothing more, but a partial light began to dawn upon my experience on the train. I had been uncomfortably carsick, and Mrs. Holmes had given me a small, flat block of fragrant medicine which she said would cure nausea. I put it in my mouth and chewed a long time, but I could not swallow it. After a while I got tired, but Mrs. Holmes was still eating hers, so, concluding that it must be a medicine possessing wonderful merit, as it would not dissolve, I wrapped it carefully in a piece of white tissue paper and put it in the little mirror case that I wore in my sash.
I never heard what originated this peculiar custom, but I think I never found anything odd in America for which I could not find an equivalent in Japan. Gum-chewing reminded me of *hodzuki*-blowing, a habit common among some Japanese children; and also much practised by tea house girls and women of humble class. The *hodzuki* is made from a little red berry having a smooth, tough peeling. The core is very soft and with proper care can be squeezed out leaving the unbroken peeling in the shape of a tiny round lantern. This little ball is elastic and though it has no special taste, children love to hold it in the mouth and by gently blowing the hollow shell make what they call “mouth music.” It sounds somewhat like the soft, distant croaking of a pond frog. *Hodzuki*-blowing is not beautiful music, nor is it a pretty custom, but it is neither harmful nor unclean. The worst that can be said of it is what many a nurse calls to her charge:
“Take that squeaky thing out of your mouth. It will make your lips pouty and ugly.”

===Chinese===

我们郊区有一座还没还清款的大石教堂；一个唤作「妇女援助会」的教会妇女团体，不时举办义卖会或音乐会，有时还用本地人才演一出戏，好给基金凑钱。
一天晚上，妈妈、松雄和我去听这样一场音乐会。节目单上有一支古典选段的女声独唱。唱歌的是本地一位富商有天赋的女儿，在欧洲受的音乐教育。我认识她，是个相当娴静的姑娘，嗓音柔和，举止端凝；所以音乐一起，看见她轻快而不拘地走上前，朝左右观众含笑一躬，随即带着丰富的面部表情，献上一串又高又脆的颤音和回声——在我这未经训练的耳朵听来，是一场奇妙万端的怪调，却也是我平生听过的最了不起的东西。
它留在我心里的印象，是明亮、迅疾、高亢。同我们的古典音乐恰成对照：那总叫人想起沉静的颜色、迟缓的动作、深而圆润的音。还有，同日本的大多数艺术一样，我们的音乐既要耳朵听，也要眼睛看。不然它的意味便失了。
我们的古典舞台一成不变。整个后身是一整块原色杉木壁，上面画一株巨大的矮松。地板是樟木的，光光的一片。歌者——自然永远是男人——端坐上面，纹丝不动如偶人。穿的是旧式的、色调柔淡的礼服。每人开唱之前，缓缓深深一躬，然后从容不迫地把扇子平放在身前的地板上。随即双掌覆膝，端坐不动，用歌声、用难以置信的朗诵之力，讲一段战争或传奇的美谈；身躯却全然不动，面容全然不变。
唱罢，歌者的脸常因动情而涨红；可他面不改色，一躬到地，再轻轻拾起扇子，恢复原先的静穆。听众端坐于深深的寂静里。他们也许感动落泪，也许激奋到极点；可这只有从压低的抽噎声或一声急促的叹息里才听得出。几百年来，一切高雅之事以抑制为基调；给一位歌者或古典戏曲演员最大的礼赞，便是深寂无声地受他的歌。
在美国，有一件事我始终习惯不了：拿女人和钱打趣的态度。各阶层的男男女女、报纸、小说、讲演的人，有一回甚至还有讲道的，都爱影射些逗笑的故事：说女人把钱藏在这藏在那，哄丈夫的钱，找朋友借钱，或偷偷攒下钱来派私用。这里头从不含什么不名誉的暗示。也许攒的钱是为了给客厅添新帘子，甚至给丈夫买生日礼物。这些玩笑于我是个谜——而且越久越大的谜；因为时日推移，我自己也见了一些事，使我明白：那些逗笑的故事底下，也许真垫着严肃的事实。
我们郊区小，人人关心彼此的事，我几乎个个都熟。我知道这些太太们都是有教养、有文化的人；可在她们中间，仿佛存在一种普遍的、公开承认的东西：对钱不负责任。她们个个穿得体面，为特定的事似乎总有钱；却没有一只敞开的钱袋，可以自由而负责地支配。有一回教会义卖，我管一张桌子；几位太太绕场看过各个摊位，买了几样便宜的小东西，把贵的留下，说：「我丈夫待会儿来，我叫他买。」或者：「等先生们来了，那些高价的东西才卖得动。」我从没见过日本男人替家里买什么东西，也从没有人指望他买。
有一回，我陪一位朋友买东西，她在她丈夫的办公室门口停下，向他要钱。我以为这已经够奇怪了；可更奇的还在后头——我随妈妈去参加教会太太们的集会，她们正为一件非常的用途凑一笔款。妇女援助会近来把丈夫们的钱包掏得太勤了，所以这回每个会员都许下诺言：各自带五块钱来，而这五块钱必须不问丈夫讨。我到的那次会正是交钱的一会；每位太太交钱时，都讲一讲自己这五块钱是怎样弄到的。多半是东省西省、一点一点攒下的。有一位说，她作了真正的牺牲：把一顶新帽子——钱付过了、还没戴——退回帽店，换了一顶便宜五块的。另一位卖掉了人家送的两张戏票。还有一位用极俏皮的韵文讲：她，一位穷的援助会太太，怎样把一星期的闲暇几乎用尽、又许下一星期的期，替她邻居——一位阔气的非援助会太太——的孩子们织补袜子。
这会开得有趣极了。它叫我想起我们的咏诗会；当然这个更热闹些，故事又不登大雅。我听得正高兴，忽有一位衣饰华美、漂亮伶俐的太太站起来说：她不会省钱，也不会挣钱。她既许诺不在店里的赊账上做手脚，又许诺不向丈夫要这五块钱；于是她做了剩下唯一可做的事：趁丈夫睡着，从他口袋里偷了来。
这报告引起满堂欢笑；我却难过了。她那句「那是唯一剩下的办法」一出口，所有的报告在我听来都带上了悲剧味。在这女人自由而有威仪的美国，一位有身份有教养的太太、一宅的主母、孩子的母亲，竟要么向丈夫伸手要钱，要么落进难堪的境地——这仿佛不可思议。
我离开家的时候，日本大体上还遵循旧俗：教女孩子为全家的福祉负责——丈夫也在其内。丈夫是一家之主；妻子却是家宅的主妇，照她自己的裁夺，支配家里的一切用度——房屋、饮食、儿女的衣着教育、一切社交与慈善的责任，还有她自己的衣装；衣料的质与式样，都须配得上丈夫的地位。
钱从哪里来？丈夫的进项就是为家的；妻子便是银行家。他自己要用钱，向她讨；她的体面，就在于调度得宜，给他一个合于他身份的数目。至于他的身份需要些什么，几乎不成问题；因为知道这个，本是她为妻的教育的一部分。丈夫也许耸耸肩说：「真不便」；可整座宅子和宅子的体面是他的光荣，任何有损全局的失调都是他的损失。所以家的需要居首。男人娶妻，第一为对神明与祖先尽义务；第二为家宅求一位主妇，把家和家人治理得为他增光。她治家有方，朋友便称羡他；她失了方寸，人家便怜恤他。
一切阶层莫不如此，只有大领地的领主或商界的金融巨子除外。他们家里有司账，可司账听主妇的传唤；她的需用，她的裁断高于一切。司账唯一的抗议之权，是满口赔罪地说一句：「主母只怕要透支了。」这一点拨照例尽够；因为日本女人，同一切身负其责的人一样，巴望把职分办得体面。
习俗的形式一年年失其森严；可即使在今天，人们仍相当受着旧日那种人人遵奉的成规的约束。同这些成规有显著的出入，至今仍被视为失仪。
我的祖国同我的归化之国，有些地方的标准相差这样远，我对两片土地的爱又都这样真；有时我便生出一种奇异的感觉：仿佛立在半空的一朵云上，用度量的眼光俯望着两个分离的世界。起初，我不断地想拿日本的标准，去解说每天落在我惊异的眼前的一桩桩怪事；因为这里的人，仿佛谁也不知道连最寻常的风俗的来历和意义，也不知道它们为什么存在、为什么奉行。我来自这样一片国土：那里每一种衣式、礼数里的每一个动作——实在说，生活里几乎每一件小事——都有一个不曾遗忘的缘由；美国人这种不闻不问，在我看来自是奇极了。
妈妈是取之不尽的问处；可我又迟疑着不肯多问；因为我的好奇，问的常常是些古怪的、琐屑的、无关紧要的事：为什么太太们在教堂里不摘帽子而男人们摘；那些漂亮人家墙上挂的瓷盘有什么用处；为什么客人被让进卧室的私处，请他把帽子大衣放在床——那张叫人想到睡眠和疾病的床——上；为什么人们在傍晚串门做客——那在日本是休闲的时辰；万圣节前夜和愚人节的那些笑闹胡闹是怎么兴起来的；还有那样一种古怪风俗——把礼物塞进长袜——长袜，一切衣裳里最卑微的一件——是怎么来的。
叫我觉得奇怪的是：这些风俗，在谈话里、书里、报纸上，竟从来没有一点暗示或提及。在日本，传统、民俗和象征时时处处都在人眼前。街上人们的衣着；店铺幌子上的商标；瓷器上的花纹；走街小贩的吆喝；士兵的军帽；女学生的褶裙：样样都指回一个人人熟知的来历故事。连人力车夫那条蓝白相间的窄毛巾、做工人的层叠饭盒，上面的花样也暗示着一首古诗或一段民间传说——日本孩子对它们的熟稔，就像美国孩子对鹅妈妈的小曲。
一天下午的小招待会上，一位太太同我愉快地谈起我这种凉鞋式样的鞋对脚多么合卫生，又带着不以为然的口气提起时下流行的尖头高跟鞋。
「为什么穿这样的鞋形？」我问，「是怎么兴起来的？」
「哦，没有什么为什么，」她答道，「就是一种时兴；就像——嗯，就像你们和服往左面掩。」
「可那个是有讲究的，」我说，「只有给死人穿，和服才从右边掩。」
这引起了她的兴味；我们短短谈了谈日本人在一切事上左尊于右的古怪习俗——从皇座直到打结。随后她轻轻碰了碰我腰带背后：「能告诉我这包袱是做什么用的吗？是背娃娃用的吗？」
「哦，不是，」我答道，「这是我的腰带，只是个装饰。娃娃是用一条吊床似的披巾兜着，挂在乳母肩上。」
「你这腰带的料子真美，」她说，「能问问为什么叠成这样一个扁垫，不摊开来叫人看见花纹吗？」
她像是真感兴趣，我便欣然讲了腰带的各种系法：身份、年龄、职业不同的人怎样系，不同的场合又怎样系。末了她问：「腰带里为什么用这么多料子？」
这话正中我的意；因为对日本人来说，一件东西材质的美，永远次于它的象征。我给她讲了带幅一尺二、带长一丈二的原始含义，讲它怎样体现着古代东方信仰里许许多多的神话与星占。
「真有意思，」她转身要走时说，「尤其是黄道十二宫那些；可这么华丽的锦缎，折起来藏掉那么多，太可惜了。小夫人，你自己说，」她朝我愉快地一笑，「买这么多码可爱的料子，只为白白浪费、毫无用处，岂不是作孽？」
说完她走了，身后一袭贵重的天鹅绒长裙曳在地板上。
妈妈家的家具，木料漂亮，有几件还带着雕花，起初使我像进了博物馆；可到别家一看，才发现没有一家是简朴的。许多家叫我想起库房——挤挤挨挨的，不光是椅子、桌子、画，还有数不清的小东西——小雕像、空花瓶、贝壳、配框的照片，还有真正珍稀贵重的摆设；全然不顾次序与合宜——照日本的标准——四处散放着。过了好几个月，我才扭转那个印象：这样杂陈的泛滥只是暂时的权宜，很快就会收回库房去。这些东西多半都美；可有些做的是鞋形，或是脚掌形。这仿佛是个受宠的花样；要不然，就是我不情愿的眼睛偏偏总能把它搜出来——几乎每进一户人家，我总能在镇纸、花瓶或别的什么小物件上看见它。有一回，我甚至看见一只小木鞋，用来插牙签。
世代的成见叫我对这个深恶痛绝；在日本，脚是身体最不受尊敬的部分；再美再贵的礼物，只要做成鞋履的样子，便一文不值了。
还有日本的古玩！到处都是，而且摆在最不相称的地方。饭盒饭碗摆在客厅桌上；低廉的画轴挂在雅致的墙上；神龛的锣用作饭厅的桌铃；刀镡用作镇纸；砚盒装手帕，信匣装手套；婚礼的杯盏做针碟；我甚至见过小竹痰盂用来插花。
日久天长，我这执拗的头脑多少学会了把一件东西同它的环境分开看；于是我开始用美国人的眼睛看它的艺术价值。我还养成了一个习惯：在这里一见到显然出于对日本的无知而生的荒唐事，便回想日本对外国东西的类似的荒唐。而每一次，我总能找到不止一桩，来抵这里的一桩。有一回，是一位小姐一句天真的问话逼我记起来的。她用不信的口气告诉我，她在一个关于日本的讲座里听说，衣着雅致的日本太太，有时竟把寻常廉价的绳绒桌毯披在肩上代替披巾。我只能笑着承认：几年前，那确实是流行的时兴。进口货又稀又贵；我们自己从来不用桌毯，做梦也想不到它们不是美丽的披肩。我没有勇气告诉她我自己就披过一条；不过我确实给她讲了我小时候长冈家里的一件事。
父亲从京城回来那趟，给阿石和阿金各带了一条大浴巾——带彩色边、缀长穗的土耳其巾。两个女仆怀着满腔的骄傲，披着毛巾上寺里做礼拜去了。我至今还看得见她们骄傲地走出大门的样子：两幅白巾平平整整摊在她们最好的衣裳上，崭新的硬穗在日本式的长袖上方一荡一荡。如今我看那该是一幅滑稽画了；可当时我满心赞叹；她们成了万人艳羡的对象，我觉着再自然不过。
在我试着用美国眼睛看日本东西的种种经历里，有一宗格外不调和的搭配，好几个月里都是我一场恼人的折磨。头一回拜访霍伊特太太——一座特别美丽的宅邸的女主人——我的眼睛就被一样可爱的雕花「孙手」（magonote）吸住了——日本管它叫「孙儿的手」，在美国它有个实用的名字：「痒痒挠」——正用丝绦挂在一只乌木柜的顶盖上。丝绦上，紧挨着它，还漫不经心搭着一串水晶珊瑚念珠。那小小的象牙指耙雕得精美，念珠是罕见的桃红珊瑚和无瑕的水晶；可在东方人眼里，这一切的美，全叫这古怪的搭配毁了。这好比把圣经和牙刷并排摆在客厅桌上。
我没有非议女主人的鉴赏。她一切艺术事物上的高品味无可置疑；在美国，孙手只是一件艺术品。从那个观点看，它摆得其所。这个道理我明白；可此后每次进那间屋，我总固执地把眼睛从乌木柜那边偏开。直到同女主人结下两年亲密的友谊，我才有勇气告诉她我初访她家所受的惊吓。她至今还笑我；我也笑；可此刻想起念珠和孙手已不再并挂在一起，我心里还存着一股温暖的快慰。
霍伊特太太家里，同念珠和「孙儿的手」分家一道撤掉的，还有一样东西：一幅日本风景的大彩照——不是旧版画，是现代照片。画面构图雅致、设色轻柔，很讨人喜欢；女主人把它摆在显眼的地方。她无知的眼睛只看见它的艺术美；我的心却羞得发痛。这张照片在日本任何体面人家都不会容许挂；因为那是东京一位有名的艺伎在她的行院门前拍的。「哦，日本人为什么要卖这种东西？」我战栗着问自己；可紧跟着又来一个更费解的反问：「美国人为什么要买呢？」
一天，我同朋友进城买东西。我们在电车上，对面一个小姑娘在吃东西，引起了我的注意。日本的孩子不在街上或公共场合吃东西；我那时还不知道，美国不像我们非上桌不吃，没有这个规矩。
我和朋友只顾说话，一时没留意那孩子；待偶然再望她一眼，我吃惊地发现她还在吃。之后又看了她两三回；末了我转向朋友。
「那孩子在吃什么呀？」我说。
「她什么也没吃，」朋友答道，「她在嚼口香糖。」
我又看那孩子。她坐着，无精打采，一脸倦色；两只手松松地搁在腿上，两脚很别扭地叉在她的包裹周围。望着她那张疲乏的脸，我忽然想起横穿大陆的火车上的一桩事。
「她病了吗？」我问。
「没有吧。为什么这么问？」
「我想我在火车上吃过那种药。」我答道。
「哦，不！」朋友笑了。「口香糖不是药。是一种胶质的玩意儿，专叫人嚼的。」
「她为什么要嚼？」我问。
「哦，她那一阶级的孩子多半都嚼口香糖，多少不等。这可不是雅事。我不许我的孩子碰它。」
我没有再说什么；可火车上的那段经历，已开始渐渐显出几分光亮。我那时晕车晕得难受，霍姆斯太太给了我一小方扁扁的、香喷喷的药，说治恶心。我放进嘴里嚼了半天，咽不下去。过了一阵我嚼累了，可霍姆斯太太还在嚼她的；于是我断定这必是一种具有神奇药力的药——因为它化不掉——便仔细用一张白棉纸包好，收进我腰带里随身带的小镜盒。
这种古怪风俗的来历，我始终没有听说；不过我想，美国再古怪的事，我在日本从没有找不到对等物的。嚼口香糖叫我想起吹酸浆（hodzuki）——一些日本孩子的通好，茶馆的女郎和下层妇女也多惯此道。酸浆是一粒小小的红果，皮光滑坚韧。果心极软，用心一挤便能挤出，留下不破的果皮，成一只小小的圆灯笼。这小泡有弹性，虽然并没有什么特别的味道，孩子们爱含在嘴里，轻轻吹那空壳，弄出他们所谓的「口乐」。那声音有点像远处池塘里青蛙的咕鸣。吹酸浆算不得好听的音乐，也不是好看的习尚；可它无害，也不脏。顶苛的考语，不过是许多乳母对乳儿喊的那句：
「把那个吱吱响的东西从嘴里拿出来。要把嘴唇吹得又撅又丑啦。」

## XIX Thinking / 十九 思考
===Original===

At the broad corner where our front and side porches joined was where my hammock swung. It was shaded by a big apple tree, and I used to put in a big cushion and sit Japanese fashion while I read. I could never get used to lying in it, as Mother sometimes did, but I liked to imagine that I was in an open *kago*﻿—a quiet, not a swaying one﻿—and watch for glimpses between the trees of carriages and country teams that passed occasionally on the road beyond the big evergreens and the stone wall.
From there, too, I could look across a little stretch of green, and on, through the break made in the lilac hedge by the drawbridge, to the home of our nearest neighbour. We did not have many close neighbours, for our suburb was a wide-spreading one with the houses far apart, each set in the midst of its own stretch of lawn and shrubbery. Many of these lawns were separated from each other by only a narrow gravelled path or a carriage road.
I loved these fenceless homes. In Japan I had never known of a home not enclosed by walls of stone or plaster. Even humble village huts had hedges of brushwood or bamboo. One of the odd fancies of my childhood was to imagine how wonderful it would be if, without warning, all hedges should fall and the hidden gardens be suddenly revealed to every passerby. In my American home I felt that my childhood wish had come true. The fences were all down and the flowers and grass free for all to see and enjoy. Then my mind drifted to the gardens of Japan where was shut-in beauty for the few.
I was thinking all this one pleasant afternoon as I sat in the hammock, sewing, while Mother was tying up the crimson rambler that covered part of the porch with a curtain of green.
“Mother,” I said suddenly, as a new thought came to me, “did you ever think of a Japanese woman as being in prison with the key to her cell in her pocket; and not unlocking the door because it would not be a polite thing to do?”
“Why﻿—no!” said Mother, surprised. “What are you thinking, Etsu?”
“That idea came to me the day I went to my first afternoon tea. Do you remember?”
“Yes, indeed,” said Mother, smiling. “You looked like a drooping blossom as you came up the path with Miss Helen. She said that everyone was there and that you were the ‘belle of the ball’; and then you sat down on the porch step and quietly remarked that people here were just like their lawns.

===Chinese===

我家前廊与侧廊相接的那个宽绰的拐角，便是我的吊床摇荡之处。那里有一株大苹果树遮荫，我常在吊床上放一个大靠垫，照日本人的样子跪坐着读书。躺在里面的滋味我始终学不来——妈妈偶尔倒那样躺——却喜欢想象自己坐在一乘敞开的驾笼里——一乘安安静静、不晃不摇的驾笼——从树隙间张望，看大常青树和石墙外面那条路上，偶或有马车和乡下的骡车队驶过。
从那里还能望过一小片草地，再穿过丁香花篱上由吊桥开出的豁口，望见我们最近一位邻人的家。近邻不多，因为这片郊区铺展得极广，人家相隔很远，各各坐落在自家的草坪与灌木丛当中。许多草坪之间，不过隔一条窄窄的碎石小径，或一条马车道。
我爱这些没有栅栏的家园。在日本，我还从不知道有一户人家不围着石墙或灰泥墙。便是寒素的村舍，也有柴篱竹篱。我幼时有个古怪的痴想：倘若毫无预兆，所有的篱笆一齐倒下，深藏的庭园蓦然显现在每个过路人眼前，那该多么美妙。在这美国的家里，我觉得童年的愿望实现了：栅栏尽数放倒，花草任人观赏、任人享用。随即我的心又飘回日本的庭园——那里是深锁的、供少数人享有的美。
一个惬意的下午，我坐在吊床上做针线，心里转着这些念头；妈妈正在给蔷薇绑扎枝条——那株红花攀藤蔷薇，已把半边门廊掩成一挂绿帘。
「妈妈，」一个新念头忽然袭来，我脱口说道，「你可曾把日本女人想成这样一个囚徒：开牢门的钥匙就揣在自己口袋里，却不去开，只因开了失礼？」
「哎呀——没有！」妈妈吃了一惊。「你在想什么呢，阿津？」
「那念头是我头一回赴午后茶会那天起的。您记得吗？」
「当然记得，」妈妈含笑道。「你和海伦小姐沿着小路走来时，活像一朵低垂的花。她说人到齐了，说你是『舞会的皇后』；随后你在门廊台阶上坐下，慢条斯理地说，这里的人跟他们的草坪一个样。

===Original===

I never quite understood what you meant.”
“I shall never forget that day,” I said. “All the time I was dressing to go, I pictured how the ladies would look, sitting in Mrs. Anderson’s parlour in their pretty dresses and wavy hair, talking pleasantly the way they do when we make calls. But they did not sit at all. It was like being in the street, for they all kept on their hats and gloves, and stood in groups or walked around the crowded rooms, all talking at once. I was so confused by the buzz of voices that my head was really dizzy, but it was all intensely interesting, and not exactly undignified. People asked me queer questions, but everyone was kind and everyone was happy.”
“Was it the noise and the excitement that tired you so?” asked Mother.
“Oh, no, I liked it. It was a happy noise. I liked everything. But on the way home, Miss Helen asked me to tell her about our ladies’ receptions in Japan. I could see in my mind just how everyone used to look at an anniversary celebration in my home at Nagaoka; Mother sitting so gentle and stately, and all the ladies in their ceremonial dresses, having a quietly nice time and expressing every emotion, in a kind of suppressed way, by smiles and bows and a few gestures; for at a formal gathering in Japan it is rude to laugh aloud or to move too much.”
“It is beautiful and restful,” said Mother.
“But it is not nature!” I cried, sitting upright in my excitement. “I’ve been thinking about it ever since. Our conventionality is too extreme. It is narrowing to the soul. I hate to be so happy here﻿—and all those patient, subdued women sitting hushed in their quiet homes. Our lives in Japan﻿—a man’s as well as a woman’s﻿—are like our tied-down trees, our shut-in gardens, our﻿—”
I stopped abruptly; then added slowly, “I am growing too outspoken and American-like. It does not suit my training.”
“You want to pull the fences down too suddenly, dear,” said Mother gently. “The flowers of Japan have blossomed in a shadowy garden, and a sudden, bright sunlight might kill their beauty and develop them into strong, coarse weeds. It is only morning there, now. The blossoms will grow with the light, and by noon the fences will have fallen. Don’t pull them down too suddenly.”
Mother leaned over the hammock and, for the first time, kissed me softly on the brow.

===Chinese===

我始终不大明白你这话的意思。」
「那一天我一辈子忘不了，」我说。「梳妆打扮的工夫，我一直在心里描画那些太太小姐的模样：坐在安德森太太（Mrs. Anderson）的客厅里，穿着漂亮的衣裳，鬈发微波，像我们拜客时那样斯斯文文地闲谈。谁知她们根本不坐。倒像到了大街上，人人帽不离头、手套不离手，一簇簇站着，或在拥挤的房间里走来走去，同时开口说话。那一片营营人语搅得我头都晕了；可这一切又有趣极了，倒也说不上失身份。人们问些古怪问题，可是个个和气，个个快活。」
「是那喧闹和兴奋把你累坏了吧？」妈妈问。
「哦，不，我喜欢呢。那是快乐的喧闹，我样样都喜欢。只是回家路上，海伦小姐请我讲讲日本女眷们的集会。我心里立时浮起从前长冈我家中祭庆时节的光景：母亲端坐上首，温婉庄严；满堂女眷一色的礼服，安安静静地受用着好时光，种种情绪都收在几分含蓄里，只凭微笑、鞠躬和少许手势传达出来——因为在日本的正式集会上，纵声大笑、举动太多都是失礼。」
「那真美，也叫人心里安宁。」妈妈说。
「可那不合天性呀！」我激动得坐直了身子，喊道。「我打那以后一直想着这个。我们的礼法太过火了，把灵魂都束窄了。我恨自己在这里这样快活——而那边那么多隐忍娴静的女人，还坐在静悄悄的家里一声不响。我们在日本的生活——男人的同女人的一样——就像我们捆扎起来的树、深锁的庭园、我们的——」
我猛地收住口，又慢慢添了一句：「我变得太直率、太美国气了，这同我受的教养不相称。」
「你是想把栅栏拆得太急了，亲爱的，」妈妈温温柔柔地说。「日本的花是在荫翳的园子里开的；骤然移到明亮的日光下，反倒会毁了她们的美丽，把她们养成粗壮的野草。那边如今才不过是清晨。花朵会随着天光生长，到正午，栅栏自会倒下的。别拆得太急。」
妈妈俯身向吊床，第一次轻轻地吻了吻我的额头。

===Original===

One time I went with some lady friends to see Ellen Terry in *The Merchant of Venice*. It was an afternoon performance, and after the play we went to some place and had tea. The ladies were all enthusiastic in their praise of the great actress, but I could say nothing, for that afternoon was one of the great disappointments of my life. I had been quite excited over seeing for the first time a Western actress of worldwide fame, and had formed a picture in my mind of a modest young doctor of laws, who would walk across the stage with slow-moving ceremony and with grave dignity deliver the wonderful monologue. Of course, I unconsciously pictured the Japanese ideal.
Instead, a tall figure in scarlet gown and cap, which reminded me of the dress of a Japanese clown, swept on to the stage with the freedom and naturalness that belong only to common-class people in Japan. Portia talked too loud and fast for a lady of elegance and culture, even in disguise. And the gestures﻿—oh, most of all, the vigorous, manlike gestures! I had no impression but one of shocked surprise.
The beautiful moonlight scene where Jessica meets her lover, and also the last act, where the two husbands recognize their wives, were full of too many kisses and seemed to be most indelicate. I wished I was not there to see.
In the midst of the conversation, one of the ladies, who had watched me rather curiously during the last scene, turned to me.
“Do you have love scenes on the Japanese stage?” she asked.
“Oh, yes,” I answered. “Our stage shows life as it is, and Japanese are just like other people.”
“But your face got crimson, little lady, and you looked as if you had never seen a lover before,” she said smilingly.
I explained as well as I could that for generations we have been taught that strong emotional expression is not consistent with elegance and dignity. That does not mean that we try to repress our feelings; only that public expression of them is bad form. Therefore on our stage the love scenes are generally so demure and quiet that an American audience would not be thrilled at all. But the dignified bearing of our actors has a strong effect on Japanese people, for they understand the feeling that is not shown.
“What do lovers do when they are﻿—well﻿—very enthusiastic?” asked a young lady.
“They gently turn their backs to each other,” I replied.
“Turn their backs to each other!

===Chinese===

有一回，我同几位女友去看爱伦·特里（Ellen Terry）演《威尼斯商人》。那是日场，散了戏，我们又到一处地方吃茶。太太们个个热烈赞叹那位名伶，我却一句话也说不出——因为那个下午，是我平生最大的失望之一。头一次要见西方享誉世界的女演员，我原是十分兴奋的，心里早描出一幅画：一位端庄年轻的法学博士，迈着徐缓而合乎礼数的台步走过舞台，以凝重的威仪道出那段精彩的独白。当然，我不自觉描画的是日本人心目中的典范。
谁知上场的是高挑的一个身影，猩红的袍子、猩红的帽——那行头叫我想起日本丑角的打扮——带着一股在日本唯有平民才有的自在放任，一扫便扫上台来。鲍西娅（Portia）说起话来又响又急，纵然是乔装改扮，也不像一位优雅有教养的闺秀。还有那些手势——唉，尤其是那些刚健的、男子汉似的手势！我心里除了震惊，什么印象也没留下。
杰西卡（Jessica）与情人相会的月夜一场，还有末一幕两位丈夫认出妻子的戏，接吻多得不得了，在我看来实在有伤大雅。我真巴不得自己不在场。
正谈着，方才末一场里一直颇有些好奇地打量我的一位太太，转过身来。
「日本戏里也有爱情场面吗？」她问。
「哦，有的，」我答道。「我们的戏台照人生本来的样子演，日本人跟别国人也是一样的。」
「可是你的脸都红透了，小娘子，」她笑吟吟地说，「瞧那样子，倒像你从来没有见过情人似的。」
我尽力解释说，世世代代以来，我们受的教导是：强烈的情感流露，与高雅尊严不相称。这并不是说我们存心压制情感；只是当众表露出来，便失了体统。因此我们舞台上的爱情场面大都娴静之极，美国观众看了怕是一点也不会心动的。可我们演员那端庄凝重的气度，对日本人却有极强的力量，因为他们读得出那不曾表露的情感。
「那么情人要是——呃——情热难禁的时候，怎么办呢？」一位年轻小姐问。
「他们便温文地各转过身去，背对着背。」我答道。
「各人转过身去——

===Original===

My stars!” was the very peculiar exclamation of the young lady.
In a moment she turned to me again.
“Is it really true,” she asked, “that in Japan there is no kissing﻿—even between husband and wife?”
“There is bowing, you know,” I replied. “That is our mode of heart expression.”
“But you don’t mean that your mother never kissed you!” exclaimed the young lady. “What did she do when you came to America?”
“Only bowed,” I replied, “and then she said very gently, ‘A safe journey for you, my daughter.’ ”
I had not been here long enough then to understand the odd expression that came over the faces of the ladies, nor the moment’s silence that followed before the conversation drifted into other channels.
Bowing is not only bending the body; it has a spiritual side also. One does not bow exactly the same to father, younger sister, friend, servant, and child. My mother’s long, dignified bow and gentle-voiced farewell held no lack of deep love. I felt keenly each heartthrob, and every other person present also recognized the depth of hidden emotion.
Japanese people are not demonstrative. Until late years the repression of strong emotion was carefully drilled into the mind and life of every Japanese child of the better class. There is much more freedom now than formerly, but the influence of past training is seen everywhere﻿—in art, in literature, and in the customs of daily life. With all the cheerful friendliness of everyday intercourse there is a certain stiffness of etiquette which holds in check all exuberance of expression. It dictates the ceremonies of birth and the ceremonies of death, and guides everything between﻿—working, playing, eating, sleeping, walking, running, laughing, crying. Every motion is chained﻿—and by one’s own wish﻿—with the shackles of politeness. A merry girl will laugh softly behind her sleeve. A hurt child chokes back his tears and sobs out, “I am not crying!” A stricken mother will smile as she tells you that her child is dying. A distressed servant will giggle as she confesses having broken your treasured piece of china. This is most mystifying to a foreigner, but it means only an effort to keep in the background. A display of one’s own feelings would be rudeness.
When American people judge the degree of affection between Japanese husband and wife by their conduct to each other, they make a great mistake.

===Chinese===

——我的老天爷！」那位年轻小姐发出一声好生奇特的惊叹。
片刻之后，她又转向我。
「日本当真没有接吻么，」她问，「连夫妻之间也没有？」
「有鞠躬呀，」我答道。「那便是我们传情达意的方式。」
「可你总不是说你母亲从来没吻过你吧！」那小姐叫起来。「你到美国来的时候，她怎么样呢？」
「只是鞠了一躬，」我答道，「随后她柔声说：『女儿，愿你一路平安。』」
那时我来美国不久，还看不懂太太们脸上何以掠过那样古怪的神情，也不懂随后那一阵短促的静默，之后话头才转到别处去。
鞠躬不只是俯身；它还有精神的一面。对父亲、对幼妹、对朋友、对仆人、对孩子，鞠躬并不一模一样。我母亲那深深的、庄重的一躬，和她轻声道出的送别，深挚的爱一点也不缺少。我心头一下一下都感得真切；在场的每一个人，也都认出了那深藏的情感。
日本人是不形于色的。直到近年，节制强烈的情感，仍是每一个上等人家日本孩子自幼在心灵与生活里受训的功课。如今自由比从前多得多了，可旧日教养的影响随处可以见到——在艺术里，在文学里，在日常生活的习惯里。日常往来尽管一团和气、热热络络，却总有一种礼法的拘谨，管束着一切奔放的表情。它规定生的仪节，也规定死的仪节，还主宰着生死之间的一切——做工、游玩、吃饭、睡觉、走路、奔跑、笑与哭。一举一动都上了锁链——而且是出于本人的意愿——锁链的名字叫礼貌。欢快的姑娘要笑，也是掩着衣袖轻轻地笑。受了委屈的孩子噙住眼泪，抽抽噎噎地说：「我才没有哭！」肝肠寸断的母亲告诉你她的孩子快死了，脸上还带着笑。闯了祸的女仆认错，说她打碎了你心爱的瓷件，却还吃吃地笑。这在外国人看来玄妙极了，其实不过是竭力退居人后、不显自己罢了。当众显露自己的情感，便是失礼。
美国人若凭日本夫妻彼此之间的举止，去判断他们感情的深浅，那就大错特错了。

===Original===

It would be as bad form for a man to express approval of his wife or children as it would be for him to praise any other part of himself; and every wife takes pride in conducting herself according to the rigid rules of etiquette, which recognize dignity and humility as the virtues that reflect greatest glory on the home of which she is mistress.
One other thing may explain some seeming peculiarities. The Japanese language has no pronouns, their place being taken by adjectives. A humble or derogatory adjective means “my” and a complimentary one means “your.” A husband will introduce his wife with some such words as these: “Pray bestow honourable glance upon foolish wife.” By this he simply means, “I want you to meet my wife.” A father will speak of his children as “ignorant son” or “untrained daughter” when his heart is overflowing with pride and tenderness.
I shall never forget my first experience in seeing kissing between man and woman. It was on my trip across the continent when I came from Japan. A seat near me was occupied by a young lady, very prettily dressed and with gentle, almost timid, manners. She was a young married woman returning from her first visit to her parents. I was much attracted by her free, yet modest, actions and planned how I would try to imitate her. One morning I noticed that she was dressed with unusual care, and it was evident that she was nearing the end of her journey. Finally the train began to slow down and she watched out of the window with eager interest. The train had barely come to a stand when in rushed a young man, who threw his arms around that modest, sweet girl and kissed her several times. And she did not mind it, but blushed and laughed, and they went off together. I cannot express my feelings, but I could not help recalling what my mother said to me just before I started for America: “I have heard, my daughter, that it is the custom for foreign people to lick each other as dogs do.”
There was no criticism in my mother’s heart﻿—nothing but wonder. I repeat her words only as an illustration of how an unfamiliar custom may appear to the eyes of a stranger. Years of residence in this country have taught me that the American mode of heart expression has its spiritual side, just as bowing has.

===Chinese===

做丈夫的若称许自己的妻子儿女，其失礼不亚于夸赞自己身上别的任何部位；而做妻子的也引以为荣，凡事恪守那一套森严的礼法——礼法只认尊严与谦卑为德，认它们是主妇最能为她所主持的家门增光的品德。
还有一件事，也许能解释一些看似古怪的地方。日本语言里没有代词，代词的位子由形容词来当。谦卑自贬的形容词便是「我的」，尊崇的形容词便是「您的」。做丈夫的介绍妻子，措辞是这样的：「请赐观愚妻。」意思不过是：「我想让您见见我妻子。」做父亲的提起儿女，口口声声是「愚儿」「拙女」，心里却涨满了骄傲与怜爱。
男女接吻的场面，我平生头一回亲眼看见，一辈子忘不了。那是我从日本渡美、横穿大陆的火车上。我邻座是一位少妇，衣饰十分漂亮，举止温柔，几乎带着羞怯。她是回娘家省亲归来——还是头一回——的年轻媳妇。她那既大方又娴静的仪态很叫我倾心，我暗自盘算怎样学她的样。一天早晨，我见她打扮得格外经心，显见得旅程快到头了。后来火车渐渐放慢，她兴致勃勃地望着窗外。车才停稳，一个青年男子便冲了进来，张开双臂搂住那位娴静可爱的姑娘，接连亲了她好几下。她竟不以为意，红着脸笑了，两人便一同去了。我那时的心情无从说起，只是不由得想起临来美国前母亲对我说的话：「女儿啊，我听说外国人有互相舔舐的习惯，就像狗那样。」
母亲心里没有半点贬责——唯有惊奇。我复述她的话，不过是举个例子，说明一个陌生的风俗落在异乡人眼里会是什么模样。在这个国度住了这些年，我已明白美国人传情达意的方式也有它精神的一面，正同鞠躬一样。

===Original===

I now understand that a kiss expresses kindness or gratitude, friendship or love; each of which is a sacred whisper from heart to heart.
Matsuo was very fond of Mother, and often, when he had received a new assignment of goods from Japan, he would select something especially pretty or appropriate and bring to her. Once he gave her a small lacquer box which looked something like an old-fashioned medicine case hung from the sash by people of ancient time. The outside was marked with lines corresponding to the partitions in a medicine case, but when I opened it, I saw that instead of being a succession of layers, it was an open box divided into two upright partitions to hold playing cards. The lacquer was poor and the work roughly done, but it was an ingenious idea to make a box to hold a means of pleasure in imitation of a case to hold a cure for pain.
“What original people Americans are!” I said. “But I didn’t know that lacquer was made here.”
Matsuo turned the little box over, and, on the bottom, I saw a label, “Made in Japan.”
A few days after, I went down to Matsuo’s store and he showed me whole shelves of articles called Japanese, the sight of which would have filled any inhabitant of Japan with a puzzled wonder as to what the strange European articles could be. They were all marked, “Made in Japan.” Matsuo said that they had been designed by Americans, in shapes suitable for use in this country, then made to order in Japanese factories and shipped direct to America, without having been seen in Japan outside the factory. That troubled me, but Matsuo shrugged his shoulders.
“As long as Americans want them, design them, order them, and are satisfied, there will be merchants to supply,” he said.
“But they are not Japanese things.”
“No,” he replied. “But genuine things do not sell. People think they are too frail and not gay enough.” Then he added slowly, “The only remedy is in education; and that will have to begin here.”
That night I lay awake a long time, thinking. Of course, artistic, appreciative persons are few in comparison to the masses who like heavy vases of green and gold, boxes of cheap lacquer, and gay fans with pictures of a laughing girl with flower hairpins. “But if Japan lowers her artistic standards,” I sighed, “what can she hope for from the world? All that she has, or is, comes from her art ideals and her pride.

===Chinese===

如今我懂得了：一个吻表达的是善意或感激，是友情或爱情；无论哪一样，都是心对心的神圣低语。
松雄很喜欢妈妈，每逢新从日本发来一批货，他常挑一件特别漂亮或特别合适的东西，给她送去。有一回他送她一只小小的漆盒，样子有些像古时人们拴在腰带上悬着的旧式药盒。盒面上描着与药盒分格相应的线条；可我打开一看，里面并非一层叠一层的抽屉，而是一只敞开的盒子，竖着分成两格，是装扑克牌用的。漆质粗劣，做工也草率，可这主意实在巧妙：仿着装止痛之药的盒子，造一只装取乐之物的匣子。
「美国人真会别出心裁！」我说。「可我原不知道这里也造漆器。」
松雄把小盒翻过来，盒底上贴着一张签：「日本制造」。
过了几天，我到松雄的铺子里去，他指给我看一整架一整架称作日本货的物件；任何日本人见了都要发怔，猜不透这些古怪的西洋玩意究竟是什么名堂。件件都标着「日本制造」。松雄说，这些东西的式样出自美国人的设计，样式合着此地使用的需要，再向日本的工厂订制，直接装运来美，出了厂门，在日本本国竟没有一个人见过。这叫我心里难受，松雄却耸耸肩。
「只要美国人要、美国人设计、美国人订购、美国人满意，总归有商人供货。」他说。
「可这些并不是日本东西。」
「不是，」他答道。「不过地道的东西卖不动。人家嫌太娇嫩，又不够花哨。」随后他慢慢添了一句：「唯一的救药在教育；而这教育得从这里开头。」
那天夜里，我睁着眼睛躺了很久。当然，比起喜欢绿底描金大花瓶、廉价漆盒和印着簪花笑靥的姑娘图样的艳俗折扇的大众来，有艺术眼光、懂得鉴赏的人终究寥寥。「可日本若放低了自己的艺术标准，」我叹道，「她还指望从世界那里得到什么呢？她所有的一切、她本身的一切，都来自她的艺术理想、她的骄傲——

===Original===

Ambition, workmanship, courtesy﻿—all are folded within those two words.”
I once knew a workman﻿—one who was paid by the job, not the hour﻿—to voluntarily undo half a day’s work, at the cost of much heavy lifting, just to alter, by a few inches, the position of a stepping-stone in a garden. After it was placed to his satisfaction, he wiped the perspiration from his face, then took out his tiny pipe and squatted down, near by, to waste still more unpaid-for-time in gazing at the reset stone, with pleasure and satisfaction in every line of his kindly old face.
As I thought of the old man, I wondered if it was worth while to exchange the delight of heart-pride in one’s work for﻿—*anything*. My mind mounted from the gardener to workman, teacher, statesman. It is the same with all. To degrade one’s pride﻿—to loose one’s hold on the best, after having had it﻿—is death to the soul growth of man or nation.

===Chinese===

——抱负、手艺、礼敬，尽收纳在这两个词里。」
我认得一个工匠——论件计酬、不按钟点的——竟自愿把半天的活计拆掉，费许多搬抬的重力气，只为把庭园里一块踏脚石挪动几寸。摆得称心了，他抹一把脸上的汗，掏出小小的烟管，蹲在一旁，再搭上些分文不取的工夫，端详那块重新安放的石头，慈祥的老脸上每一道纹路都是欢喜与满足。
想起这位老人家，我不禁自问：把对自家活计引以为傲的那份心头欢喜，拿去换取——随便什么东西——可值得？我的心思从园艺匠升及工匠，升及教师，升及政治家。都是一样。辱没自己的骄傲——曾经握住至美、再松手放开——无论于人于国，都是灵魂生长的死。

## XX Neighbours / 二十 邻居
===Original===

When I came to America I expected to learn many things, but I had no thought that I was going to learn anything about Japan. Yet our neighbours, by their questions and remarks, were teaching me every day new ways of looking at my own country.
My closest friend was the daughter of a retired statesman, the General, we called him, who lived just across the steep little ravine which divided our grounds from his. Our side was bordered by a hedge of purple lilacs, broken, opposite the path to the well, by a rustic drawbridge. One autumn afternoon I was sitting on the shady step of the bridge with a many-stamped package in my lap, watching for the postman. Just about that hour his funny little wagon, looking, with its open side-doors, like a high, stiff *kago*, would be passing on its return trip down the hill, and I was anxious to hurry off my package of white cotton brocade and ribbons of various patterns and colours﻿—the most prized gifts I could send to Japan.
Suddenly I heard a gay voice behind me reciting in a high singsong:

> “Open your mouth and shut your eyes

And I’ll give you something to make you wise.”

===Chinese===

来美国时，我料到要学许多东西；却没想到还要学关于日本的东西。可我们的邻居们，用他们的提问和议论，天天教我用新的眼光看自己的祖国。
我最亲近的朋友，是一位告老的政界人士的女儿；我们唤他「将军」。他家就在隔开两家园子的那条陡峭的小溪谷对岸。我们这边界着一道紫丁香的树篱；正对着通往水井的小径，开了一个口子，架着一座朴拙的吊桥。一个秋日的午后，我捧着一件贴满邮票的大包裹坐在桥阶背阴的一头，等邮差。约莫正是那个时辰，他那滑稽的小马车——敞着侧门，活像一乘高而僵硬的驾笼——回程下山要打这里过；我急着把这包白棉锦缎和各式各样的缎带发走——我能寄往日本的最贵重的礼物。
忽然，背后一个欢快的声音，高声拖腔地念道：

> 「张开嘴，闭上眼，

我给你一样东西叫你变聪明。」

===Original===

I looked up at a charming picture. My bright-eyed friend, in a white dress and big lacy hat, was standing on the bridge, holding in her cupped hands three or four grape leaves pinned together with thorns. On this rustic plate were piled some bunches of luscious purple grapes.
“Oh, how pretty!” I exclaimed. “That is just the way Japanese serve fruit.”
“And this is the way they carry flowers,” she said, putting down the grapes on the step and releasing a big bunch of long-stemmed tiger lilies from under her arm. “Why do Japanese always carry flowers upside-down?”
I laughed and said, “It looked very odd to me, when I first came, to see everybody carrying flowers with the tops up. Why do you?”
“Why﻿—why﻿—they look prettier so; and that’s the way they grow.”
That was true, and yet I had never before thought of anyone’s caring for the appearance of flowers that were being carried. We Japanese have a way of considering a thing invisible until it is settled in its proper place.
“Japanese seldom carry flowers,” I said, “except to the temple or to graves. We get flowers for the house from flower-venders who go from door to door with baskets swung from shoulder poles, but we do not send flowers as gifts; and we *never* wear them.”
“Why?” asked Miss Helen.
“Because they wither and fade. And so, to send flowers to a sick friend would be the worst omen in the world.”
“Oh, what a lot of pleasure your poor invalids in hospitals are losing!” said Miss Helen. “And Japan is the land of flowers!”
Surprised and thoughtful, I sat silent; but in a moment was aroused by a question. “What were you thinking of when I came﻿—sitting here so quietly with that big bundle on your lap? You looked like a lovely, dainty, picturesque little peddler.”
“My thoughts were very unlike those of a peddler,” I replied. “As I sat here watching the dangling end of the bridge chain I was thinking of a Japanese lover of long ago who crossed a drawbridge ninety-nine times to win his ladylove, and the one hundredth time, in a blinding snowstorm, he failed to see that it was lifted, and so fell to his death in the moat below.”
“How tragic!” exclaimed Miss Helen. “What did the poor lady do?”
“It was her fault,” I said. “She was vain and ambitious, and when she saw a chance to win the love of a high official at court, she changed her mind about her lover and commanded her attendants not to lower the bridge the day he expected to come triumphant.”
“You don’t mean that the cold-blooded creature actually planned his death?”
“It was the storm that caused his death,” I said. “She was fickle, but not wicked. She thought that when he found the bridge lifted he would know her answer and go away.”
“Well, sometimes our girls over here are fickle enough, dear knows,” said Miss Helen, “but no American woman would ever do a thing like that. She was actually a murderess.”
I was shocked at such a practical way of looking at my romantic tale, and hastened to add that remorseful Lady Komachi became a nun and spent her life in making pilgrimages to various temples to pray for the dead. At last she partially lost her mind, and, as a wandering beggar, lived and died among the humble villagers on the slopes of Mount Fuji. “Her fate is held up by priests,” I concluded, “as a warning to all fickle-minded maidens.”
“Well,” said Miss Helen, drawing a deep breath, “I think she paid pretty dearly for her foolishness, don’t you?”
“Why﻿—well, perhaps,” I replied, rather surprised at the question, “but we are taught that if a woman so loses her gentle modesty that she can treat with scorn and disrespect the plea of a loyal lover, she is no longer a worthy woman.”
“Suppose a man jilts a maid, what then?” quickly asked Miss Helen. “Is he no longer considered a worthy man?”
I did not know how to reply. Instinctively I upheld to myself the teachings of my childhood that man is the protector and guide and woman the helper﻿—the self-respecting, but nevertheless, uncritical, dutiful helper. Often afterward Miss Helen and I had heart-to-heart talks in which her questions and remarks surprised and sometimes disturbed me. Many of our customs I had taken for granted, accepting the ways of our ancestors without any thought except that thus they had been and still were. When I began to question myself about things which had always seemed simple and right because they were in accordance with laws made by our wise rulers, sometimes I was puzzled and sometimes I was frightened.
“I am afraid that I am growing very bold and man like,” I would think to myself, “but God gave me a brain to use, else why do I have it?” All my childhood I had hidden my deepest feelings. Now again it was the same. My American mother would have understood, but I did not know; and so, repressing all outward signs, I puzzled my way alone, in search of higher ideals﻿—not for myself, but for Japan.
Miss Helen’s father was ninety years old when I knew him. He was a wonderful man, tall, with broad shoulders just a trifle stooped and with thick iron-gray hair and bushy eyebrows. A strong face he had, but gentle and humorous when he talked. I looked upon him as an encyclopedia of American history. I had always loved the study of history, in childhood and at school, but I had learned little of the details of America’s part in the world; and would sit with the General and his invalid wife listening by the hour while he told stories of early American life. Knowing that incidents of personal history especially appealed to me, he once told me that his own large estate was bought by his father from an Indian chief in exchange for one chair, a gun, and a pouch of tobacco; and that Mother’s large home was once an Indian village of bark tents and was purchased for half-a-dozen split-seated kitchen chairs. These incidents seemed to me almost prehistoric; for I had never known anyone whose home did not date back into a far past.
When America was a still youthful nation the General had represented his country as a diplomat in Europe, and, with his beautiful young wife, had taken part in the foreign social life in Paris and later in Washington. My first glimpse of American life abroad, I received through the word pictures of this gracious lady, and through her experiences I began to understand, with sympathy, something of the problem in Japan of Americans trying to understand the Japanese, which heretofore I had looked upon only as the problem of Japanese trying to understand Americans.
From childhood until I met the General the word “ancient” had commanded my reverence. I had been conscious that the Inagaki family tree was rooted in a history centuries old, and that our plots in the cemetery were the oldest in Nagaoka. It had seemed an unquestioned necessity that we should follow the same customs that our ancestors had observed for hundreds of years, and it was my pride that they were the customs of a dynasty which was among the very oldest in the world.
After I became acquainted with the General and heard him talk of the wonderful development of a nation much younger than my own family tree, the word “ancient” lost some of its value. Even the General’s own lifetime﻿—the years of only one man’s life﻿—represented such a marvellous advance in national growth that sometimes I looked upon him almost with awe, wondering how much real value should be attached to antiquity. “Perhaps,” I sometimes said to myself, “it would be better not to look back with such pride to a glorious past; but instead, to look forward to a glorious future. One means quiet satisfaction; the other, ambitious work.”
One evening, after Matsuo and I had been over to call on the General, Miss Helen walked back with us across the drawbridge. Matsuo went on to join Mother on the porch, and Miss Helen and I sat down on the step of the bridge, as we often did, to talk.
“When Father told that story about Molly Pitcher,” said Miss Helen, “I wondered if you were thinking about Japanese women.”
“Why?” I asked.
“Well,” she replied hesitatingly, “several times I’ve heard you say that American women are like Japanese. I don’t see that Molly Pitcher is much of a Japanese specimen.”
“Oh, you don’t know Japanese history,” I exclaimed. “We have many women heroes in Japan.”
“Yes, of course,” said Miss Helen quickly. “In every country there are heroic women who rise to noble sacrifice on occasion. But they are exceptions. Books and travellers all speak of Japanese women as being quiet, soft-spoken, gentle, and meek. That picture doesn’t apply to the American type of women.”
“The training is different,” I said, “but I think that at heart they are much the same.”
“Well,” said Miss Helen, “when it becomes the fashion for us to wear our hearts on our sleeves, perhaps we will appear gentle and meek. But,” she added as she rose to go, “I don’t believe that Japanese men think as you do. Tonight, when I spoke of the book on Japan that I have been reading, and said that I believed the author was right when he declared that ‘for modesty and gentle worth, Japanese women lead the world,’ your husband smiled and said, ‘Thank you,’ as if he thought so too.”
“Miss Helen,” I said earnestly, “although our women are pictured as gentle and meek, and although Japanese men will not contradict it, nevertheless it is true that, beneath all the gentle meekness, Japanese women are like﻿—like﻿—volcanoes.”
Miss Helen laughed.
“You are the only Japanese woman that I ever saw﻿—except at the Exposition,” she said, “and I cannot imagine your being like a volcano. However, I’ll give in to your superior knowledge. You have had Molly Pitchers among your women, and flirts﻿—that Lady What’s-her-name whom you told me about the other day: she *was* a flirt, with a vengeance!﻿—and now you say that you have volcanoes. Your demure-appearing countrywomen sees to have surprising possibilities. The next time I come over I’m going to challenge you to give me a specimen of a Japanese genuine woman’s-rights woman.”
“That is easy,” I said, laughing in my turn. “A genuine woman’s-rights woman is not one who *wants* her rights, but one who *has* them. And if that means the right to do men’s work, I can easily give you a specimen. We have a whole island of women who do men’s work from planting rice to making laws.”
“What do the men do?”
“Cook, keep house, take care of the children, and do the family washing.”
“You don’t mean it!” exclaimed Miss Helen, and she sat down again.
But I did mean it, and I told her of Hachijo, a little island about a hundred miles off the coast of Japan, where the women, tall, handsome, and straight, with their splendid hair coiled in an odd knot on top of the head, and wearing long, loose gowns bound by a narrow sash tied in front, work in the ricefields, make oil from camellia seeds, spin and weave a peculiar yellow silk which they carry in bundles on their heads over the mountains, at the same time driving tiny oxen, not much larger than dogs, also laden with rolls of silk to be sent to the mainland to be sold. And in addition to all this they make some of the best laws we have and see that they are properly carried out. In the meantime, the older men of the community, with babies strapped to their backs, go on errands or stand on the street gossiping and swaying to a singsong lullaby; and the younger ones wash sweet potatoes, cut vegetables, and cook dinner; or, in big aprons and with sleeves looped back, splash, rub, and wring out clothes at the edge of a stream.
The beginning of this unusual state of things dates back several centuries, to a time when the husbands and sons were forced to go to another island about forty miles away, for fishing, very little of which could be done near Hachijo. When silk proved more profitable than fish, the men returned to the island, but the Government was in capable hands which have never given up their hold.
I told all this to Miss Helen, and closed by saying, “A subject for your meditation is the fact that with these women rulers, both men and women are healthy and happy; and the social life there is more strictly moral than it is in any other community of equal intelligence in Japan.”
“You had better join the Equal Suffrage party,” said Miss Helen, “and go on the lecture platform with that story. It has a list toward moral uplift and might win voters for the cause. Well,” and again she rose to go, “your women are such unexpected creatures that I am more than ever convinced that American women are not like Japanese. We talk so much and are so noisily interested in public affairs that we are expected to do almost anything. Whatever happens, we cannot surprise the world. But for one of your timid, shrinking kind suddenly to burst out into a bold, strong act, like lifting drawbridges and that sort of thing, completely upsets our preconceived ideas. And then to hear of its being quietly but effectively done en masse, like those island women, is rather﻿—disconcerting.”
She ran over the bridge, calling back, “Anyway, although you are the sweetest little lady that ever walked on sandals, you haven’t convinced me. American women are *not* like Japanese women﻿—more’s the pity!”
With this absurd compliment from my extravagantly partial friend ringing in my ears, I started to walk toward the porch, when suddenly a voice called from the dusky shadows across the bridge, “Oh, I didn’t think of Mrs. Newton! I’ll give up. *She* is like a Japanese woman. Good night.”
I smiled as I walked on toward the porch, for I was thinking of something Mother had told me that very morning about Mrs. Newton. She was our nearest neighbour on the opposite side of our place from Miss Helen’s home, and I knew her very well. She was a gentle woman, soft-voiced and shy, who loved birds and had little box-houses for them in her trees. I understood why Miss Helen should say that she was like a Japanese woman, but I had never thought that she was. Her ideas were so very sensible and practical; and she allowed her husband to be too attentive to her. He carried her cloak and umbrella for her; and once, in the carriage, I saw him lean over and fasten her slipper strap.
What Mother had told me was that, a few days before, Mrs. Newton was sitting by the window sewing, when she heard a frightened chirping and saw a large snake reaching up the trunk of a tree to one of her bird-boxes on a low branch. She dropped her sewing, and running to a drawer where her husband kept a gun, she shot through the open window, right into the snake’s head, and her little bird family was saved.
“How could she do it?” I said to Mother. “I never would have believed that frail, delicate Mrs. Newton would dare even *touch* a gun. She is afraid of every dog on the street, and she starts and flushes if you speak to her unexpectedly. And then, anyway, how could she ever *hit* it?”
Mother smiled.
“Mrs. Newton can do many things that you don’t know about,” she said. “When she was first married she lived for several years on a lonely ranch out West. One stormy night, when her husband was gone, she strapped that same gun around her waist and walked six miles through darkness and danger to bring help to an injured workman.”
I recalled Mrs. Newton’s soft voice and gentle, almost timid manner. “After all,” I said to myself, “she *is* like a Japanese woman!”

===Chinese===

我抬头看见一幅动人的画。我那位眼睛明亮的的朋友，一身白裙，戴一顶大花边帽，正站在桥上；她捧着的双手里，托着三四片用刺针别在一起的葡萄叶。这只天然的叶盘上，堆着几串甘美的紫葡萄。
「呀，多好看！」我叫道，「日本人待客的水果，正是这样摆的。」
「她们拿花，是这个样子，」她说着，把葡萄搁在桥阶上，又从胁下放出一大束长梗的卷丹。「为什么日本人拿花总是头朝下？」
我笑了，说：「我刚来的时候，看见人人拿花都是头朝上，也觉得怪极了。你们为什么要朝上？」
「为什么——为什么——那样好看些；花本来就是这么长的呀。」
这话不错；可我从来没有想过，会有人讲究拿花时的样子。我们日本人有这样一个习性：一样东西没有安顿到它应在的位置上，便视同未见。
「日本人很少拿花，」我说，「除了上寺里或上坟。家里的花，向走街串巷、肩挑花筐的卖花娘子买；我们不把花当礼物送人；更从来不戴花。」
「为什么？」海伦小姐问。
「因为花要枯要谢。给生病的朋友送花，是天底下最坏的兆头。」
「哎呀，你们医院里可怜的病人，错过了多少乐趣啊！」海伦小姐说，「日本还偏偏是花之国！」
我又惊又想，坐着没作声；顷刻又被一个问题唤醒：「我来的时候你在想什么——抱着那么大一个包，安安静静坐在这儿？活像一位可爱、雅致、如画的小货郎。」
「我的心事可同货郎大不一样，」我答道，「我坐在这儿，看着吊桥铁链荡来荡去的一头，正想着古时日本的一个恋人：他为赢得心上人，九十五回跨过一座吊桥；到第一百回，遇上漫天大雪，没看见桥已经吊起，坠进桥下的壕里摔死了。」
「多惨！」海伦小姐喊道，「那位可怜的小姐后来怎样？」
「那是她的错，」我说，「她虚荣，又有野心；见有机会赢得朝廷一位大官的爱，便对情人变了心，吩咐仆从，在他以为准能凯旋而来的那天，不许放下吊桥。」
「你总不是说，那个冷血的东西真是有心害死他吧？」
「害死他的是那场风雪，」我说，「她薄情，却并不狠毒。她想着，他见桥吊着，自然明白她的答复，便会走开。」
「唉，我们这边的姑娘有时候也够薄情的，天知道，」海伦小姐说，「可哪个美国女人也做不出这样的事。她实实在在是个凶手。」
这番看故事的讲求实际的法子叫我吃了一惊；我赶紧补说，悔恨的小町夫人后来出家做了尼姑，一生巡拜各地寺院，为亡人祈祷；末了精神有些失常，做了个流浪的乞婆，在富士山坡上卑贱的村民中间了却一生。「她的下场，」我收尾道，「被僧人们悬为鉴戒，警诫一切三心二意的少女。」
「唉，」海伦小姐深深吸了口气，「我想她为自己的愚蠢付的代价也够重的了，你说呢？」
「呃——唔，也许，」我答道，对这问题颇感意外，「可我们受的教诲是：女人若失去了温婉的矜持，竟至轻侮忠心恋人的恳求，她就不再是一个配得上敬重的女人。」
「要是男人负了姑娘呢，那又怎样？」海伦小姐立刻问，「他还算不算一个配得上敬重的男人？」
我不知道怎么回答。我本能地护持着童年的教训：男人是保护者和引领者，女人是辅佐者——自尊的、却不置可否的、尽本分的辅佐者。此后，海伦小姐同我常有知心的长谈；她的提问和议论，有时叫我惊奇，有时叫我不安。我们的许多风俗，我向来视为当然，先人怎样、如今还怎样，照收不疑。待我开始就这些事自问——它们一向显得简单而正当，因为合乎我们贤明统治者立的法——我有时困惑，有时害怕。
「我怕自己是变得太大胆、太像男人了，」我常对自己说，「可上帝既然给了我一个脑子叫人用，不然为什么给我？」整个童年，我都把最深的情感藏着。如今还是一样。我的美国母亲本会懂的，可我不知道；于是我按住一切外在的表露，独自一人，在困惑里摸索前行，寻求更高的理想——不是为我自己，是为日本。
海伦小姐的父亲，我认识他时已九十岁。他是个了不起的人，高个儿，宽阔的肩膀微微伛了一点，一头浓密的铁灰发，两道浓眉。一张刚毅的脸，一开口却温和而幽默。我把他看作一部美国历史的活百科。我从小就爱读史，在学校也爱；可美国在世界上那一页的细节，我知道得很少；我便常同将军和他多病的太太坐在一起，一听就是几个钟头，听他讲早期美国生活的故事。他知道个人的往事尤其能打动我，有一回告诉我：他自己那份大的产业，是他父亲用一把椅子、一杆枪和一袋烟草，从一位印第安酋长手里换来的；而妈妈那座大宅的地界，从前是一座树皮帐篷的印第安村落，是花半打劈柴面的厨房椅买下的。这些事在我听来几乎像洪荒远古；因为我从前认识的人，没有一个的家世不追溯到遥远的过去。
美国还是个年轻国家的年代，将军曾出使欧洲做外交官，携着他年轻美丽的太太，出入巴黎的外交界社交，后来又到华盛顿。我对美国人在海外生活的第一瞥，便是从这位雍容夫人的口述里得来的；借着她的经历，我开始带着同情懂得：在日本，美国人设法了解日本人，是怎样一个问题——而此前我只把它看作日本人设法了解美国人的问题。
从童年直到结识将军，「古老」这个词在我心中一向是令人敬畏的。我知道稻垣家的家谱扎在几百年的历史里；我们家的墓地在长冈是最老的。遵奉先人几百年来的旧俗，仿佛是天经地义；而那些习俗属于世界上最古老的王朝之一，正是我的骄傲。
同将军相熟、听他讲一个比我家家谱还年轻许多的国家怎样惊人地发达之后，「古老」二字失了几分分量。单是将军的一生——一个人一生的年月——便代表着国家发展中那样惊人的迈进，以致我有时望着他，近乎敬畏，心里盘算：古老到底该值几分。「也许，」我有时对自己说，「与其这样骄傲地回望光荣的过去，不如向前望着光荣的将来。一个意味着安静的满足；另一个，意味着奋发的工作。」
一天傍晚，松雄和我到将军家做客回来，海伦小姐同我们一道走过吊桥。松雄先去了游廊上陪妈妈；海伦小姐同我照常在桥阶上坐下谈话。
「爸爸讲莫莉·皮彻那段故事的时候，」海伦小姐说，「我一直在想，你是不是在想日本的女性。」
「为什么？」我问。
「唔，」她迟疑地答道，「好几回听你说，美国女人同日本女人相像。我看莫莉·皮彻可不大像日本人。」
「哦，你不了解日本的历史，」我叫道，「日本有许多女英雄。」
「是，当然，」海伦小姐立刻说，「哪个国家都有临大事挺身赴义的女子。可那是例外。书籍和旅行家都讲，日本女子安静、语声轻柔、温文、柔顺。这幅画像对美国类型的女人可不管用。」
「训练不同，」我说，「可我想，心底下是一样的。」
「唉，」海伦小姐说，「等哪天时兴把心挂在袖口上，我们大概也就显得温文柔顺了。不过，」她起身要走时又添了一句，「我不信日本男人同你想的一样。今晚我说起我正在读的那本讲日本的书，说作者宣称『论谦静贤淑，日本女性举世第一』，我相信他说得对——你丈夫微微一笑，说了声『谢谢』，仿佛他也这样想。」
「海伦小姐，」我恳切地说，「我们的女性尽管被描成温文柔顺，日本男人也决不反驳；可千真万确：在那一切温婉柔顺底下，日本女人像——像——火山。」
海伦小姐笑了。
「你是我见过的唯一的日本女人——博览会上见过的不算，」她说，「我实在想不出你像火山。不过，我服从你的权威。你们的女性里有过莫莉·皮彻，有过卖弄风情的人——就是前几天你讲的那位什么夫人：她可真是个了不得的卖弄风情的人！——如今你又说有火山。你们那些外表娴静的女同胞，看来藏着惊人的可能。下回我过来，要考考你：给我举个例子，一个地道的日本女权女人。」
「那容易，」我轮到我笑了，「地道的女权女人，不是想要她的权利的女人，而是拥有权利的女人。倘若那意味着有权做男人的活，我随手就能给你举一例。我们有一整座岛的女人，做男人的活——从种稻直到立法。」
「那男人做什么？」
「做饭，管家，带孩子，洗全家的衣服。」
「你不是当真的吧！」海伦小姐喊道，又坐了回去。
我可当真；我给她讲了八丈岛——离日本海岸约一百英里的一座小岛。那里的女人，高挑、标致、挺拔，一头好发在头顶挽成别致的髻，穿宽松的长袍，胸前系一条窄带。她们下稻田，用山茶籽榨油，纺一种特别的黄丝、织黄丝；成捆的丝顶在头上翻山越岭，同时赶着小得比狗大不了多少的牛，牛身上也驮着绸卷，要送到大陆去卖。还不止这些：她们制定我们一些最好的法律，还监督切实执行。与此同时，社里的老汉们，背上捆着娃娃，出门跑腿，或站在街上闲谈，身子一摇一晃哼着催眠的调子；年轻的洗红薯、切菜、做午饭；或者系着大围裙、绾起袖子，在溪边哗哗地搓洗、拧衣。
这番不同寻常的景象，起头在几百年前：丈夫和儿子们被迫到约四十英里外的另一座岛捕鱼——八丈附近捕不得什么。后来养丝比捕鱼有利，男人们回到岛上；可政府稳稳在女人那双从没撒过手的手里。
我把这一切讲给海伦小姐听，末了说：「有一桩事够你参详：在这些女人当政的地方，男女都健康而快乐；那里的社会风气，比日本任何同等智识的社区都更严谨纯正。」
「你不如去加入平权选举党，」海伦小姐说，「带着这个故事上讲台。它有劝善的倾向，说不定还能替这事业拉到选票。好了，」她又起身要走，「你们的女性是这样出人意料，我比先前更信美国女人不像日本女人。我们话多，对公众事务闹哄哄地热心，人家指望我们什么都干得出来。无论出什么事，我们都惊动不了世界。可你们那种腼腆畏缩的族类，忽然爆出一桩大胆刚烈的行为——像放下吊桥那一类——就全盘推翻了人家的成见。再听说这事还是悄悄的、却干脆利落地众心齐力干出来的，就像那些岛上的女人——这就有点——叫人发窘了。」
她跑过桥去，回头喊道：「反正，你虽然是穿草鞋走路的人里最甜美的小夫人，你可没有说服我。美国女人就是不像日本女人——可惜呀！」
这位偏心得没边的朋友的荒唐恭维还在我耳边响，我朝游廊走去；忽然，桥那边暗影里传来一声：「哦，我没想到牛顿太太！我认输了。她像日本女人。晚安。」
我笑着走向游廊；因为我想起就在那天早晨，妈妈给我讲的牛顿太太的事。她是我们家另一侧最近的邻居，我同她很熟。她是个温柔的女人，嗓音轻软，性子怕羞，爱鸟，在树上给鸟挂了小木屋。海伦小姐为什么说她像日本女人，我懂得；可我从不觉得她像。她的见解那样明白务实；又许丈夫对她太殷勤。他替她拿斗篷、拿伞；有一回在马车里，我还看见他俯身替她扣凉鞋的搭襻。
妈妈讲的是：几天前，牛顿太太坐在窗边做针线，忽听一阵惊惶的啁啾，抬头看见一条大蛇正顺着一根树干往上探，目标是低枝上她的一只鸟屋。她撂下针线，奔到丈夫存枪的抽屉前，隔着敞开的窗口就是一枪，正中蛇头；她的小小鸟一家得救了。
「她怎么下得去手？」我对妈妈说，「我怎么也不信，纤弱斯文的牛顿太太竟敢碰一碰枪。街上条条狗她都怕；冷不防同她说话，她都要一惊一红。再说，她怎么打得中呢？」
妈妈笑了。
「牛顿太太会做的事多着呢，你不知道，」她说，「她刚结婚那几年，独自住在大西部一座偏远的牧场上。一个风暴夜，丈夫不在家；她把那杆枪挎在腰上，摸黑走了六英里，穿过重重危险，给一个受了伤的工人讨救兵去了。」
我想起牛顿太太轻软的嗓音和那近乎怯懦的温柔举止。「说到底，」我对自己说，「她真像日本女人！」

## XXI New Experiences / 二十一 新的阅历
===Original===

As the weeks and the months had drifted by, unconsciously in my mind the present had been linking itself more and more closely with the past; for I had been learning more clearly each day that America was very like Japan. Thus, as time passed, the new surroundings melted into old memories and I began to feel that my life had been almost an unbroken continuation from childhood until now.
Beneath the chimes of the church bells calling: “Do not﻿—forget﻿—to thank﻿—for gifts﻿—you ev﻿—ery day﻿—enjoy,” I could hear the mellow boom of the temple gong: “Protection for all﻿—is offered here﻿—safety is within.”
The children who, with their burden of books, filled the streets with laughter and shouts at 8:30 a.m. made the same picture to me as our crowds of boys in uniform and girls in pleated skirts and shining black hair, who, at 7:30 a.m., clattered along on wooden clogs, carrying their books neatly wrapped in squares of patterned challie.
Valentine’s Day with its lacy scenes of bowing knights and burning hearts, all twined about with ropes of rosebuds, and with sweet thoughts expressed in glowing, endearing words, was our Weaving Festival, when swaying bamboos were decorated with festoons of gay sashes and scarfs, and hung with glittering poem prayers for sunshine, that the herdsman and his weaver wife might meet that day on the misty banks of the Heavenly River which Americans call the Milky Way.
Decoration Day, with its soldiers of two wars, with its patriotic speeches and its graves with tiny flags and scattered blossoms, was our Shokonsha memorial to our soldier dead, when, all day long, hundreds march through the great stone arch to bow with softly clapping hands; then march away to make room for hundreds more.
The Fourth of July with its fluttering flags, with snapping crackers, with beating drums and its whirling, shooting rockets in the sky, was our holiday on which the flag of Japan waved beneath crossed cherry branches in honour of the coming to the throne, twenty-five centuries ago, of our first Emperor﻿—a large bearded man in loose garments, tied at wrist and ankle with twisted vines, and wearing a long, swinging necklace of sickle-shaped gems which is today one of the three treasures of the throne.

===Chinese===

几个星期、几个月悄然漂过，不知不觉间，我心底里的「现在」同「过去」越连越紧；因为我一天比一天更明白：美国实在很像日本。于是随着时光流转，新的环境渐渐融进旧的记忆里，我开始觉得，我这一生几乎是从童年一路不曾间断地延续到了今天。
教堂的钟乐悠悠唱着「不——要忘了——感——谢恩物——你每——天都——在享受」，在这底下，我听得见寺院梵钟浑圆的嗡鸣：「众生——有托——此处——平安」。
上午8:30，孩子们背着书包，笑着嚷着挤满街道——在我眼里，这同我们的景象一般无二：清晨7:30，成群的男生穿制服、女生穿褶裙、一头乌亮的黑发，趿着木屐嗒嗒走过，书用一方印花布齐齐整整地包着。
情人节卡片上，蕾丝花边里画着躬身的骑士和燃烧的心，缠满玫瑰花蕾的彩绳，还有炽热的甜言蜜语——那便是我们的织女节：摇曳的竹枝上挂满彩带巾帕，悬着祈求晴天的、闪亮的诗笺，好教牛郎和他的织女，能在那一天相会于雾蒙蒙的天河岸边——美国人管那河叫银河。
阵亡将士纪念日，两场战争的老兵，爱国讲演，插着小旗、撒着花朵的坟墓——那便是我们招魂社（Shokonsha）祭奠阵亡将士的日子：一整天，成百的人列队穿过那座大石门，躬身行礼，轻轻合掌拍手；再退出来，让位给后面成百的人。
七月四日国庆，飘扬的旗、炸响的爆竹、咚咚的鼓，还有天上打旋蹿升的焰火——那便是我们的节日：樱花枝交搭成拱，日本国旗在枝下招展，纪念两千五百年前我们的初代天皇登基——那是个高大多须的男子，穿宽松的衣裳，手腕脚踝都用藤蔓缠束，胸前垂着一条长长的、摇来荡去的镰刀形玉珠项链——那串项链，如今是皇位三件神器之一。

===Original===

Halloween, with its grotesque lanterns, its witches and many jokes, was the Harvest Festival of Japan, when pumpkins were skilfully scraped into lovely pictures of shady gardens with lanterns and flowers; when ghost games were played and pumpkins piled at the gate of round-faced maidens; and when orchards of the stingy man were raided and their trophies laid on graves for the poor to find.
Thanksgiving, the homecoming day, with its turkey and pie, and jolly good cheer, was our anniversary when married sons and daughters with their children gathered for a feast of red rice and whole fish, gossiping happily while they ate, with the shrine doors open wide and the spirits of kindly ancestors watching over all.
Christmas, with its gay streets and merry, hurrying, bundle-laden crowds, with its sparkling tree and many gifts, with its holy memories of a shining star and a Mother with her Babe, was something like our seven days of New Year rejoicing, but with a difference﻿—the difference between the soft organ tones of an old melody and the careless, lilting song of a happy child.
At New Year’s time, above every doorway in our crowded streets was stretched a rope of ragged rice-straw with pine trees growing on either side, and the air resounded with children’s laughter and the tinkle of tiny hidden bells in running shoes; with the gay *tap-tap* of flying shuttlecocks and the cheerful greetings of bowing friends. In every home were thick rounded cakes of mochi; every babe had another birthday, every maiden had a new sash, and poetry cards were played by boys and girls together. Oh, it was gay in Japan at New Year’s time! There was no thought of solemnity anywhere, for the chrysalis of the past was broken, the butterfly had burst forth, and the world had begun again.
My first Christmas Day in America was a disappointment. We were all invited by a lady friend to attend Christmas services and afterward to go home with her to dinner and to see her tree. She had children, and I had pictured the scene as being gay, pretty, and pleasant, but with an undercurrent of dignity and reverence. I had idealized too much the wide influence of the symbolism of the day; and everything seemed such a strange combination of the spiritual and the material that I was lost.

===Chinese===

万圣节前夜，古怪的灯、巫婆和种种玩笑——那便是日本的丰收节：南瓜被巧手刮成清荫庭园、灯笼花草的可爱图画；人们玩鬼戏，把南瓜堆在圆脸姑娘的门口；还要去洗劫吝啬鬼的果园，把战利品摆到坟头上，留给穷人去捡。
感恩节，阖家归来的日子，火鸡和馅饼，一团的欢喜——那便是我们的祭日：成了家的儿女带着孩子齐聚一堂，吃红饭、吃整鱼，边吃边快快活活地闲话家常；神龛的柜门敞开着，慈爱的祖先之灵看顾着这一切。
圣诞节，欢腾的街市，快活匆忙、大包小包的人流，灿灿的圣诞树和琳琅的礼物，还有那圣洁的记忆——一颗明亮的星，一位圣母婴孩——有些像我们的新年七天欢庆，却又不同——那不同，就像一支古老旋律的柔曼琴声，同一个快乐孩子无忧无虑的轻快歌儿之间的不同。
新年时节，我们热闹的街上家家门楣都横着一条稻草绳，两边插着松枝；空气里满是孩子们的笑声，跑动的鞋里藏着的小铃铛叮当作响，板羽球欢快地嗒嗒飞舞，躬身拜年的朋友互道吉语。家家户户都有厚厚的圆年糕；每个娃娃都添一岁，每个姑娘都有新腰带，男孩女孩还一起玩和歌纸牌。啊，日本的新年多热闹！处处不见一点庄严肃穆，因为旧岁的茧壳已经破了，蝴蝶飞了出来，天地又重新开始。
我在美国的头一个圣诞节，是扫兴的。一位女友邀我们全家去望圣诞礼拜，之后到她家吃饭、看圣诞树。她有孩子，我心里描画的场面又欢腾、又漂亮、又愉快，底下还该涌动着一股庄重虔敬。我把这一天的象征意义想得太广大了；结果样样东西都像是精神与物质的奇妙混合，把我弄糊涂了。

===Original===

The star on the tree and the thought of unselfish giving were beautiful, but little was said of either﻿—except in church; and just beneath the star were festoons of popcorn and cranberries﻿—things we eat. Indeed, except for the gaiety of giving and receiving gifts, most things especially belonging to the day seemed to be only the serving of certain kinds of food and the very inartistic and peculiar custom of hanging in a prominent place the garments of the lowest part of the body for the purpose of holding gifts of toys and jewellery﻿—even candy and fruit. That was a custom difficult for a Japanese to understand.
That evening, Mother and I went over to call on Miss Helen. And there, in her big quiet parlour, spreading over a large snowy cloth on the floor, stood her tree﻿—large and pine-scented, sparkling with lights and coloured, swinging ornaments. It was wonderful! The tree, though so big and beautiful, reminded me, as an American skyscraper may remind one of a tiny temple pagoda, of the fairy-like branch of our Cocoon Festival from which swing and float, swaying with the lightest breath, myriads of fairy-like, sugar-blown replicas of every delicate symbol of the day. Miss Helen’s father and mother were there, and we talked of the holidays of America and of Japan. Then a little niece and a neighbour’s child sang Christmas carols, and my heart was full of joy, for I felt that my ideal Christmas had really come.
The morning after Christmas we had our first snow﻿—a flying mist of dry, feathery flakes that was no more like the heavy fall of Echigo’s damp, solid clots than fluffy silk-floss is like weighty cotton-batting. All day long it fell, growing thicker toward nightfall, and when we wakened the next morning the world was white.
Just at the curve where our driveway turned into the broad public road stood the coachman’s cottage. He had three children and they asked Mother if they might make a snowman on our back lawn. Mother gave her consent, and then the most interesting things happened! The children rolled a big ball, then piled on it another, and on the top of that, a small one. Then with much pushing and patting of red-mittened hands, they formed rude features and, with shiny bits of hard coal, gave the image a pair of bright eyes and a row of buttons down the front.

===Chinese===

树顶的星，还有无私馈赠的心意，都是美的；可是除了在教堂里，很少听人提起这两样——而就在那颗星的下面，挂的是一串串爆米花和蔓越莓——都是吃的东西。真的，除了送礼收礼的欢快，这一天特有的事物，多半看来只是端上几样吃食，加上一个很不风雅、也很古怪的习俗：把身上最底下那件衣裳挂在显眼的地方，专为盛礼物的玩具和首饰——连糖果水果也放在里头。这个习俗，日本人实在难于理解。
那天晚上，妈妈和我过去拜望海伦小姐。在她那间宽敞安静的客厅里，地板上铺开一大块雪白的布，布上立着她的圣诞树——高高的，带着松香，缀满灯火和彩色摇曳的饰物，闪闪发亮。真是美妙！那树虽然这样大、这样美，却叫我想起我们蚕茧节上那仙灵似的树枝——正如美国的摩天大楼也会叫人想起小小的寺塔——枝上悬着、浮着千万件精巧的小仙物，都是糖吹的、应节的细巧象征，最轻的一丝气息也教它们摇曳。
海伦小姐的父母也在，我们谈起美国的节日和日本的节日。随后一个小侄女和邻家的孩子唱起圣诞颂歌，我心里满是欢喜——我觉得，我理想中的圣诞节，这回真的来了。
圣诞转天的早晨，下了我们入冬的第一场雪——干燥羽絮似的雪片，飞飞蒙蒙，同越后那湿而结实的雪团相比，就像蓬松的丝绵比上沉甸甸的棉花胎。下了一整天，入暮时分越下越密；第二天早晨醒来，天地一片白。
我们家的车道拐上大路的地方，住着车夫一家。他家有三个孩子，孩子们问妈妈，能不能在我们后院的草地上堆个雪人。妈妈应允了，于是顶有趣的事情发生了！孩子们滚起一个大球，又摞上一个，顶上再放个小的。然后戴着红连指手套的小手又推又拍，捏出眉眼口鼻，再拣亮闪闪的硬煤渣嵌上，那雪人便有了一双明亮的眼睛，胸前还有一排扣子。

===Original===

An old hat of their father’s and a pipe from somewhere completed their work, and there stood a clumsy, shapeless image that reminded me of Daruma Sama﻿—the Indian saint whose devotion cost him his feet.
I had never expected to see a Buddhist saint in America, but I greeted the likeness with merriment and entertained the children by telling them the story of the cheerful rice-pounder who threw away his pestle to become the founder of a new religion; and who asked that his image be not honoured with reverential bows, but be made into amusing toys that children’s hands would use and children’s hearts enjoy. Later on I saw a Damura Sama at other places than on our snowy lawn. To my surprise, the little squatting figure muffled in a scarlet cloak seemed to be a familiar object, but no one knew his story or his name. All my life I had been accustomed to seeing Daruma Sama in the shape of every toy that can be made for careless baby fingers; but I was really shocked one evening at a card party to find the little red, rolling figure used as a booby prize.
“It is such an odd selection for a card-game prize,” I said to Matsuo. “Why should a Daruma Sama be chosen?”
“Not odd at all,” replied Matsuo. “Very appropriate. A man so well balanced that, however he may fall, the next moment he is again right side up, makes an excellent booby prize. It means, ‘Down only for a moment.’ Don’t you see?”
In Japan we always treat a Daruma Sama rather disrespectfully, but it is a kind of affectionate disrespect; and my sensations, as I walked home with Matsuo from the party, were rather mixed. Finally, just as I reached the iron gate, I drew in a long breath, and with a ridiculous feeling of loyalty and protection tugging at my heart, I surprised Matsuo by saying, “I wish that either you or I had won the booby prize!”
It was an unusual thing for snow to remain on the ground longer than a few days, but Mother laughingly declared that the American gods of the weather had evidently planned a special season in order to keep me from being homesick. At any rate, more snow fell and still more, and we began to see sleighs go by﻿—light, carriage-like vehicles, filled with laughing ladies in furs and with gay scarfs floating behind them as they flew by. It was like a scene from the theatre.

===Chinese===

再戴上他们父亲的旧帽子，不知从哪儿弄来一支烟斗，工程便告完成——立在那里一个笨拙粗憨的雪人，叫我想起了达摩大人（Daruma Sama）——那位印度来的圣者，虔修苦行，连脚都坐没了。
我从没想到会在美国见到一位佛教圣者，不过我还是欢欢喜喜向这尊「法相」行了礼，还给孩子们讲故事开心：说那位快活的捣米人，撂下手中的杵，去做了新宗教的祖师；又嘱咐后人，不要对他老人家顶礼膜拜，只管把他做成好玩儿的玩意儿，让孩子的手去拨弄，孩子的心去欢喜。
后来，除了我们那片雪地草坪，我在别处也见到了达摩大人。叫我吃惊的是，那尊裹在猩红斗篷里盘坐的小小身影，竟像是个人人熟识的物件，只是没有一个人知道他的来历，也不知道他的名号。我这辈子看惯了达摩大人变成各式各样给小娃娃毛手毛脚玩的玩具；可有一天晚上，在牌会上看见那红红的小家伙被用作「末名奖」，我着实吃了一惊。
「拿他当牌局的奖品，这选择真古怪，」我对松雄说。「为什么偏偏选达摩大人呢？」
「一点也不古怪，」松雄答道。「再合适不过了。一个人身子这样稳，无论怎么跌倒，转眼又端端正正坐起来，拿来作末名奖再好不过。意思是：『跌倒不过片刻。』你还不明白？」
在日本，我们对达摩大人向来不大恭敬，可那是一种亲昵的不恭敬。那晚从牌会回家，一路走着，我心里滋味复杂。末了，走到铁门前，我长长吸了口气，心口被一种可笑的忠心和护犊之情牵扯着，冲口说出一句叫松雄吃惊的话：「真希望你或者我，把那末名奖赢了来！」
雪在地上留过几天还不化，本不是常有的事；妈妈却笑着说，美国管天气的众神分明是特意安排了这样一个冬天，免得我想家。不管怎样，雪一场接一场地下，我们开始看见雪橇驶过——轻巧的、马车似的雪橇，满载着裘皮里欢笑的太太小姐，鲜艳的头巾在身后飘飞。简直像戏台上一景。

===Original===

How different from the deep snows of Echigo, over which snow-booted men pulled heavy sledges﻿—built for work, not fun﻿—chanting, as they pulled, a steady, rhythmic “*En yara-ya! En yara-ya!*” I missed the purity of Echigo’s clear skies and snowy mountainsides, for it was only a few days until the coal-tainted air had stolen the fresh whiteness from our snow, but the happiness of the children was not spoiled. Daruma Samas stood on every lawn, and the streets were filled with boys throwing snowballs. One day from my window I saw a lively snow-fight in which a group of besiegers pressed hard a heroic few, bravely dodging behind two barrels and a board with snow piled beneath. When the besiegers called a truce and ran around the corner for reinforcements, I pushed up my window and clapped as hard as I could.
The boys had a good time, but as I watched their soiled tracks in the snow and the smoky colour of the balls, my mind went to Ishi’s stories of the snow-battles held in the courtyard of the old mansion at Nagaoka during the first years of Mother’s life there. In those days life in the daimyo households of even small castle towns was based on the customs of the lords and ladies in the court of the shogun, and, in a less degree, it was as luxurious and as frivolous.
Occasionally, when the winter season was late, the first snows that fell were light and dry. On the morning after such a snow had fallen, when the air was full of the cool sunshine of Echigo, and the ground white and sparkling, the men would lay aside their swords, and with their pleated skirts gracefully caught up at the sides, run out into the big open court. Soon they would be joined by the women, their gay trains looped over their scarlet skirts and their long, bright sleeves held back with gay cords. No one wore wooden shoes or even sandals, for that would mar the purity of the snow, but with only the white foot-mitten on the feet, with bare heads and tinkling hairpins, all joined in the battle of snowballs. There was running, with laughter, and merriment, and the air filled with flying and breaking balls through which could be seen the tossing of bright sleeves and dodging black heads powdered with snow.

===Chinese===

这同越后的深雪多么不同！在那边，踏雪靴的汉子们拉着笨重的雪橇——为干活造的，不是为玩耍——一边拉，一边哼着匀称的号子：「恩呀啦呀！恩呀啦呀！」我怀念越后清朗的天空和雪坡的纯净，因为不出几天，含煤烟的空气便偷走了我们这里积雪的新白；可孩子们的快乐一点没受影响。家家草坪上都站着达摩大人，满街都是扔雪球的男孩。有一天，我从窗口望见一场雪仗：一小队好汉被围攻得紧，英勇地躲在两只木桶和一块搭起来的板子后面，桶下堆着雪。围攻的喊了停战，绕过墙角去搬救兵；我推开窗，拼命给他们鼓掌。
男孩们玩得痛快；可我看着雪地上他们踩出的污迹和雪球上烟灰的颜色，心里想起阿石讲的故事：母亲初到长冈老宅那几年，庭院里也打过雪仗。那时，连小城堡町的大名家，起居也照着幕府将军宫廷里王公贵妇的规矩，奢华轻靡，只是差着一层罢了。
有时冬来得迟，头场雪便又轻又干。这样的雪下过一夜，第二天早晨，越后清冽的阳光洒满空气，地上又白又亮，男人们便卸下刀，把裙裤的褶裥在两侧潇洒地掠起，跑到开阔的大院子里。不一会儿女人们也来了：华美的长裾挽在绯红裙上，长长的鲜亮衣袖用彩绳束起。谁也不穿木屐，连草鞋也不穿——那要精蹋了雪的洁白——只穿一双白足袋，光着头，发簪叮当，人人投入雪球大战。有人奔跑，有人欢笑喧闹；满空气都是飞着、破着的雪球，雪球影里只见鲜亮的衣袖翻飞，乌黑的头颅闪躲，扑了一层白粉似的雪。

===Original===

Our old servants often told me of those gay scenes, and Baya, the oldest of them all, would solemnly shake her head from side to side and sigh over the fact that Etsu-bo’s enjoyment must consist only of climbing the snow hills piled in the street, and of racing with Sister on snowshoes as we went to and from school.
The children of my American neighbours had no snow shoe races, but there was great excitement over coasting. Ours was a hilly suburb and almost every lawn had at least one curving slope; but the snow was thin and no one wanted the grass worn off or beaten down. Of course the sidewalks were cleaned and the streets were forbidden. The older boys had discovered a few long slopes and monopolized them, but the smaller children could only stand around and watch, unless some big brother or kind friend would occasionally take pity and give a ride.
One day I saw a group of four or five little girls with two red sleds standing by our iron gates and looking wistfully up at the long slope of our side lawn.
“It would ruin the appearance of the whole place for them to be allowed to make a brown track there,” I said to Mother.
“It is not the appearance, Etsu,” Mother replied. “Probably all the track those little folks would make would not kill the grass; but it is too dangerous. They would have to bump over two gravel paths and end abruptly at the top of the stone wall. The battlements are not high, and the sleds might leap over on to the outside walk, four feet below. I should be afraid to risk it.”
That afternoon as Mother and I were walking to a meeting of the Ladies’ Club we passed the home of Doctor Miller. His lawn was small but it was one of the prettiest and best kept in our neighbourhood. The hill began at the roadway and swept in a straight, rather steep slope ending in a level stretch. At least a dozen children were gathered there, among them the forlorn little group with the two red sleds that I had seen in the morning. A long, smooth track had already been worn on which every moment a sled went down laden with a squealing, shrieking mass of hunched-up little figures. And on an uphill path beside the track a line of rosy-cheeked, rosy-nosed, panting coasters were pulling their sleds and shouting﻿—not for any reason at all, except that they were having the best time of any coasters in the world.

===Chinese===

家里的老仆人们常给我讲那些热闹光景；阿婆（Baya）——她们里头最年长的——总要一本正经地左右摇头，叹说津保样的乐子，竟只剩爬街上堆起的雪山、跟姐姐踏雪鞋赛跑去上学放学了。
美国邻居家的孩子们不赛雪鞋，滑橇却使他们兴奋得不得了。我们这带郊区多丘，几乎家家草坪都有一道弯弯的斜坡；可雪薄，谁也不肯让草皮磨坏压坏。人行道自然都扫净了，街上又是禁地。大男孩们发现了几个长坡，占住了不放；小孩子们只好站在一边看，除非哪位兄长或好心的朋友偶尔发了慈悲，带他们滑一趟。
有一天，我看见四五个小姑娘，带着两架红雪橇，站在我们家铁门外，眼巴巴地望着侧草坪那道长长的斜坡。
「要是让她们在那里滑出一条黄泥道子，这整个地方的样子就毁了。」我对妈妈说。
「倒不是为了样子，阿津，」妈妈答道。「那几个小家伙滑出来的道子，未必就毁了草；是太危险。她们得颠过两条石子路，末了猛地停在石墙跟前。墙头的雉堞不高，雪橇没准会腾过去，摔到墙外边的人行道上——底下可有四英尺深呢。我可不敢冒这个险。」
那天下午，妈妈和我走路去妇女俱乐部开会，路过米勒医生（Doctor Miller）家。他的草坪不大，却是我家这一带最漂亮、最见照料的一块。坡从路边起，一道笔直的、相当陡的斜坡，收在一片平地上。至少十来个孩子聚在那里，其中就有早晨我见到的那一小队可怜巴巴的红雪橇。一条长长的、光滑的雪道已经碾了出来，时时刻刻都有雪橇载着一团尖叫欢呼、缩着身子的小人儿冲下去。雪道旁的上坡路上，排着一溜红脸蛋红鼻头、气喘吁吁的孩子，拉着雪橇喊啊叫啊——不为任何缘故，只因为他们是天底下滑橇滑得最快活的人。

===Original===

Day after day, as long as the snow lasted, that hill was reserved for the little folks, and every child that went gliding down the smooth slide, and every one that came struggling up the broken path, had laughter in the eyes, happiness in the heart, and, hidden somewhere within, a growing germ of unselfishness, kindness, and godliness that had been planted there by the kind act of a man who could see from the viewpoint of a child.
It was like my father to have done that kind deed. Afterward I never saw Doctor Miller, even to pass him on the street, that I did not look to see if behind his fine, grave, intellectual face I could not see the heart of my father. I have not seen it, but I know it is there, and that some day, on the other side of the Sandzu River, those two beautiful souls will be friends.
January brought to Matsuo and me a quiet celebration of our own. For weeks before, the letters from Japan had been coming more frequently, and occasionally the postman would hand in a package wrapped in oil-paper and sealed with the oval stamp of Uncle Otani’s house, or the big square one of Inagaki.
One of these packages contained a thin sash of soft white cotton, each end of which had been dipped in rouge, and also two emblems of congratulation﻿—baby storks of rice-dough, one white and one red.
These were Mother’s gifts for the “Five-month ceremony,” a special celebration observed by expectant parents on that date. My thoughtful, loving, faraway mother! The tears came to my eyes as I explained it all to my dear American mother, who in sweet understanding of the sacred ceremony asked how to prepare everything according to Japanese custom.
At this celebration, besides the husband and wife, only women members of the two families are present. The young father-to-be sits beside his wife and the sash is passed through the sleeves of his garment from left to right. Then it is properly adjusted around the wife. From then on, she is called “a lady of retirement,” and her food, exercise, amusements, and reading are all of a character called “education for the Coming.” The gay, light balls of many-coloured silk thread which are seen in American shops belong to this time.
In the package with the sash was a charm-card from my good Ishi.

===Chinese===

雪在多久，那道坡就给小家伙们留多久；每个顺着滑道溜下去的孩子，每个沿着踩烂的小路爬上来的孩子，眼里都有笑，心里都有乐；而且每个人心里深处，都埋着一粒悄悄生长的种子——无私、仁爱、敬神——那是一位能从孩子的眼里看世界的先生，用他的善举种下的。
做出这等善事的，正是我父亲那样的人。从那以后，我每次见到米勒医生，哪怕只是在街上打个照面，都要端详端详，想在他那张清俊、庄重、睿智的脸后面，看出我父亲的心来。我没有看出来，可我知道那颗心在那里；也知道将来有一天，在三途川（Sandzu River）的彼岸，那两个美丽的灵魂会结成朋友。
正月里，松雄和我有了一场我们自己的、安安静静的庆贺。此前几个星期，日本的来信越来越勤了；邮差偶尔会递进来一个油纸包着的包裹，封着大谷叔父（Uncle Otani）家的椭圆印记，或是稻垣家的大方印记。
其中一个包裹里，是一条软白棉布的细腰带，两端浸过胭脂红；另有两件贺仪——米面团捏的仙鹤，一只白，一只红。
这是母亲的礼物，为的是「五月贺仪」——有身孕的夫妇在那个日子里特别庆贺一番。我那思虑周到的、慈爱的、远隔重洋的母亲啊！我把这一切讲给我亲爱的美国妈妈听时，眼泪涌了上来；她怀着甜美的体贴，问怎样按日本的风俗把样样备妥。
这场庆贺上，除夫妻二人，只有两家的女眷在场。将要做父亲的青年坐在妻子身旁，腰带从他衣袖里从左到右穿过去，再端端正正地系到妻子腰上。从那以后，她便称「闭居的夫人」，她的饮食、起居、消遣、阅读，都归于一种唤作「迎生之教」的名目。美国铺子里那些五颜六色丝线缠的轻巧彩球，就是这时候用的物件。
同腰带一起寄来的包裹里，还有我那好阿石给我求的一张护符。

===Original===

To obtain it she had made a pilgrimage of two days to the temple of Kishibo-jin﻿—“Demon of the Mother-heart”﻿—believing sincerely that the bit of paper with its mysterious symbols would protect me from every evil.
According to an ancient legend there lived in the time of the Buddha a mother of many children, who was so poor that she could not obtain food for them, and in helpless misery saw them starving. At last her agony became so great that it changed her loving mother heart into that of a demon. Every night she roamed the country stealing little babes, so that, in some uncanny way belonging to demon lore, their nourishment might be transferred to her own children. Her name became a horror to the world. The wise Buddha, knowing that however many children a woman may have she always loves the youngest with special tenderness, took her babe and hid it in his begging bowl. Hearing the child’s voice, but not being able to trace it, the mother was wild with distress and grief.
“Listen,” said the merciful Buddha, restoring the infant to her arms: “You have a thousand children, while most women have but ten; yet you mourn bitterly for the loss of one. Think of other aching hearts with the sympathy you feel for your own.”
The mother, thankfully clasping the babe to her breast, saw within the tiny arms a pomegranate, and recognized it as the miracle-fruit whose never-withering freshness can nourish the world. Remorse and gratitude healed her heart, and she vowed to become forever a loving guardian to little children. This is why in all Kishibo temples the goddess of the altar is a demon-faced woman surrounded by children and standing in the midst of draperies and decorations of pomegranate.
These recollections flooded my mind as I sat stitching on dainty, wee garments into every one of which I breathed a prayer that my baby might be a boy. I wanted a son, not only because every Japanese family believes it most desirable that the name should be carried on without adoption, but also for the selfish reason that both Matsuo’s family and my own would look upon me with more pride were I the mother of a son.

===Chinese===

为求这张符，她徒步两天，去鬼子母神（Kishibo-jin）——「慈母心之鬼神」——的寺里进香；她诚心相信，那张画着神秘符记的小纸片，能保佑我不遭一切凶邪。
相传佛在世时，有个妇人儿女成群，穷得讨不到儿女的吃食，眼睁睁看着他们挨饿，毫无办法。末了她苦痛到极处，那颗慈母的心竟化成了鬼心。她夜夜游荡各地，偷人家的小小婴孩，好按鬼法里某种诡秘的路数，把婴孩的滋养渡给自己的儿女。她的名字成了世间的恐怖。睿智的佛知道，一个女人无论有多少儿女，疼幼子的心总是格外软；便把她的幼子抱来，藏进自己的钵里。那母亲听得见孩子的声音，却寻不见踪影，急得肝肠寸断。
「你听着，」慈悲的佛把婴孩还到她怀里，说道，「你有上千个孩子，寻常女人不过十个；失了一个，你便这样哀恸。把你对自己孩子这份痛，也拿去体谅别人的痛吧。」
那母亲感激涕零，把孩子搂在怀里，看见孩子小小的臂弯里抱着一枚石榴；她认出那是永不枯萎、能滋养世间的灵果。悔恨与感激医好了她的心，她发愿从此永远做幼儿们的慈爱守护者。所以凡是鬼子母神的殿堂，坛上的神像总是一张鬼脸的女人，四围环绕着孩子，立在重重石榴纹的幔帐装饰中间。
我坐着给小小的、精致的小衣裳缝针脚，这些往事涌满了心头；每一针里我都默默祈求，愿我腹中的孩子是个男孩。我想要儿子，不单因为日本家家都以为，家名能不靠养子传下去才是最称心的事；也有一点私心：若我生的是儿子，松雄家和稻垣家看我的眼光，都会多几分得意。

===Original===

Neither Matsuo nor I had, to any great extent, the feeling that woman is inferior to man, which has been so common a belief among all classes in Japan; but law and custom being what they were, it was such a serious inconvenience﻿—yes, calamity﻿—to have *no* son, that congratulations always fell more readily from the lips when the firstborn was a boy.
Little girls were always welcome in Japanese homes. Indeed, it was a great sorrow to have all sons and no daughter﻿—a calamity second only to having all daughters and no son.
The laws of our family system were planned in consideration for customs which themselves were based on ancient beliefs, all of which were wise and good﻿—for their time. But as the world moves on, and the ages overlap each other, there come intervals when we climb haltingly; and this means martyrdom to the advanced. Nevertheless, perhaps it is wiser and kinder to the puzzled many for the advanced few to accommodate themselves somewhat to fading beliefs, instead of opposing them too bitterly, unless it should be a matter of principle, for we are climbing; slowly, but﻿—climbing. Nature does not hasten, and Japanese are Nature’s pupils.
Mother had a magic touch with flowers, and when spring came the crimson rambler that formed a heavy brocade curtain on one side of our veranda was thick with tiny buds. One morning I had gone to the door to see Matsuo off, and was wondering how soon the tiny roses would bloom, when I was joined by Mother.
“There are hundreds of buds here,” I said. “This will be a bower of rich beauty some day. How much joy we Japanese miss because of superstition! Roses do not look beautiful to us because they have harmful thorns.”
“And how much joy you have because of traditions,” said Mother, smiling. “In the poem you taught me last night,

> “The sacred lotus that bravely lifts its snowy head in purity and beauty,

Although its roots are buried in earthly mire,

Holds a lesson of pride and inspiration.

“Have you another blossom that is ‘a teacher’?”
“And the modest plum,” I answered quickly, “that blossoms on snow-laden branches, is a bridal flower, because it teaches courage and endurance.”
“And how about the cherry?” asked Mother.
“Oh, that has an important meaning,” I quickly replied.

> “The quick-falling cherry, that lives but a day

And dies with destiny unfulfilled,

Is the brave spirit of samurai youth,

===Chinese===

女人不及男人——这层意思，在日本的各阶层原是极普遍的信念；松雄同我倒不大有。只是法度风俗摆在那里，家里竟没有儿子，实在是天大的不便——是的，是灾祸——所以头胎若生的是男孩，贺喜的话总更容易说出口些。
日本人家向来也是欢喜小女孩的。真的，只有儿子没有女儿，是莫大的憾事——其祸仅次于只有女儿没有儿子。
我们家族制度的法度，是照着风俗拟定的；风俗又本于古老的信仰——这一切在其当时，原都是明智的、好的。只是世界在前行，时代与时代交叠，其间便有一段踉跄攀爬的日子；这对先走一步的人，就意味着殉道。不过，除非关乎原则，先觉的少数与其苦苦抵拒那些正在消褪的旧信，不如稍稍迁就之，或许这样对惶惑的大多数更明智也更仁厚——因为我们终究在往上爬；慢，可是——在爬。自然从不性急，而日本人是自然的学生。
妈妈侍弄花草有如魔法。春来时，我们游廊一侧那架织锦重帘似的红攀藤蔷薇，密密缀满了小小的花苞。一天早晨，我送到门口，望松雄出门，正自思量这些小蔷薇还要几日才开，妈妈走到我身边来了。
「这里足有几百个花苞，」我说。「将来有一天，这里会是一座浓艳的凉棚。我们日本人因为迷信，错过了多少乐趣啊！玫瑰在我们眼里不美，就因为它长着伤人的刺。」
「可你们因传统而得的乐趣又多着呢，」妈妈含笑道。「你昨晚教我的那首诗里——

> 「圣洁的莲花，勇敢地擎起雪白的头，那样纯净，那样美丽，

纵然根须深埋在尘世的泥淖里，

也自有一课骄傲与奋励。」

「还有哪种花也是『先生』吗？」
「还有谦逊的梅，」我立刻答道，「开在积雪压枝的枝头上，是新娘的花，因为它教人勇气与坚忍。」
「那么樱花呢？」妈妈问。

「哦，那个意义可大了，」我立刻答道。

> 「匆匆飘落的樱花，只活一日，

便怀着未竟之志死去，

那是武士少年的英魂——

===Original===

Always ready, his fresh young strength

To offer to his lord.”

“Bravo!” Mother cried, clapping her hands. “This is a real, albeit a second-rate, poetry contest that you and I are having. Do you know any more flower poems?”
“Oh, yes﻿—Morning glories!” And I rapidly recited in Japanese:

> “In the dewy freshness of the morning, they smile respectful greetings to the goddess of the Sun.

“Oh, Mother, this is just like Japan﻿—the way you and I are doing now! Japanese people often gather﻿—a group of friends﻿—and write poems. They meet at a Flower Viewing festival and hang poems on the flowery branches; or at a moon-gazing party where they sit in the light of the moon and make poems. There is one place where the moonlight falls on a plain of ricefields and from the mountainside the silvery reflection can be seen in every separate field. It is wonderful! And then everybody goes home feeling quiet and peaceful﻿—and with new thoughts.”
“Ah!” exclaimed Mother, starting quickly toward the door, adding, as she looked back over her shoulder, “Our poetry contest has given *me* a new thought!” And she disappeared within the house.
Our conversation had reminded her of a package of morning-glory seeds that a friend had sent when she learned that a Japanese lady was living with her.
“I had almost forgotten about them,” said Mother, returning with a trowel in her hand. “These were gathered from the vines which my friend had grown from seeds that came from Japan. She says the blossoms are wonderful﻿—four and five inches across. Where shall we plant them? We must choose some appropriate spot for the little grandseeds of a Japanese ancestor.”
“I know exactly the place!” I cried, delighted, and leading Mother to our old-fashioned well I told her the legend of the maiden who went to a well to draw water and, finding a morning-glory tendril twined about the handle of the bucket, went away rather than break the tender vine.
Mother was pleased, and she planted the seeds around the well curb while I softly hummed, over and over, the old poem:

> “The morning-glory tendril has chained my heart.

Let it be:

I’ll beg water of my neighbour.”

===Chinese===

——随时预备着，把自己鲜活的少壮之力，

献给他的主君。」

「好！」妈妈拍着手喊道。「咱们这真成了一场诗会——虽是二流的。你还会别的花诗么？」
「哦，会——牵牛花！」我用日语飞快地吟道：

> 「清晨带露的清新里，她们含着恭敬的笑意，问候太阳的女神。

「啊，妈妈，这多像日本——就是你我此刻的样子！日本人常常聚起来——一群朋友——作诗。他们在花会上相聚，把诗笺挂在花枝上；或者在赏月的夜会上，坐在月光里联句。有一处地方，月光落在一片稻田上，从山腰望下去，每一块田里都映着一片银光。美极了！完了大家回家，心里静静的、安安稳稳的——还带着新的念头。」
「啊！」妈妈喊了一声，快步朝门口走去，又回头添了一句：「咱们的诗会倒给了我一个新念头！」说罢人已进了屋里。
原来我们这场谈话，使她想起一小包牵牛花籽——一位朋友听说她家住了位日本太太，特意寄来的。
「差点儿忘了它们，」妈妈说着回来，手里拿一把花铲。「这是我那朋友收的籽，她的藤是日本来的籽种的。她说花开得才好呢——足有四五英寸宽。种在哪儿好？咱们总得给这日本老祖宗的小小种子孙，挑个相称的地方。」
「我知道地方了！」我欢喜地喊道。我把妈妈领到我们那口老式井边，给她讲了那个少女的传说：少女去井边打水，见一条牵牛花的嫩蔓缠住了井桶的柄，不忍折断那柔嫩的藤，宁可空手走开。
妈妈很欢喜，便把花籽种在井栏四周；我在一旁轻轻地、一遍一遍哼着那首老诗：

> 「牵牛花的藤蔓拴住了我的心。

由它去吧：

我去邻家讨水。」

===Original===

We watched the vines eagerly as they reached out strong arms and climbed steadily upward. Mother often said, “The coming of the blossoms and of the baby will not be far apart.”
One morning I saw from my window Mother and Clara standing by the well. They were looking at the vines and talking excitedly. I hurried downstairs and across the lawn. The blossoms were open, but were pale, half sized weaklings﻿—not resembling at all the royal blossoms we treasure so dearly in Japan.
