Then I remembered having read that Japanese flowers do not like other lands and, after the first year, gradually fade away. With a superstitious clutch at my heart, I thought of my selfish prayer for a son and vowed to be gratefully content with either boy or girl if only the little one bore no pitiful trace of the transplanting.
And then the baby came﻿—well and sweet and strong﻿—upholding in her perfect babyhood the traditions of both America and Japan. I forgot that I had ever wanted a son, and Matsuo, after his first glimpse of his little daughter, remembered that he had always liked girls better than boys.
Whether the paper charm of Kishibo-jin was of value or not, my good Ishi’s loving thought for me was a boon to my heart during those first weeks when I so longed for her wisdom and her love. And yet it was well that she was not with me, for she could never have fitted into our American life. The gentle, time-taking ways of a Japanese nurse crooning to a little bundle of crêpe and brocade swinging in its silken hammock on her back would never have done for my active baby, who so soon learned to crow with delight and clutch disrespectfully at her father’s head as he tossed her aloft in his strong arms.
We decided to bring the baby up with all the healthful freedom given to an American child, but we wanted her to have a Japanese name.
The meaning of Matsuo’s name was “pine”﻿—he emblem of strength; mine was “ricefield”﻿—the emblem of usefulness. “Therefore,” said Matsuo, “the baby is already a combination of strength and usefulness, but she must have beauty also. So let us give her the name of our kind American mother, which, translated, means ‘flower.’ ”
“And if we use the old-fashioned termination,” I cried with delight, “it will mean ‘foreign fields’ or ‘strange land.’ ”
“Hanano﻿—Flower in a Strange Land!” cried Matsuo, clapping his hands. “Nothing could be better.”

===Chinese===

我们急切地望着藤蔓伸出壮健的臂膀，一步步稳稳向上攀。妈妈常说：「花开和小宝宝，来的日子不会隔多远。」
一天早晨，我从窗口看见妈妈和克拉拉站在井边，望着藤子，兴奋地说着话。我急忙下楼，跑过草坪。花开了——却淡弱得很，只有半大——一点也不像我们在日本那样珍爱的雍容花朵。我想起读到过：日本花不喜他乡，过了头一年，便渐渐褪色。我心里迷信地一紧，想起自己求子的私愿，便默默立誓：男孩女孩，我都感激地领受，只求小家伙身上别带上那移栽的可怜痕迹。
后来，宝宝降生了——健康、可爱、壮实——以她完美的婴儿之身，把美国和日本两边的传统都承了下来。我忘了自己曾想要过儿子；松雄看了小女儿一眼，也想起来他素来就喜欢女孩胜过男孩。
鬼子母神的纸符灵验不灵验且不说，我那好阿石对我的疼爱，在最初那几个星期里，实在是我心头的一大慰藉——那时我多么盼望她的智慧和她的爱。可她不来也有不来得好：她断断乎融不进我们的美国生活。日本保姆那慢悠悠、费工夫的做派——哼着调儿，背上背一个绉纱锦缎裹的小小包袱，在丝吊兜里摇着——绝然对付不了我那活泼的宝宝：她那么快就学会咯咯地欢叫，父亲用有力的胳膊把她高高抛起时，她还毫无敬意地一把去抓父亲的头。
我们决定让宝宝照美国孩子的样子养，给她一切有益的自由；只是名字，我们要她有一个日本名字。
松雄的名字，意思是「松」——力量的象征；我的名字，意思是「稻田」——实用的象征。「这么说，」松雄道，「宝宝已经兼有力量与实用了；她还须再添上美。那就把咱们那位慈爱的美国妈妈的名字给她吧——那名字译出来，意思是『花』。」
「要是再添上老式的词尾，」我欢喜地喊道，「那就成了『异国之野』或『陌生的土地』的意思了。」
「花野（Hanano）——异乡之花！」松雄拍着手喊道。「再好不过了。」

===Original===

Mother consented, and thus it was decided.

===Chinese===

妈妈应允了，事情就这样定了。

## XXII Flower in a Strange Land / 二十二 异乡之花
===Original===

For months after the baby came my entire life centred around that one small bit of humanity. Wherever I went, and no matter who came to see me, the conversation was sure to drift to her; and my letters to my mother held little else than the information that a few ounces had been added to the baby’s weight, or a new accent to the little cooings and gurglings, or that she had developed a dimple when she smiled. My mother must have seen the germ of a too-selfish love in my devotion; for one day I received from her a set of Buddhist picture-books which had belonged to Father’s library. How familiar and dear they looked! There were no stories﻿—only pictures﻿—but as I turned the pages, I could hear again the gentle voice of Honourable Grandmother and see the old tales acted before my mind as plainly as in the days of my childhood. Mother had marked some of the pages with a dot of vermilion. On one of these was a scene from “The Mount of Spears.” The story is of a favourite disciple of Buddha who grieved so bitterly over the loss of his beloved mother that the pitying Master exerted his holy power and took the sorrowing son to a place from which the mother could be seen. The disciple was horrified to behold his precious mother climbing painfully over a hilly path made of sharp spears.
“Oh, good Master,” he cried, “you have brought me to the ‘Hell of Seven Hills.’ Why is my mother here? She never, throughout her life, did a wicked deed.”
“But she had a wicked thought,” sadly the Buddha replied. “When you were a baby, her only care was for you, and one day when she saw a little field-mouse happily playing, she so longed to have its gray, silky tail for a cord to tie your holiday coat, that her wish was thought murder.”
I closed the book with a half-smile, for I understood at once the wordless warning of my gentle, anxious mother; but my heart was full of loving gratitude as I bowed respectfully in the direction of Japan and resolved that my love for my baby should make me more thoughtful and tender toward all the world.
One of the first callers the baby had was our faithful black laundress, Minty. She had been washing for Mother for years, and, when I came, she accepted the additional burden of my queer clothes with kind good nature.

===Chinese===

宝宝降生后的好几个月里，我的整个生命都围着那一小团血肉打转。无论我到哪里，也不管有谁来看我，话头迟早总要飘到她身上；我写给母亲的信里，翻来覆去也无非是：宝宝又重了几两，咿咿呀呀的咕哝里又添了新的调子，或是她笑起来时又显出一个酒窝。母亲想必是看我这份痴爱里生了自私之爱的芽；因为有一天，我收到她寄来的一套佛教画册，原是父亲书房里的旧藏。它们看上去多么熟悉，多么亲切！里面没有故事——只有画——可我一页页翻过去，耳边仿佛又响起祖母大人温软的声音，那些古老的传说仿佛又在我心里一一上演，分明如我童年的时日。母亲用朱砂小点标记了其中几页。其中一页画的是《枪山》里的一景。故事说的是佛陀的一位爱徒，痛失慈母，悲恸欲绝；慈悲的师尊便施展法力，把那哀伤的儿子带到一处可以望见母亲的地方。徒弟惊骇地看见，他挚爱的母亲正在一条由利枪铺成的山坡上，一步一步痛苦地攀爬。
「哦，慈悲的师尊啊，」他喊道，「您把我带到了『七山地狱』。我的母亲怎么会在这种地方？她一生一世，没做过一件恶事啊。」
「可她起过一个恶念。」佛陀悲悯地答道，「你还在襁褓里的时候，她一心只牵挂你。有一天她看见一只田鼠欢欢喜喜地玩耍，忽然动了念头，好想剪下它那条灰色的、丝缎似的尾巴，给你节日的衣裳做一根系带——单是这一个愿望，便算下了杀业。」
我含着半抹微笑合上画册，因为温婉而忧心的母亲这无声的告诫，我一下子就懂了；可我心里盛满感激的爱意，朝日本的方向恭恭敬敬拜了一拜，暗自决意：我对宝宝的爱，当使我待整个世界更加体贴、更加温柔。
宝宝最早接待的访客里，有一位是我们忠实的黑人洗衣妇明蒂（Minty）。她替妈妈洗衣已有多年；我来了之后，我那些古怪的衣裳又给她添了活计，她和和气气地一并接了下来。

===Original===

She had never spoken of them as being different from others, but several times I noticed her examining them with interest, especially my white foot mittens. These were made of cotton or silk, with the great toe separated, as is the thumb of a hand mitten. When she came upstairs to see the baby, the nurse was holding the little one on her lap, and Minty squatted down by her side and began talking baby talk, cooing and clucking in the most motherly fashion.
Presently she looked up.
“Can I see her feet?” she asked.
“Certainly,” said the nurse, turning up the baby’s long dress and cuddling the little pink feet in her hand. “My lawsy me!” cried Minty in a tone of the greatest astonishment. “If they ain’t jus’ like ourn!”
“Of course,” said the surprised nurse. “What did you think?”
“Why, the stockin’s is double,” said Minty, almost in a tone of awe, “and I s’posed they wuz two-toed folks.”
When the nurse told my husband he shouted with merriment and finally said, “Well, Minty has struck back for the whole European race and got even with Japan.”
The nurse was puzzled, but I knew very well what he meant. When I was a child it was a general belief among the common people of Japan that Europeans had feet like horses’ hoofs, because they wore leather bags on their feet instead of sandals. That is why one of our old-fashioned names for foreigners was “one-toed fellows.”
Neither Mother nor I knew much about the latest theories of taking care of babies; so I rocked Hanano to sleep with a lullaby. Whether or not it was the influence of the foreign atmosphere which so entirely surrounded me I do not know, but it seemed more natural for me to sing “Hush-a-bye, baby!” than the old Japanese lullaby that Ishi used to croon as she swayed back and forth with me snuggled comfortably against her back.

> “Baby, sleep! Baby, sleep!

Where has thy nurse gone?

She went far away to Grandmother’s home

Over the hills and valleys.

Soon she will bring to thee

Fish and red rice,

Fish and red rice.”

===Chinese===

她从没说这些衣服和旁人的有什么两样，可我好几次留意到，她带着兴味端详它们，尤其是我的白布脚套（foot mittens）。那东西用棉布或丝绸做成，大脚趾分开，就像手手套分出大拇指一样。她上楼来看宝宝的那天，保姆正把小家伙抱在膝上，明蒂挨着她在地板上蹲下身，说起宝宝话来，咕咕哝哝、咂嘴弄舌，一副十足的母亲腔调。
一会儿她抬起头来。
「能看看她的脚吗？」她问。
「当然。」保姆说着，撩起宝宝的长衣，把两只粉嫩的小脚拢进手心。「我的老天爷！」明蒂叫起来，满口是说不出的惊诧，「这不跟咱们的一模一样嘛！」
「可不就是嘛，」保姆也吃了一惊，「你还当是怎样的？」
「嗐，这袜子是双层的呀，」明蒂说，口气里几乎带着敬畏，「我一直当你们是两个脚趾的人种呢。」
保姆把这话学给我丈夫听，他放声大笑，末了说道：「好啊，明蒂这是替整个欧洲种族扳回一局，跟日本扯平了。」
保姆听得莫名其妙，我却再明白不过他是什么意思。我小时候，日本民间普遍相信欧洲人的脚长得像马蹄，因为他们脚上套的是皮口袋，不是草履。所以我们旧时称呼外国人，有一个名目便是「独趾的家伙」。
妈妈和我都不大懂得照料婴孩的最新学说，我便摇着花野（Hanano），唱摇篮曲哄她入睡。是不是周身弥漫的异国空气熏染了我，我说不清；只是唱起「宝宝睡，宝宝睡」（Hush-a-bye, baby!），似乎比阿石从前哼的那支日本老调更自然——那时她背着我摇来晃去，我舒舒服服地贴在她背上。
「宝宝睡！宝宝睡！
保母去哪儿了？
她到远远的外婆家去了，
翻过一道道山，一条条谷。
她就要带回来
鱼和赤饭，
鱼和赤饭。」

===Original===

It was not the foreign atmosphere, however, that was responsible for the prayer with which, as soon as she was old enough to lisp it, Hanano was tucked into her little bed at night. That dates back to the memory-stone day when my wonderful *Tales of the Western Seas* came to me. In one of the thin volumes of tough paper tied with silk cord was a musical little poem that I committed to memory, all unknowing that years after I would teach it, clothed in strange, foreign words, to my own little child. It was﻿—

> Ware ima inentosu.

Waga Kami waga tamashii wo mamoritamae.

Moshi ware mesamezushite shinaba,

Shu yo! waga tamashii wo sukuetamae.

Kore, ware Shu no nani yorite negotokoro nari.

> Now I lay me down to sleep.

I pray the Lord my soul to keep.

If I should die before I wake,

I pray the Lord my soul to take.

This I ask for Jesus’ sake.

===Chinese===

然而，花野一到能咿呀学语的年纪，每晚被安放进小床时的那句祷告，却不是异国空气的功劳。那要追溯到我的《西海奇谈》送到我手里的那个「记程石」之日。在那套用丝绳捆扎的韧纸小书里，有一支乐感十足的小诗，我把它背了下来，全然不知多年之后，我会给它换上一身陌生的外国字句，教给我自己的小孩子。那首诗是——
我今卧下入眠。
愿我神守护我的灵魂。
我若长眠不醒，
主啊！求你拯救我的灵魂。
此我所求，皆奉我主之名。
此刻我躺卧入睡，
求主看顾我的灵魂。
我若在醒来之前死去，
求主接取我的灵魂。
如此祈求，是为耶稣的缘故。

===Original===

There is a saying in Japan, “Only the fingers of a babe can tie a uniting knot that will pull two families together.” As the Japanese marriage is not an affair of individuals I had never applied the saying to Matsuo and myself, but one day some Mysterious Power twisted this bit of truth into an incident that played an unsuspected and important part in my life and in that of my husband.
Matsuo was a man who had always been vitally interested in his business. I think that, before the baby came, there had been nothing in his life to which it was second. He and I were very good friends, but we seldom talked freely to each other except in the presence of others. Indeed, we had no common topic of conversation; for he was interested in his own plans, and my mind was taken up with my home and my new friends. But from the day the baby came, everything was changed. Now we had many things to talk about, and for the first time I began to feel acquainted with my husband.
But always, deep in my heart, was the feeling that the baby was *mine*. I did not trace any likeness to Matsuo; nor did I want to. I do not mean that I objected to her resembling him, but that I never thought of her as really *belonging* to anyone but myself and my own family.
One day when I was in the city I stopped for a few moments at my husband’s store. He happened to be busy and I waited in the office. His desk looked to me in great disorder, and right in front, in a wide pigeonhole, was an odd thing to be in a cluttered-up office. It was a little lacquer box of exquisite workmanship and bearing a crest that is rarely seen outside a museum. I lifted the lid, and there, before my startled eyes, were three strange objects﻿—a green paper whirligig, some little pieces of clay the baby’s fingers had pressed into crude shapes, and a collapsed balloon.
I stood still, my heart beating quickly; then I turned away, feeling as if I had taken an unbidden glance into the heart of a stranger. In that moment came the realization that there was another claim on my baby as tender and as strong as my own, and with a throb of remorse my heart turned toward my husband with a strange new feeling.
Among the strong influences in Hanano’s life were the frequent calls and unfailing kindness of our good friend Mrs. Wilson.

===Chinese===

日本有句老话：「唯有婴孩的手指，才打得成把两家拉到一处的结。」日本的婚姻本就不是个人的事，所以我从不曾把这话往松雄和我自己身上想；可是有一天，冥冥之中有股力量，把这一点真意拧进了一桩小事里，而这件事在我和我丈夫的生命中，扮演了一个谁也料不到的重要角色。
松雄向来是把全部生气都倾在生意上的人。我想，在宝宝出世以前，他的生命里没有一样东西排在生意之上。他和我是一对很要好的朋友，可除非有旁人在场，我们难得敞开来说话。的确，我们之间没有共同的话题；他心里装的是他的种种计划，我心里装的是我的家和新交的朋友。可自打宝宝来的那天起，一切都变了。如今我们有说不完的话，我也头一回觉得，我开始真正认识自己的丈夫了。
可是在我心底深处，总存着一种感觉：宝宝是*我的*。我在她身上寻不出一点像松雄的地方，也不想去寻。我倒不是不愿意她像她父亲，而是从来不曾把她当作真正*属于*我本人和我娘家以外任何人的孩子。
一天进城，我在丈夫的铺子里逗留了片刻。他正巧脱不开身，我便在写字间里等。他那张书桌在我看来乱作一团，而正当中一个宽宽的信格子里，搁着一件在这凌乱屋子里显得古里古气的东西。那是一只小小的漆盒，做工精美，盒面上印着一枚纹章——那样的纹章，出了博物馆是难得见到的。我掀起盒盖，一霎间，三样古怪东西撞进我惊愕的眼睛——一只绿纸风车，几团被宝宝的小手指捏出粗陋形状的泥巴，还有一只瘪了的气球。
我呆立在那里，心怦怦直跳；随后我转过身去，只觉自己仿佛偷觑了一眼一个陌生人的心底。就在那一刻我明白了：对我的宝宝，还另有一份情分，同我这份一样温柔，一样深重；我心底一阵悔疚的悸动，怀着一种陌生的新情意，转向了我的丈夫。
在花野生命里种种有力的感化之中，还有我们好友威尔逊太太（Mrs. Wilson）频频的来访和她从不间断的慈爱。

===Original===

She seldom came that she did not bring flowers for Mother, and on Easter and family anniversaries our parlours were bowers of bloom from her generous conservatory.
One day, when Hanano was about a year old, she was sitting on Mother’s lap by the window when she saw the familiar carriage coming up the driveway. It stopped and Mrs. Wilson stepped out. Glancing up and seeing the baby she waved a white-gloved hand and smiled. The sun was shining on her stately figure in its gown of soft heliotrope shade, with flowers in her arms.
“Oh, oh!” cried the baby, joyfully clapping her hands. “Pretty Flower Lady! Pretty Flower Lady!”
Thus was she christened in the baby’s heart, and “Flower Lady” she has been to us all ever since. May the many blossoms which her generous hands have scattered far and wide bloom anew for her in all their symbolism of happiness and peace when she reaches the beautiful gardens across the river.
From the time when Hanano first recognized her father as a separate individual, he brought her toys, and she was no sooner toddling about and beginning to prattle than he spent most his leisure time in playing with her, carrying her about or even taking her to call on the neighbours.
One Sunday afternoon just after Matsuo had started off somewhere with her, Mother said: “I have never known a more devoted father than Matsuo. Are all Japanese men as unselfish with their children?”
“Why, I﻿—don’t﻿—know,” I replied slowly. “Aren’t American men fond of their children?”
“Oh, yes,” she answered quickly, “but Matsuo comes home early every evening to play with Hanano, and the other day he closed his store for the entire afternoon just to take her to the zoo.”
My mind went back to my father﻿—and Mr. Toda﻿—and other fathers; and suddenly I saw Japanese men in a new light. “They have no chance!” I thought, a little bitterly. “An American man can show his feelings without shame, but convention chains a Japanese man. It pulls a mask over his face, closes his lips, and numbs his actions. However a husband many feel toward his wife, he cannot in public show her affection, or even respect; nor does she wish him to. It is not good form. The only time a man of dignity dares betray his heart is when he is with a little child﻿—either his own or another’s. Then he has the only outlet that etiquette allows; and even then he must guide his actions by rule.

===Chinese===

她来时几乎没有不给妈妈带花的；每逢复活节和家中的纪念日，我们家的客厅便成了她那座慷慨暖房捧出来的花棚。
花野一岁上下的时节，有一天正坐在妈妈膝头，靠着窗，望见那辆熟悉的马车驶上车道。车停了，威尔逊太太走下来。她一抬眼望见宝宝，便挥起戴白手套的手，笑了。阳光照在她端庄的身影上——一袭藕荷色的软缎长裙，臂弯里抱满了花。
「哦，哦！」宝宝欢喜地拍起小手，「花太太！花太太！」
宝宝就这样在自己心里给她行了命名礼；从那时起，我们全家也都唤她「花太太」。愿她那双慷慨的手撒向四方的繁花，在她抵达河彼岸那座美丽花园的时候，带着幸福与安宁的全部花语，重新为她盛开。
自花野头一回认出父亲是一个独立的人起，松雄便不断给她带玩具；她才刚会摇摇摆摆满地走、咿咿呀呀学说话，他便把大半闲空都拿来陪她玩，抱着她到处走，甚至带她去串邻家的门。
一个礼拜天的下午，松雄刚带着她出门去哪儿，妈妈说道：「我从没见过比松雄更尽心尽力的父亲。日本男人对自己的孩子，都这样无私吗？」
「呃，我——说——不上来，」我慢吞吞地答，「美国男人不疼自己的孩子吗？」
「哦，疼，」她很快答道，「可松雄天天傍晚早早回家陪花野玩，前些天他索性把铺子关了整整一个下午，就为带她去动物园。」
我的心飘回我的父亲——还有户田先生——还有别的做父亲的人；霎时间，日本的男人在我眼前现出一副新面目。「他们没有机会啊！」我略带酸楚地想。「美国男人可以毫无愧色地表露感情，礼法却锁住了日本男人。它给他脸上罩一张面具，闭住他的嘴唇，麻住他的举止。丈夫对妻子心里纵有千般情意，也不能当众对她示爱，连敬意也不便表露；妻子也不要他表露。那是不成体统的。一个有身份的男人，唯一敢泄露真心的时候，便是同小孩子在一起——不管是自己的还是别人的孩子。那是礼法留给他唯一的出口；就连那时，他的一举一动也还得循着规矩来。」

===Original===

A father becomes his little son’s comrade. He wrestles with him, races with him, and acts with him scenes of samurai daring, but he loves his little daughter with a great tenderness and accepts her gentle caresses with a heart hunger that is such pathos it is tragedy.”
Matsuo was more demonstrative to me than would have been polite had we been living in Japan, but we both respected formality, and it was years before I realized how deep were his feelings for his family.
After that remark of Mother’s and the thoughts that it aroused I delayed Hanano’s bedtime, and she had many a romp with her father after the hour when children are supposed to be asleep. One moonlight evening I came down and found them running around the lawn, chasing each other and dodging this way and that, while Mother sat on the porch laughing and applauding. They were playing, “Shadow catch Shadow.”
“I used to play that on moonlight nights when I was a little girl,” I said.
“Why, is there a moon in Japan?” asked Hanano in great surprise.
“This very same one,” her father replied. “Wherever you go, all your life, you will see it above you in the sky.”
“Then it walks with me,” said Hanano with satisfaction, “and when I go to Japan, God can see my Japanese grandma.”
Matsuo and I glanced at each other, a little puzzled. Hanano had always associated the Man in the Moon with the face of God, but I did not know until afterward that she had heard a lady who was calling on Mother that afternoon express regret that “beautiful Japan is a country without God.”
Hanano’s odd idea was somewhat startling, but it was a pleasant one to her and I did not correct it. “She will learn soon enough in this practical country,” I thought with a sigh. In Japan children are saved many a puzzling heartache, for most of our people retain sympathy for childish illusions even to old age; thus poetic fancies are less apt to be too suddenly shattered. Daily life over there is full of mystic thought. To the masses of people, nothing in the active life about us is more real than the unseen forces which people the earth and air; and no day passes that does not bring to almost everyone some suggestion of the presence of kindly spirits. Most of the gods we look upon as friendly comrades, and the simple duties we owe them we perform with calm and pleasant feelings of gratitude and courtesy.

===Chinese===

做父亲的成了小儿子们的伙伴。他同他们摔跤，同他们赛跑，同他们演武士豪杰的戏文；可他爱小女儿，爱得深沉温柔，领受她轻轻的偎抱时，那副如饥似渴的心肠，直教人酸楚得近乎悲剧。
松雄待我，若在日本过日子，早已嫌亲昵逾分；可我们两个都敬着礼法，直到多年之后，我才明白他对家人的感情有多深。
自打妈妈那一问和它勾起的思绪之后，我便推迟了花野的上床时刻；孩子们本该入睡的钟点过后，她还能常常同父亲疯玩一场。一个有月亮的晚上，我下楼来，只见他们俩在草地上绕着圈儿互相追逐，东躲西闪，妈妈坐在游廊上，又笑又拍手。他们在玩「影子捉影子」。
「我小时候，月亮地里常玩这个呢。」我说。
「咦，日本也有月亮吗？」花野大为惊讶。
「就是这一轮。」她父亲答道，「你这辈子不管走到哪儿，抬头都会看见它挂在天上。」
「那它就是跟着我走的了，」花野一脸满足，「等我到了日本，上帝就看得见我的日本外婆了。」
松雄和我对望了一眼，都有点纳闷。花野一向把月中人影当作上帝的脸，可我直到后来才知道，那天下午有位来探望妈妈的太太，曾当着她叹惋说，「美丽的日本，是个没有上帝的国度」。
花野这个古怪的念头有点叫人吃惊，可在她心里是一桩美事，我便没有去纠正。「在这个讲求实际的国度，她很快就会学个够。」我叹口气想。在日本，孩子们省去了多少叫人心碎的困惑；因为我们大多数人直到老年，都还存着一份对童稚幻想的怜惜，诗意的想象便不至于被骤然击得粉碎。在那边的日常生活里，处处充盈着神秘的意绪。对寻常百姓来说，我们周遭这活跃的人世里，没有什么比那充盈天地空气之间的无形众灵更真实；几乎没有什么日子，不叫每一个人多多少少领略到友善精灵的临在。大多数神明，我们都看作和气的同伴；对他们，我们尽着些简简单单的本分，怀着平静而愉快的感激与敬意。

===Original===

There is little fear of penalty for neglect other than humiliation for a lack of politeness, which weighs a good deal with a Japanese. The house shrines remind us that relatives are watching over us, and we show our appreciation with incense and prayer. The fire goddess is the helpful ruler of the kitchen, whose thanks are the slender ends of a weave of cloth hung beside the kitchen firebox. The goodly god of rice asks that we keep the fire beneath the rice-kettle free from rubbish. The water goddess, who blesses the streams and rivers, demands that the wells be clean. The seven gods of fortune﻿—Industry, Wealth, Wisdom, Strength, Beauty, Happiness, and Long Life﻿—are seen everywhere and always greeted with a smiling welcome; and the two especially honoured by tradesmen, Industry and Wealth, are perched on a prominent shelf in every store, from which their faces look down, giving to the master the comfortable assurance that friends are near. The hideous gods beside temple doors are not hideous to us, for they are the fierce watchdogs who protect us from danger, and the gods of the air﻿—Thunder, Wind, and Rain﻿—are guardians for our good. Above all these lesser gods the Sun goddess, ancestress of our Imperial line, watches over the entire land with kindly, helpful light.
These various gods are a confused mixture of Shinto and Buddhist; for the religion of the masses vaguely combines both beliefs. As a rule this is not a religion of fear, although the evil spirits of the hells, if seriously accepted as pictured in ancient Buddhist books, are fearful indeed; but even they allow two days in each year when the repentant may climb to a higher plane. Thus, to the Japanese, even the sad and puzzling path of transmigration, into which unconscious footsteps so often wander, leads at last, after the long period of helplessness and gloom, to a final hope.
Buddhism, on its ages-long journey from India to Japan, seems to have dropped many of its original elements of terror; or else they were softened and lost in the goodly company of our jolly and helpful Shinto gods. Not one of these do we dread, for, in Shintoism, even Death is only a floating cloud through which we pass on our journey in the sunshine of Nature’s eternal life.

===Chinese===

至于怠慢了敬神的功课，倒几乎不怕什么责罚，所怕的不过是失礼之辱——这一层，在日本人心里分量着实不轻。家中的神龛提醒我们，亲眷的亡灵还在看顾着我们，我们便焚香祷告，聊表感念。灶神是厨房里帮衬的主宰，她所得的谢礼，是一方织物抽下的细纱，悬在灶膛旁边。堂堂的稻神只要求我们把饭锅底下的火拾掇干净，不留一点秽屑。赐福溪流江河的水神，要求水井洁净。七位福神——勤、富、智、力、美、福、寿——随处可见，人人都笑脸相迎；商人格外敬重其中两位，勤与富，端坐在每一家铺子显眼的架子上，居高临下地望着主人，叫他心里妥妥帖帖：朋友就在近旁。庙门两侧那些面目狰狞的神，在我们眼里并不狰狞，他们是护我们免遭凶险的猛犬；空中的诸神——雷、风、雨——是护佑我们的守护。在这一众小神之上，太阳女神，我们皇统的始祖，正以慈爱而有益的光，照临着整个国土。
这形形色色的神明，是神道与佛教驳杂的混合；因为大众的宗教，本就把两种信仰含含糊糊地融作一处。大体说来，这不是一个叫人惧怕的宗教；尽管古佛教典籍里描画的地狱恶鬼，若认真当真起来，也着实骇人；可就连他们，一年之中也宽限两日，许忏悔的人攀上更高一层。这样，对日本人来说，就连那条凄惶而费解的轮回之路——无知的脚步何等容易误入其中——在走过漫长的无助与幽暗之后，最终也通向一个希望。
佛教在从印度到日本的长途跋涉中，似乎一路抛下了许多原初的恐怖；要不然，便是那些恐怖在我们神道诸神——一群快活而肯帮衬的神明——的美意做伴中，被软化、被消磨掉了。这些神里没有一个叫我们畏惧；因为在神道里，就连死亡，也不过是一朵飘浮的云，我们穿过它，继续行在自然永恒生命的阳光里。

===Original===

Our man-made laws of convention have had more power in moulding the lives of the people and have left a more lasting stamp on their souls than have our gods. Our complex religion arouses the interest of the intellectual, and it teaches genuine resignation; but it does not guide the ignorant with a comprehending wisdom, nor does it give to the brooding and the sorrowful the immediate comfort of cheerfulness and hope that comes with a belief in the peasant priest of Nazareth.

===Chinese===

而我们人造的礼法，在塑造国民生活上，力量比我们的诸神更大，在人们灵魂上留下的印记也更经久。我们驳杂的宗教，能唤起智识者的兴味，也教给人真正的顺从；可它既不能以洞达的智慧引领蒙昧的人，也不能给那些愁绪萦怀、哀伤无告的人，那种立时便得的欢欣与盼望——那要信了拿撒勒的农家祭司，才会随之而来。

## XXIII Chiyo / 二十三 千代
===Original===

After Hanano had learned that the moon was a friend she could depend upon wherever she might travel, she became intensely interested in moon stories. I postponed telling her the legend of the white rabbit who is fated for all time to pound rice dough in a great wooden bowl, for it is his shadow which Japanese children see in every full moon; and I thought I would allow her to drift gradually from her idealization of the American legend. But I told her of our moon-gazing parties where families or groups of friends gather in some beautiful open spot and write poems praising the brilliant leaves of the moon vine which causes the glow of autumn that in America is called Indian summer.
We were sitting on the doorstep of the back parlour one evening, looking out across the porch at the moon sailing round and clear in a cloudless sky, and I told her how in Japan, on that very night, every house, from the palace of the Emperor to the hut of his humblest subject, would have on the porch or in the garden where it could catch the glow of the full moon a small table with fruits and vegetables﻿—everything round﻿—arranged in a certain manner, in honour of the goddess of the moon.
“Oh, how pretty!” cried Hanano. “I wish I could be there to see!”
There was the rustling of a newspaper behind us.
“Etsu,” called Matsuo, “there is some kind of a child’s story about that celebration. I remember once when my elder sister and I had been teasing our little sister, who was a timid child, that my aunt told us a story of gentle Lady Moon and naughty Rain and Wind who tried to spoil her pleasure on an August full-moon night.”
“Oh, tell me!” cried Hanano, clapping her hands and running to her father.
“I’m not much on stories,” said Matsuo, taking up his paper again, “but your mother will know it. Etsu, you tell it to her.”
So Hanano came back to the doorstep, and I tried to recall the half-forgotten story of

===Chinese===

花野自从知道月亮是一位朋友、随她走到哪里都靠得住，便对月亮的故事着了迷。
那白兔的传说，我暂且按下不讲——那兔子命定永远在一具大木钵里捣米团，日本孩子望圆月时看见的黑影就是它；我想让她从对美国月亮传说的美化里，慢慢自己漂出来。
但我给她讲了我们的赏月会：一家老小或一群好友，聚在某处风景开阔的地方，作诗赞美葛藤明亮的叶子——正是那藤叶，染出美国唤作「小阳春」的秋光。
一天晚上，我们坐在后客厅的门阶上，隔着游廊望那轮又圆又亮的月亮在无云的天上航行。我对她说，在日本，就在这样一夜，家家户户——从天皇的宫殿到最微贱小民的茅屋——都会在游廊上或庭院里月光照得到的地方，摆一张小桌，供上果品菜蔬——样样都是圆的——按一定的规矩陈设，敬奉月宫的女神。
「呀，多好看！」花野喊道。「真想也去那里看一看！」
身后传来一阵报纸的窸窣。
「阿津，」松雄喊道，「那个庆节有一个孩子听的故事。记得有一回，我和姐姐捉弄胆小的妹妹，婶母就给我们讲了一个故事：温柔的月娘子，和存心搅她兴致的风跟雨——在八月一个月圆之夜。」
「哦，讲给我听！」花野拍着手喊，朝父亲跑去。
「我不大会讲故事，」松雄说，重新拿起报纸，「你妈妈知道。阿津，你讲给她听。」
于是花野回到门阶上，我便试着追忆那个半已忘却的故事——

===Original===

> One pleasant evening in August the beautiful Lady Moon was sitting in front of her toilet stand. As she lifted the powder puff to clear and soften her bright colouring she said to herself:
> “I must not disappoint the Earth people tonight. Of all the nights of the year they look forward to the ‘Honourable Fifteenth,’ for this is the time when my beauty is at the crown of its glory.”
> Turning the mirror a trifle, she carefully arranged her fluffy collar.
> “It seems a poor sort of life﻿—to do just nothing but smile and look happy! But that is my only way to gladden the world, so tonight I will shine my brightest and best. And,” she added, as she peeped over the edge of her balcony and saw the Earth beneath, “after all, it is a pleasant duty﻿—especially tonight!”
> It was no wonder she smiled with pleasure, for the whole world was decorated in her honour. Every city and town, every little village, every lonely hut on the mountainside, and every humble fisher cot on the shore had upon its porch or placed in front where it could be seen by the eye of the Lady Moon a tiny table laden with treasure balls. There were rice dumplings, chestnuts, potatoes, persimmons, peas, and plums, and, standing in their midst, two circular sake vases, holding, stiff and upright, their folded white papers. Everything had been carefully selected as being the nearest a perfect round in shape that could be obtained, for “round” is the symbol of perfection, and on this night only the very best of everything was considered worthy to be shown to the pure and perfect “Lady of the Sky.”
> Mistress Rain, who lived near Lady Moon, peered through her misty windows with envious eyes. She saw the Earth houses decorated in honour of her neighbour, and caught the breath of the messages floating upward from the lips of young girls: “Great Mysterious! Make my heart as pure as the moonbeams and my life as perfect as the bright and round Lady Moon above!”
> As Mistress Rain listened she swished her skirts so viciously that all the umbrellas which decorated them suddenly flew open, and she had to clutch them quickly to keep the water with which they were filled from spilling over the Earth. Even as it was, a shower of drops fell sparkling through the moonlight, and the Earth people looked up in surprise.
> “I haven’t seen the like since last August,” continued the angry Mistress Rain.

===Chinese===

> 八月里一个宜人的夜晚，美丽的月娘子正坐在妆台前。她拿起粉扑，扑了扑脸上明艳的颜色，自言自语道：
> 「今夜我万不可教地上的人们失望。一年之中的夜晚里，他们顶盼的就是『十五之夜』，因为这正是我的美到了极盛的时候。」
> 她把镜子微微转了转，细心整了整那蓬松的领圈。
> 「这样的日子似乎太清闲了——什么也不做，只是微笑，只是快快活活！可这是我给世界添欢喜的唯一法子，所以今夜我要放出我最亮最好的光。」她又探头往阳台边上望了望底下的人间，添了一句：「说到底，这也是桩愉快的差事——尤其今夜！」
> 难怪她笑得那样甜——整个世界都张灯结彩地贺她的节。每一座城、每一个镇、每一个小村庄、山坡上每一座孤零零的茅屋、海边每一间低矮的渔舍，都在游廊上或门前月娘子眼睛望得见的地方，摆了一张小桌，堆满圆圆的宝物：米团子、栗子、山药、柿子、豆子、李子；当中立着两只圆酒壶，壶口端端正正插着折好的白纸。样样都经过细心挑选，是所能寻到的最圆最圆的形状——因为「圆」是圆满的象征；而这一夜，只有顶好顶好的东西，才配呈给纯洁圆满的「天上娘娘」过目。
> 雨太太住在月娘子近旁，正隔着雾蒙蒙的窗子，眼红地窥望。她看见地上的人家为她的邻人张灯结彩，又听见姑娘们飘上来的祝告：「伟大的神妙啊！愿我的心像月光一样纯净，愿我的一生像天上那又亮又圆的月娘子一样圆满！」
> 雨太太听着，狠狠一甩裙裾——裙上缀着的那些伞忽地全张了开来，她慌忙一把把住，才没叫伞里的水泼到人间去。饶是这样，也洒下一天亮晶晶的水珠，穿过月光落下来，地上的人们吃惊地抬头张望。
> 「打去年八月起，还没见过这样的光景，」雨太太怒气冲冲地继续说道。

===Original===

“Every flower vase on the earth appears to be filled with August moon-flowers, and all the porches are newly polished and spread with finest cushions, so the honourable aged ones may be seated where they can behold the glory of Lady Moon. It is not fair!”
> There was another swish, and again a shower of rain drops went sparkling through the moonlight.
> Just then the Wind god sailed by, holding tight in his hands the ends of his bag of breezes. Mistress Rain noticed the dark scowl on his brow, and called:
> “Good evening, Kase no kami San! I am glad to see you passing this way. You look as if you are searching for unexpected work.”
> The Wind god stopped and seated himself upon a cloud, still holding tight to the ends of the bag.
> “Earth beings are the queerest of creatures!” he complained. “Lady Moon lives in the world of Sky, and so do we; yet they think only of her! She has an honourable title given to her, and not a single month of the year passes that the fifteenth day is not observed in her honour. Even on the third day, when she climbs out of her cellar, they welcome her face as she peeps over the wall with such joy that one would think they had never expected to see her again!”
> “Yes, yes!” excitedly cried Mistress Rain, “and especially this August night! They always look with anxious eyes for fear that you or I may appear, although uninvited and unwelcome.”
> “This August night!” exclaimed the Wind god with great scorn. “Yes, this very night I’d like to show those Earth creatures what I could do!”
> “It would be such fun,” said sly Mistress Rain, “to go with a rush and upset all the things displayed in honour of Lady Moon.”
> “Ho! Ho! Ho!” laughed the Wind god, so pleased with the idea that he loosened his hold on one end of the bag, and a sudden gust of wind swept through the sky, causing consternation among the Earth people.
> Lady Moon was quietly and calmly smiling upon the world, her mind busy with gentle and unselfish thoughts, when the Wind god and Mistress Rain silently slipped behind the mountains and journeyed a long way so that they could come unexpectedly from the side of the sea. But Lady Moon saw them, and, sad and disappointed, she hid behind a curtain while her triumphant enemies swept on over the world.
> Oh, it was a terrific whirl of angry Wind and Rain!

===Chinese===

> 「地上的花瓶里，朵朵插满了八月的月光花；游廊个个新擦得亮亮的，铺着顶细软的坐垫，好让尊长的老人们坐下来瞻仰月娘子的光辉。太不公平了！」
> 又是一声甩裙子的哗啦，又是一阵雨珠，亮晶晶地穿过月光洒下来。
> 正这时，风神驶过，两手紧紧攥着他那袋风的袋口。雨太太看见他眉间一团怒气，便招呼道：
> 「晚上好，风神大人（Kase no kami San）！您打这儿过，真叫我高兴。瞧您这样子，像是正找一份不寻常的活计呢。」
> 风神停住，在一朵云上坐下来，手里还紧紧攥着袋口。
> 「地上的生灵真是顶古怪的东西！」他抱怨道。「月娘子住在天界，我们不也住在天界？他们心里却只有她！她得了尊贵的封号，一年里没有一个月，十五那天不替她过节。就连初三她从地窖里爬出来、刚在墙头探一探脸，他们也欢天喜地迎接，那股欢喜劲儿，倒像一辈子没指望再见到她似的！」
> 「正是，正是！」雨太太激动地喊道，「尤其这八月的一夜！他们总提心吊胆地张望，就怕你我露面——不请自来，谁也不欢迎。」
> 「这八月的一夜！」风神满脸鄙夷地喊道。「好啊，就在今夜，我倒要教教地上那些生灵，看看我的本事！」
> 「那才有趣呢，」狡黠的雨太太说，「冷不防冲过去，把供月娘子的那些摆设统统掀翻。」
> 「嚯！嚯！嚯！」风神大笑起来，这主意叫他欢喜得松开了一头袋口——一阵狂风猛地扫过天际，地上的人们顿时大乱。
> 月娘子正安安静静地对着人间微笑，心里想着温良无私的念头；风神和雨太太却悄悄溜到群山背后，绕了老远的路，好从海上冷不防地扑过来。可月娘子看见了他们，又伤心，又失望，躲进一重帘幕后头；她那得胜的仇家便横扫过世界。
> 唉呀，那是狂怒的风和雨的一场大旋涡！

===Original===

On rushed the god, pushing his big bag before him with loosened ends, and close behind whirled Mistress Rain with a loud “swish!﻿—swish!” as torrents of water poured from the hundreds of wide-open umbrellas on her skirts.
> But, ah, what disappointment was theirs! The rollicking laugh of the Wind god, which had loosened for an instant his hold on the end of the bag, had been warning enough, even if the sharp-eyed Earth people had not seen the clouds of mist sweeping around the mountains. Every house was prepared for the storm. The beautiful little tables had disappeared, and the wild rushes of Wind and Rain were met by closed wooden doors. They howled and shrieked and darted and whirled until both were exhausted; then, with the god muttering and Mistress Rain weeping, they hurried across the valley to their homes.
> When all was once more quiet the sorrowful Lady Moon lifted her head.
> “My pleasure is spoiled!” she sighed. “The beautiful decorations of the Earth houses are now hidden, and the people have closed their eyes in sleep.”
> Suddenly a brilliant smile spread over her face, and she said bravely:
> “But I will do my duty! Even though no one sees me, I will smile my brightest and best!”
> She pushed aside her curtain and looked down upon the world. Her gentle, unselfish sweetness received its reward, for all the doors of the Earth houses were open wide, and the people were gathered on the porches watching for her face. When it appeared songs of welcome floated upward.
> “Oh, see the beautiful Lady Moon!” the voices cried. “Again she smiles upon us! After a storm she is always doubly beautiful, and all the world is doubly glad!”

===Chinese===

> 风神冲将过来，把敞开的大口袋推在身前；雨太太紧随其后，打着旋儿，裙上百十把张开的伞哗啦！——哗啦！地泻下倾盆的水。
> 可是，唉，他们失望啦！风神那一阵放浪的大笑——笑得他一时松了袋口的——早就是足够的警信，何况眼睛尖利的人们又亲眼看见了绕山的雾阵。家家都防备了风暴：美丽的小桌不见了踪影，风和雨的狂扑只撞上一扇扇关严的木门。他们嚎、叫、扑、旋，直闹得筋疲力尽；末了，风神嘟嘟囔囔，雨太太哭哭啼啼，急忙忙穿过山谷，各自回家去了。
> 万籁重归寂静，伤心的月娘子才抬起头来。
> 「我的良宵毁了！」她叹道。「人间各家美丽的陈设都藏了起来，人们也阖眼睡了。」
> 忽然，她脸上绽开一片明亮的笑，勇敢地说：
> 「可我还要尽我的本分！哪怕没有一个人看我，我也要笑得最亮、最好！」
> 她掀开帘幕，俯望人间。她那温良无私的甜美得了报偿：家家户户的门都敞得开开的，人们聚在游廊上，等着她的脸。她一露面，欢迎的歌声便袅袅升上来。
> 「啊，看那美丽的月娘子！」歌声唱道。「她又朝我们笑了！风雨过后，她总是加倍地美，全世界也加倍地欢喜！」

===Original===

“That’s a very moral story,” said Hanano thoughtfully. “I feel kind of sorry for Mr. Wind and Mrs. Rain, but I love Lady Moon. Let us fix a table like they have in Japan. Clara will give us the things and the moonshine is beautiful on our porch edge.”
“I have something just as good,” said Matsuo, starting for the stairway. “Wait a moment.”
He brought a small wooden box and put it on the table. It was a phonograph with records on spools of wax and with a little horn attached, into which we could talk and make records of our own voices. Matsuo was to start to Japan in a few days on a business trip and he had selected the phonograph as a gift for my mother, that it might carry to her the voice of her little granddaughter. We called Mother, and all of us had quite an exciting time watching Matsuo arrange the machine. Then he took his seat before it, with Hanano on his lap, and they had a rehearsal. Not until she began to prattle away in her sweet, childish English did it dawn upon us that her puzzled grandmother would not be able to understand a word that she said.
This made us realize what a little American we had in our Japanese nest, and brought directly before us one of the great problems of Japan.
“If our daughter were a boy,” Matsuo said that night, “we might have reason to look serious. I should not want to prepare my son to live in a country where, if capable, he would not be welcome to occupy the highest position his country has to offer its citizens.”
“Even for our daughter,” I replied, “there is no permanent place in this country; nor in Japan either, with only an American education.”
The result of this conversation was that when Matsuo returned from Japan he brought an entire set of school readers, from kindergarten to high school; also the five steps of articles for the Doll Festival. This festival is ages old and educational in character. Anyone who understands it thoroughly has a nearly complete knowledge of Japanese folklore, history, customs, and ideals. Every girl has a doll festival set, and when she marries, takes it with her to her new home. The set Matsuo brought to Hanano was mine﻿—the one which Brother objected to my bringing with me to America.

===Chinese===

「这个故事很教人学好，」花野想了想说。「风先生和雨太太有点儿可怜，可是我爱月娘子。咱们也照日本的样子摆一张桌子吧。克拉拉会给咱们东西，月亮照在游廊边上，多好看呀。」
「我有一样同样好的东西，」松雄说着朝楼梯走去。「等等。」
他抱来一只小木箱，放在桌上。那是一架留声机，蜡筒上可以灌唱片，还带一只小喇叭，对着它说话，便能录下自己的声音。过几天松雄要动身去日本办事，他挑了这架留声机送给我母亲，好教小孙女的声音随它传到她那里去。我们把妈妈唤来，一家人围着看松雄摆弄机器，热闹了好一阵。然后他坐到机器跟前，花野坐在他膝上，先排演了一遍。直到她用那甜美的、孩子腔的英语叽叽呱呱说起来，我们才恍然明白：她那莫名其妙的日本外婆，一个字也听不懂。
这使我们看清，我们这日本窝里养了一只怎样的小美国人；也把日本的一大难题，直直地摆到了我们眼前。
「要是咱们生的是儿子，」那天夜里松雄说，「咱们倒真该发愁了。我不愿教儿子准备在那样一个国度里过活：他纵然有本事，也不配坐上国家能给公民的最高位子——没人欢迎他去坐。」
「就是女儿，」我答道，「在这个国度也没有长久的立足之地；光受美国教育，回日本也一样没有。」
这番谈话的结果是：松雄从日本回来时，带回了整套的教科书，从幼稚园一直到中学；还有女儿节那五层的一套陈设。这个节日古已有之，性质是启智的：凡是通晓它的人，对日本的民间传说、历史、风俗、理想，便近乎无所不晓。日本姑娘人人有一套女儿节人偶，出嫁时随身带到夫家。松雄带给花野的那套，正是我的——当年哥哥还不许我带来美国的那一套呢。

===Original===

When the set came we all went out to the big, light carriage house, and after William had opened the rough board box, Matsuo and he carefully lifted out the smooth, various-sized whitewood boxes, each holding a doll. My eyes fell on a long, flat package wrapped in purple crêpe bearing the Inagaki crest.
“Why, Mother has sent the Komoro *kamibina*!” I cried in astonishment, lifting the package respectfully to my forehead.
“I thought all the Komoro dolls were gone except the two that you used to play with,” said Mother.
“The *kamibina* are different,” said Matsuo.
“Yes,” I said slowly, “the *kamibina* are different. They belong to the family. They can never be sold, or given away, or disposed of in any way. My mother must have had these put away for years﻿—and now she has sent them to me.”
I was touched, for it brought forcibly before me the truth that I was the last of the “honourable inside” of the house of Inagaki. A doll festival set belonged to the daughter; the master of the house having no control over the home department.
No doll festival set, however elaborate, is complete without these two long, odd-shaped dolls. In olden time they were always of paper. Later, extravagant families sometimes made them of brocade or crêpe, but however rich the material, they were *called* paper dolls and were always folded in the same crude shape of the primitive originals. When the set is arranged for the celebration, these dolls have no fixed place, as all the others have, but may be put anywhere, except on the top shelf reserved for the Emperor and Empress.
The origin of the Doll Festival reaches back to the crude days of Shintoism. At that time a sinful person would seek purification by bathing in a stream. As time passed, and power or riches brought independent thought, it became customary for the lazy and the luxurious to send a substitute. Still later, an inanimate sacrifice in human form was considered satisfactory, and from anything near and dear as a part of one’s own self the two images were made. There were tiny wooden spools, two cocoons or simply shaped bunches of floss, the most valued possession of weaving villages; even crudely cut vegetables in farming districts. There were always two, supposed to be male and female, thus representing the entire family﻿—both men and women members.

===Chinese===

人偶运到那天，我们全家都到那间宽大明亮的车房去。威廉撔开粗板条箱，松雄和他小心翼翼地捧出一只只光滑的、大小不一的白木匣，每匣装着一个人偶。我的目光落在一个长长的扁包裹上——紫绉纱裹着，上头印着稻垣家的家纹。
「哎呀，我的母亲把小诸（Komoro）的纸雏人偶（kamibina）寄来了！」我又惊又喜，恭恭敬敬把包裹举到额前。
「我原以为小诸的人偶都散尽了，只剩你从前玩的两个。」妈妈说。
「纸雏人偶不一样。」松雄说。
「是，」我慢慢地说，「纸雏人偶不一样。它们是家里的东西，永远不能卖，不能送人，不能有半点处置。我的母亲必定把它们收存了好些年——如今寄给了我。」
我感动了；这叫我痛切地想起：我是稻垣家「内眷」的最后一人了。女儿节的人偶归女儿所有；一家之主，对内宅的东西是做不了主的。
无论多么奢华的女儿节陈设，少了这两个细长古怪的人偶，便不算齐全。古时候它们一律用纸做。后来，奢侈人家也有用锦缎绉纱做的，可料子再贵重，名义上还叫纸人偶，形状也永远照着太初原样那副质朴的折法。过节摆设的时候，别的人偶都有定位，这两个却没有，搁在哪里都行——只是顶层不行，那是留给天皇皇后的。
女儿节的源头，一直上溯到神道太初的年代。那时候，获罪的人要到溪流里沐浴，以求洗净。时日推移，权势和财富带来了独立的想头，懒惰的人和安逸的人便兴出了替身代浴的法子。再后来，人们觉得一件人形的无生物作替身也就尽了礼数；于是拿各人身边顶亲近、顶心爱、算得自身一部分的东西，做成那一对人偶：小小的木线轴啦，两只蚕茧或简单团起的丝绵啦——那是织布村里最贵重的物事；种田的地方，甚至有粗粗削成的菜蔬。人偶总是两个，一雄一雌，代表全家——男女老幼，一体在内。

===Original===

Gradually, dolls rudely cut from paper﻿—a precious material in those days﻿—came to be universally used and were called *kamibina* which means “paper dolls.”
In time one fixed date was decided upon for universal atonement, and the “First Serpent Day of Spring” was chosen, because the time of the dragon’s change of skin is symbolic of the slipping from winter’s darkness of sin into the light and hope of spring. That date is the one still observed.
In the days of shogun power, when the Emperor was considered too sacred to be seen, this festival represented an annual visit from the invisible ruler to show his personal interest in his people; thus it encouraged loyalty to the loved and unseen Emperor. In feudal times, when, in the samurai class, a wife’s duties became those of her absent husband, and children were necessarily left to the care of high-bred attendants, this festival became, in those families, the only opportunity for girls to be trained in the domestic duties which were such an essential part of every Japanese girl’s education.
The lunar calendar advanced “First Serpent Day” to March 3rd, and after Hanano’s set came, we celebrated that day each year just as it is done in Japan. Five steps were put up in the parlour and covered with red cloth. On these we arranged the miniature Emperor and Empress with court ladies, musicians, and various attendants. There were also doll furniture and household implements. On the lowest steps were tiny tables with food prepared by Hanano herself, with some help from me, and served by her to the playmates who were always invited to join her. And so “Third Day of Third Month” came to be looked forward to by Hanano’s little American friends just as it has been by little Japanese girls for almost a thousand years.
One of these celebrations, when Hanano was almost five years old, was an especially busy day for her, as, in addition to her duties as hostess, she received several telephone messages of congratulation, to which, with a feeling of great importance, she replied in person. Her happy day was made more so because her best friend, Susan, brought her little sister, a delicate-faced, golden-haired child who was just learning to walk. Hanano was a gracious hostess to all, but she was especially attentive to the dainty little toddler.

===Chinese===

渐渐地，用纸剪出的粗朴人偶——那年头纸还是贵重材料——用得人人皆是，唤作纸雏人偶，意思就是「纸人偶」。
后来，普天赎愆的日子定下了一个固定日期，选的是「春季第一个巳日」；因为龙蛇蜕皮的时节，正象征着从冬天罪恶的黑暗里，蜕进春天的光明与希望。这个日子，至今仍照旧奉行。
幕府当权的年代，天皇尊贵得不能示人，这个节日便象征着那位看不见的君王一年一度的临幸，表示他亲身垂顾他的子民；于是更坚固了人们爱戴那位未见之君的忠心。到了封建时代，武士人家的妻子，要代不在家的丈夫履行职责，儿女只好托给有教养的侍仆照管，这个节日在那些人家便成了唯一的时机，让女孩儿受一受家政的训练——那原是日本女子教育里顶要紧的一课。
阴历把「第一个巳日」挪到了三月三日。花野那套人偶来后，我们年年照日本的样子过这一天。客厅里搭起五层台阶，蒙上红布，上面排开小小的天皇皇后，带宫女、乐师和各色侍从；还有人偶的家具和日用器具。最低几层摆着小桌，桌上的吃食由花野亲手备办——我略帮了帮忙——由她端给每次必请的小客人们。于是「三月三日」，渐渐也成了花野的美国小朋友们盼望的日子，一如它千年来是日本小姑娘们盼望的日子。
有一年过节，花野快五岁了，那天她格外忙：做女主人之外，还接了好几通贺节的电话；她自觉责任重大，每一通都亲自答复。这一天更加圆满，是因为她的好友苏珊（Susan）带来了小妹妹——一个眉目清秀、满头金发、刚学走路的孩子。花野对客人个个殷勤有礼，对那娇小小娃儿却照料得格外经心。

===Original===

That night when she was ready for her usual evening prayer she looked up at me very seriously.
“Mamma, may I say to God just what I please?” she asked.
“Yes, dear,” I replied, but I was startled when, from the little bowed figure with clasped hands, came a sudden, “Hello, God!”
I reached out my hand to check her. Then I remembered that I had always taught her to respect her father next to God, and that was the greeting she used to him when he was too far away to be seen. I softly withdrew my hand. Then again I was startled by the solemn little voice, whispering, “Please give me a little sister like Susan’s.”
I was too much surprised to speak, and she went on with “Now I lay me” to the end.
As I tucked her into bed I said, “How did you happen to ask God for a little sister, Hanano?”
“That’s how Susan got her sister,” she replied. “She prayed for her a long time, and now she’s here.”
I went away a little awed, for I knew her prayer would be answered.
The March festival was long past, and May almost gone, when one morning Hanano’s father told her that she had a little sister and led her into the room where the baby was. Hanano gazed with wide-open, astonished eyes upon black-haired, pink-faced little Chiyo. She said not a word but walked straight down the stairs to Grandma.
“I didn’t pray for *that*,” she told Mother, with a troubled look. “I wanted a baby with yellow hair like Susan’s little sister.”
Clara happened to be in the room, and with the freedom of an American servant, said, “Yellow hair on a Japanese baby would be a funny sight!” and burst out laughing.
“It’s *not* a Japanese baby!” Hanano indignantly cried. “I didn’t *ask* for a Japanese baby! I don’t *want* a Japanese baby!”
Mother took the child on her lap and told her how proud we all were to have two little Japanese girls in our home, and so brought a slow comfort to the disappointed little heart.
That afternoon Mother saw Hanano sitting a long time very quietly in front of the big mirror that stood between the two front windows of the parlour.
“What is it you see, dear?” Mother asked.
“I s’pose I’m a Japanese girl, too,” Hanano answered slowly. “I don’t look like Susan or Alice.”
She winked several times very fast, then, with a choking gulp, her loyalty to blue eyes and yellow hair succumbed to loyalty to love, and she added, “But Mamma is pretty! I’m going to be like her!”

===Chinese===

那天夜里，她照例要念睡前的祷词了，忽然一本正经地仰起脸看我。
「妈妈，我可以想跟上帝说什么就说什么吗？」她问。
「可以呀，宝贝。」我答道。可等那小小的身影合掌低下头去，忽然冒出一句「喂，上帝！」我还是吓了一跳。
我伸手想去拦她。随即想起，我一向教她：父亲是仅次于上帝要敬重的人；而当父亲远得望不见时，她招呼他用的正是这一声「喂」。我轻轻收回了手。接着那严肃的小嗓音又叫我吃了一惊——只听她低声说：「请给我一个小妹妹，像苏珊家的那样。」
我惊讶得说不出话来。她自顾自把「现在我躺下安睡」念到了底。
替她掖被角时，我问：「花野，你怎么想起跟上帝讨一个小妹妹呢？」
「苏珊的妹妹就是这么来的呀。」她答。「她求了好久好久，妹妹就来了。」
我走出来，心里带着几分敬畏——因为我知道，她的祷告必蒙应允。
三月节早过完了，五月也将尽，一天早晨，花野的爸爸告诉她，她有小妹妹了，领她进那间躺着婴儿的屋子。花野睁大惊奇的眼睛，望着黑头发、粉面孔的小千代（Chiyo）。她一句话没说，径自下楼找奶奶去了。
「我没有求这样的呀，」她满脸困惑地对奶奶说。「我想要一个黄头发的娃娃，像苏珊家小妹妹那样的。」
恰巧克拉拉也在房里，便带着美国仆人的那种自由劲儿说：「日本娃娃长黄头发，那才好看呢！」说完放声大笑。
「她不是日本娃娃！」花野气呼呼地喊。「我没有求过日本娃娃！我不要日本娃娃！」
奶奶把孩子抱到膝上，跟她说，家里有了两个日本小姑娘，我们个个多么得意；这才慢慢安慰了那颗失望的小心灵。
那天下午，奶奶看见花野在客厅两扇前窗之间那面大镜子跟前，安安静静坐了好久。
「看见什么啦，宝贝？」奶奶问。
「我大概也是个日本姑娘吧，」花野慢慢答道。「我不像苏珊，也不像爱丽丝（Alice）。」
她飞快地眨了好几下眼睛，接着喉咙里哽了一口——她那忠于蓝眼睛黄头发的忠心，终于降给了忠于爱的忠心——她添上一句：「可是妈妈好看！我要长得像她！」

===Original===

and climbed down from the chair.
No one can sound the depths of a child’s thoughts, but from that day Hanano developed an interest in Japanese things. Matsuo was fond of listening to her prattle and of playing with her, but she depended upon me for stories; and so, night after night, I would talk of our heroes and repeat to her the songs and fairy lore which had been part of my child life. Best of all she liked to have me talk of the pretty black-haired children﻿—I always said they were pretty﻿—who made chains of cherry blossoms or played games in a garden with a stone lantern and a curving bridge that spanned a pond set in the midst of flowers and tiny trees. I almost grew homesick as I painted these word pictures for her, or sat in the twilight singing a plaintive Japanese lullaby to the baby, while Hanano stood beside me, humming softly beneath her breath.
Was this sudden love for the land she had never seen an inheritance, or﻿—for children sometimes seem to be uncannily endowed with insight﻿—was it premonition?
One day the old familiar world ended for me, leaving me with memories﻿—comforting ones and regretful ones﻿—all closely wrapped in a whirl of anxious, frightened questioning, for no longer had I a husband or my children a father. Matsuo, with a last merry word and a sleepy smile, had quickly and painlessly slipped over the border into the old-new country beyond our ken.
And now, for my children and myself, nothing was left but farewells and a long, lonely journey. The country that had reached out so pleasant a welcome to me, that had so willingly pardoned my ignorance and my mistakes, the country where my children were born and where I had received kindness greater than words can express﻿—this wonderful, busy, practical country had no need of, nor did it want, anything that I could give. It had been a broad, kindly, loving home for me and mine, but a place for the present only. It held no promise of usefulness for my growing children and had no need of my old age. And what is life if one can only learn, and of what one learns give nothing?
The past years were like a dream. From a land of misty, poetic ideas I had drifted through a puzzling tangle of practical deeds, gathering valuable thoughts as I floated easily along, and now﻿—back to the land of mist and poesy. What was ahead of me? I wondered.

===Chinese===

说着，从椅子上爬了下来。
孩子的心思，谁也量不到底。可是打那天起，花野对日本的东西渐渐有了兴致。松雄爱听她叽叽喳喳，爱陪她玩；故事却全指望我。于是夜复一夜，我给她讲我们的英雄，教她唱那些歌谣、讲那些仙怪——原是我童年的家常。她顶爱听的，是我讲那些黑头发的漂亮孩子——我总说他们漂亮——他们拿樱花编花链，或在园子里做游戏，园中有石灯笼，一道弯弯的小桥跨在池塘上，池塘四围尽是花草和小小的树。我给她描画这些光景，几乎想家想得发疼；或是坐在暮色里，给婴儿哼一支幽幽的日本摇篮曲，花野站在旁边，压着嗓子轻轻地跟着哼。
她对那从未见过的国土忽然生出的爱，是血里带来的呢，还是——孩子有时仿佛天赋奇异的先见——是预感呢？
有一天，我那熟稔的旧世界忽然到了头，只给我留下些回忆——宽慰人的和悔恨人的——都紧紧裹在一阵焦灼惊惶的疑云里：我从此没有了丈夫，我的孩子从此没有了父亲。松雄带着最后一句玩笑话、一个睡意朦胧的微笑，安安迅迅、毫无痛楚地越过了边境，滑进我们眼界之外那个亦旧亦新的国度去了。
如今，留给我和孩子们的，只有道别和一场漫长孤寂的旅程了。这个曾向我伸出那样和暖的欢迎之手的国度，这样宽宏地饶恕过我的无知和过失的国度，我两个孩子出生的、我领受了言语说不尽的恩惠的国度——这个了不起的、忙碌的、务实的国度，却用不着我能给的一切，也不想要。它曾是我和我的孩子们一个宽广、仁厚、慈爱的家，却只是眼前的家。它不会给我日渐长大的孩子什么用世的许诺，也用不着我的晚年。人若只能领受、领受了却一无回赠，那还叫什么人生呢？
过去的岁月像一场梦。我从一片雾蒙蒙的诗之国土出发，一路顺流漂过一座纷乱的、务实的事功之林，随波拾得了许多可贵的思想，如今——又要漂回那雾与诗之乡去了。前面等着我的是什么呢？我不知道。

## XXIV In Japan Again / 二十四 重回日本
===Original===

When the weary sight of tumbling and tossing waves was past and I was once again in Japan, I found myself in the midst of surroundings almost as strange as those I had met when I landed in America.
The provinces and classes in Japan had for so many centuries held steadfast, each to its own customs, that even yet there were only occasional evidences to be seen of their slow yielding to the equalizing influences of modern life; and I had gone at once to Matsuo’s home in western Japan, where standards of dress and etiquette, ideals, and even idioms of speech were entirely different from those of either Nagaoka or Tokyo.
We were met on our arrival by a crowd of Matsuo’s relatives, all in ceremonial dress, for we had brought the sacred ashes with us; and from then until the forty-nine days of ceremonies for the dead were over, I was treated as an honoured messenger-guest. After that my position was very humble, for a son’s widow is an unimportant person in Japan, and, virtually, that is what I was, Matsuo having been, until he decided to remain in America, the adopted son of Uncle Otani.
I was very anxious about my little girls; for in Japan children belong to the family﻿—not to the parents. Hanano, on the death of her father, had become the head of our little family, but we were only a branch of the main family of which Uncle Otani was the head. So it had been taken for granted by all relatives, my own as well as Matsuo’s, that the children and I would make our home with Uncle Otani. He would have made room for me in his handsome house and would have supplied me with beautiful clothes, but I should have had no authority, even over my own children. This might not have been so bad under some circumstances; for Uncle Otani would have been generous in giving the children every advantage that he considered proper for them to have. But with all his kindness﻿—and a kinder man never lived﻿—I could not forget that he belonged to the old-fashioned merchant class that considered education beyond the grammar school undesirable for girls.
The situation was difficult; for, from my humble position, I could not say a word. But I had one hope. Hanano, although legal head of our family, was a minor; and her mother, as present regent, held a certain power. Exerting this, I asked for a consultation with Uncle Otani.

===Chinese===

等那教人困乏的、颠簸翻腾的波涛终于望到头，我又站在日本土地上的时候，我发觉自己又落进了一片几乎同样陌生的境地，一如当年初登美国那日。
日本的各藩国与各等级，几百年来各守各的习俗，纹丝不动；纵到如今，也只有零零星星的痕迹，看得出它们在慢慢让步于现代生活那种把众生拉平的力量。而我一到日本，便径直去了松雄在西日本的家；那里的衣冠规矩、理想观念，乃至说话的腔调习语，都与长冈不同，与东京也不同。
我们到达时，迎接我们的是一大群松雄的亲族，个个穿着礼服，因为我们随身带回了神圣的骨灰。从那时起，直到四十九日的丧仪法事做毕，我一直被当作一位可敬的使者之客款待。此后，我的地位便一落千丈；因为在日本，儿子的遗孀是个无足轻重的人，而我实际上正是如此——松雄在决意留在美国之前，本是大谷叔父的养子。
我为我的两个小女儿忧心忡忡；因为在日本，孩子属于家族，不属于父母。父亲一死，花野便成了我们这个小家的家主；可我们不过是主家的一房，主家的家主是大谷叔父。因此双方亲族——我娘家的和松雄家的——都认定，孩子们和我自然会住进大谷叔父家里去。他会在他那座漂亮的宅子里给我腾出地方，会供给我华美的衣裳；可我将没有任何权威，连对自己亲生儿女的权威也没有。换一种情形，这或许还不算太糟；因为凡是合他心意的便宜，大谷叔父都会慷慨地给孩子们。可是——他再仁厚不过，世上再没有比他更仁厚的人了——我忘不了他属于旧式的商家一等人；那一等人认为，女孩子书读到小学以上，便是不宜。
局面很难；因为我地位卑微，一句话也说不得。可我还有一线希望。花野虽然名分上是一家之主，到底还未成年；她母亲暂代摄理，手中便握有几分权力。我便行使这权力，请求与大谷叔父一谈。

===Original===

I explained to him that Matsuo had expressed in his will a desire that, since he had no son, his daughters should receive the liberal education that had been planned for them in America. Then I boldly asked, in Hanano’s name and by the power of her father’s request, that I should be allowed the privilege of guiding their studies.
Uncle Otani was astonished at such an unheard-of request, but the situation was unusual and a family council was summoned at once. In the case of a consultation concerning a widow, it is customary for her family to be represented; and Brother being unable to be present, Mother sent in his place my progressive Tokyo uncle﻿—the one who had taken so vigorous a part in our council meetings before my marriage. It was necessary for Hanano, as official head of her family, to be present, but of course she was to speak only through me.
Since she had not yet learned to wear Japanese dress properly, I put on her best white dress, trimmed with lace and ruffles. I arranged everything so that it would be very loose; for it is difficult to sit quietly in Japanese fashion while wearing American clothing, and yet it is inexcusably rude at a ceremonial gathering to move﻿—however slightly﻿—the lower part of the body. I explained this to Hanano, and told her how her grandfather, when two years younger than she, had held the seat of state in the formidable political meetings before the Restoration. “Honourable Grandmother told me he always sat very straight and was dignified,” I said, “and you must be like him.” Then we went in to the meeting.
I could not help being uneasy about the way my bold request might be received. To most of the council I was nothing but a widowed dependant of my daughter﻿—a woman with advanced and peculiar notions﻿—and they had the power, if three voices of the council disapproved of me and my ideas, not only to refuse my request, but to separate me from my children entirely. I should be well provided for, in my present home, if I chose, or elsewhere, but the children would remain with their father’s people; and no law of Heaven or earth was powerful enough in Japan to prevent it. Matsuo’s family had no desire to do any unjust thing; nor did I suspect that they had, but﻿—they held the power.
The conference, which was long, consisted of a series of polite suggestions and earnest, but never excited, arguments.

===Chinese===

我向他说明，松雄在遗嘱里表达过一个心愿：他既没有儿子，几个女儿便当领受那为她们在美国拟定的开明教育。随后我大胆请求——以花野的名义，凭她父亲遗命之力——准许我享有引导她们学业的权柄。
大谷叔父听了这闻所未闻的请求，愕然不已；可这局面本就非常，家族会议当即召集起来。凡议到寡妇的事，照例要她娘家的人到场代言；哥哥脱不开身，母亲便派了我的东京叔父前去——就是婚前家族聚议时那位慷慨陈词的维新派。花野身为一家之主，按理必须在场；当然，她只能由我代言。
她还不会得体地穿日本衣裳，我便给她穿上她那条镶着花边和褶裥的最好看的白裙子。我把一切都收拾得宽宽绰绰；因为穿着美国衣裳，很难端端正正按日本坐姿坐定；而在正式的集会上，身子下半部——哪怕极轻微地——动了一动，也是不可宽恕的失礼。我把这层道理讲给花野听，又告诉她，她外公在她现在小两岁的年纪，便已在明治维新前那些威严肃穆的政治会议上坐镇主位了。「祖母大人告诉我，他总是坐得笔直，端庄得很，」我说，「你也得学他的样。」然后我们便进去了。
我那大胆的请求会落个怎样的下场，我不由得惴惴不安。在多数与会者眼里，我不过是女儿名下寄人篱下的寡妇——一个满脑子先进而古怪念头的女人；倘若会上有三个声音不赞成我和我的主张，他们便有权不但驳回我的请求，还可以把孩子们从我身边彻底分开。我若愿意，尽可以在眼下的家里安身度日，衣食无缺；也可以另置别处；可孩子们要留在他们父亲一族人那里；天上地下，没有任何一条律法，在日本有力量拦得住。松雄的家人并无半点不公道的心思；我也不曾疑心他们有——只是，权柄在他们手里。
会议很长，自始至终是一连串客客气气的进言和恳切——却从不激烈——的辩难。

===Original===

I listened with my head bowed, occasionally﻿—but not too often﻿—glancing toward my little anxious-eyed daughter, sitting erect and motionless in the midst of the dignified row of elders. For two hours she did not move. Then one poor, cramped little leg jerked, her fluffy dress spread out, and with a quick catching at her knee, she gasped, “Oh!”
Not a face turned toward her, but with an anguished clutch in my throat I bowed to the floor, saying, “I humbly pray the honourable council to pardon the rudeness of my foreign-trained child, and permit her to retire with me from the august assembly.”
Uncle Otani, without moving, gave a grunt of assent.
As I made my last bow at the sliding door and slipped it back in place, my Tokyo uncle tapped his pipe carefully against the rim of the tobacco box by his side.
“It is fortunate that O Etsu San seems a reliable woman,” he said slowly; “for surely it would be a puzzling venture for any of us to take into our family two rough American children with their untrained feet, their flouncing garments, and their abrupt speech.”
Whether that remark was intended to be kind or cruel, I never knew; and whether or not it had influence, I never knew; but after another hour of slow, careful, earnest, and perfectly fair discussion, the council decided that on account of Matsuo’s request, combined with the fact that his widow appeared to be a trustworthy person, consent was given to a temporary trial of the experiment.
That night I pulled my cushions in between my children’s beds﻿—close, close﻿—and crept beneath the covers, faint with relief and gratitude.

===Chinese===

我垂着头听着，偶尔——不敢太频——抬眼望一望我那小女儿：她端坐在一排威严的长辈中间，眼里含着焦灼，一动也不动。两个钟头，她纹丝没动。后来，一条可怜的、坐麻了的小腿猛地一颤，她那蓬蓬的裙子散了开来，她一把抱住膝盖，倒抽一口气：「哦！」
没有一张脸朝她转过来；可我喉咙里一阵发紧，俯身拜到地板上，说道：「恳请诸位大人宽恕我这外国教养的孩子失礼，准她随我退出这庄严的会议。」
大谷叔父身也不动，从鼻子里哼出一声应允。
我在拉门前深深行了最后一礼，把门轻轻拉回原位；我的东京叔父拿起烟管，在他手边的烟丝盒沿上仔仔细细磕了磕。
「阿津桑看来是个可靠的人，这倒算我们的运气，」他慢吞吞地说，「要不然，把两个粗野的美国孩子领进家门——那双没调教过的脚，那身乱蓬蓬的衣裳，那套冒冒失失的言语——对我们哪一位，都是一桩难办的差事呢。」
这句话是善意还是刻薄，我始终不知道；它有没有分量，我也始终不知道；可是又过了一个钟头缓慢、审慎、恳切而全然公道的商议之后，家族会议议定：念在松雄的遗命，兼之他的遗孀看上去是个可信托的人，准予试行一时。
那天夜里，我把自己的铺盖拖到孩子们的床中间——紧紧地、紧紧地挨着——钻进被窝，一阵宽慰和感激，几乎叫人发晕。

## XXV Our Tokyo Home / 二十五 我们的东京之家
===Original===

A few weeks later the children and I, with capable little Sudzu in the kitchen, were settled in a pretty home in Tokyo. The arrangement with Matsuo’s family was that some one of the relatives would visit us at intervals to see that everything was satisfactory; and that I was to consult the council about every new, even trifling, problem which might arise.
I was chained﻿—but I was content.
My relatives in Nagaoka were much concerned over my peculiar position; and Mother, because it would be undignified for a young widow to be alone, decided to come and live with us. Not being able, however, to make immediate arrangements, she sent Taki, who was now a widow, and who, because her father and her grandfather had served in our family, had claimed the right to return to Mother and calmly settle herself as a member of the household. When she came to Tokyo she at once assumed the combined responsibilities of chaperon, housekeeper, cook, seamstress, and commander-general of us all﻿—including Sudzu.
In less than three days Taki had discovered the best fish-shop in the neighbourhood; and in less than a week all second-rate vegetable venders and fruit peddlers went trotting by our kitchen door, holding their swinging baskets away from the keen eyes of our countrywoman who knew so well when the first blush of freshness was gone.
From the first I relied entirely upon Taki’s judgment. Nevertheless, I had some annoying experiences, for to her heart I was still little Etsu-bo Sama, although her lips acknowledged that I had reached the dignified position of “Oku Sama”﻿—Honourable Mistress﻿—and although I had acquired some wonderful ideas and possessed two astonishingly active children, who dressed queerly and talked too loud.
My troubles began the very first night. After Taki had closed the outside gates and fastened the front and kitchen doors I heard her sliding the wooden panels which ran along the outer edge of the porch overlooking the garden. These were for protection in stormy weather and to keep us safe at night, but when closed they shut out the air completely.
“Don’t close the *amadoes* tight, Taki,” I called. “Leave a little space between them. We need fresh air for the rooms.”
“*Maa! Maa!*” cried Taki, with profound astonishment in her voice. “You left your home when you had but little learning, Oku Sama.

===Chinese===

几个星期以后，我和孩子们——外加能干的小阿铃（Sudzu）在厨房里——便在东京一处漂亮的宅子里安顿下来了。同松雄家的约定是：亲族中隔些时日便有人来看望一回，查看诸事是否妥帖；而凡有新的问题出现，哪怕再琐碎，我都须禀报家族会议。
我是被锁链拴住了——可我心甘情愿。
长冈的亲族们对我这份特殊的处境很是挂念；母亲觉得，一个年轻的寡妇独居失了体统，便决定来同我们同住。只是一时安排不开，她便先派了多喜来。多喜如今也是寡妇；因为她的父亲和祖父都曾在我家帮佣，她便主张自己有权回到母亲身边，安安稳稳地做了家中的一员。她一到东京，立刻把各项职责一肩挑起：既是陪伴监护的嬷嬷，又是管家、厨娘、裁缝，还是我们全体的总司令——连阿铃也在其内。
不出三天，多喜已寻访出左近最好的鱼铺；不出一个礼拜，那些二等的菜贩果贩，路过我们厨房门口时，都一路小跑，把晃悠的篮子挪得远远的，避开我们这位乡下同胞那双利眼——第一抹新鲜劲儿是什么时候褪的，她一望便知。
从一开始我就全然倚重多喜的决断。话虽如此，我也吃过些气恼的苦头；因为在她心里，我仍旧是小小的津保大人，尽管她嘴上也承认，我已荣升了体面的「夫人」（Oku Sama）——可敬的主母；也尽管我添了许多了不得的新见识，还带着两个精力旺盛得惊人的孩子——穿得古里古怪，说话嗓门太大。
我的麻烦头一晚就来了。多喜关好大门、闩好正门和厨房门之后，我听见她在推沿游廊外沿的那一排木板雨户。那是刮风下雨时护宅、夜里保平安用的；可一关严，空气就全被隔断了。
「雨户（amado）别关死，多喜，」我喊道，「板与板之间留一条缝。屋里要新鲜空气。」
「哎呀呀！哎呀呀！」多喜叫起来，声音里满是深深的骇异，「您离开家门的时候，学问还浅得很呢，夫人。」

===Original===

Air without the smile of the august Sun goddess has poison in it.”
“But, Taki,” I protested, “this is like a foreign house. It has gas for the heaters, and we need outside air, even at night.”
She hesitated, evidently much distressed.
“It may be that air in the honourable foreign house is different,” she muttered, “but it seems peculiar﻿—peculiar. And besides, it is not safe in a great city where burglars live.”
She walked away shaking her head and grumbling to herself. Feeling that I had established my authority, I went to bed, only to be awakened by a stealthy, intermittent rumbling, which presently ended in a muffled snap as Taki pushed in the wooden bolt of the last panel.
“Well,” I said to myself, half provoked, half amused, “Taki always had her own way, even with the jailer of Nagaoka prison. So what could *I* expect!”
Like many Japanese women of the working class, Taki had been obliged to take a large share of the burden of livelihood on her own shoulders. Her husband was a kind man and a good workman, but he drank too much sake, and that meant not only a mysterious slipping away of wages, but frequent imprisonment for debt.
Whenever this happened Taki came to our home, and Mother would give her employment until she had saved enough to set her husband free. One day while she was working for us, my older sister went out with her on an errand. Just beyond the gate they saw two men approaching. One was a well-dressed man, his head covered with the basket mask worn by all prisoners outside the walls. Sister said that Taki stood still, watching the men suspiciously, and did not seem surprised when they stopped.
The officer bowed and said pleasantly: “Only three yen is due now. Pay that and he is free.”
“Oh, please, Mr. Officer,” exclaimed Taki in great distress, “*please* keep him just a few weeks longer. Then I shall have all the debts paid and a little start for the next time. *Please* keep him just a little longer. Please!”
The husband, poor man, stood meekly by while his wife and the officer argued, but Taki stubbornly refused to pay the three yen, and the officer walked away with his basket-headed prisoner. Taki stood looking after them, triumphant. But a few moments later she pulled a fold of paper from her sash and, wiping her eyes, sniffed a few times and said: “Come, little Mistress; we have wasted much time. We must hurry!”

===Chinese===

「见不着尊贵的天照大神笑面的空气，里头是带着毒的。」
「可是，多喜，」我分辩道，「这房子跟外国房子一样。取暖烧的是煤气，夜里也要外面的空气呀。」
她迟疑着，显然十分为难。
「外国的贵宅里，空气兴许是两样的，」她咕哝道，「可这事透着蹊跷——蹊跷。再说了，大城里头有贼，不安全。」
她摇着头、一路自言自语地走开了。我自觉权威已然立起，便上床睡了；谁知半夜里被一阵鬼鬼祟祟、断断续续的隆隆声惊醒——末了「咔哒」一声闷响，是多喜把最后一块雨户的木闩推进了闩槽。
「好嘛，」我半是好气、半是好笑地自忖，「多喜连长冈监狱的狱卒都要由着她的性子来，何况我呢！」
多喜同日本许多劳动妇女一样，生计的重担，大半是不得不自己挑的。她丈夫是个和善的人，也是个好工匠，只是酒喝得太凶；这不仅意味着工钱会莫名其妙地越缩越少，还意味着常常因欠债坐牢。
每逢这样的事发生，多喜便回我们家来，母亲给她活计做，直到她攒够了钱赎出丈夫。有一天她正在我们家帮工，姐姐带着她出门办事。刚出大门，便见两个男人迎面走来。一个衣冠齐整，头上却罩着囚徒出狱门必戴的篓式面罩。姐姐说，多喜站定，疑心地打量着那两个人；他们停下脚步时，她也不显得意外。
那差役鞠了一躬，和和气气地说：「如今只欠三块钱了。交了这笔，他就自由了。」
「哦，差役老爷，行行好，」多喜急得什么似的，喊道，「求您再留他几个礼拜。这样我把债全还清，还能给下一回攒个起头。求您再留他一阵子。求求您！」
那可怜的丈夫，温温顺顺地站在一旁，听凭妻子同差役争执；多喜死活不肯交那三块钱，差役便带着他那篓头囚徒走了。多喜站在那里望着他们的背影，一副得胜的神气。可过了一会儿，她从腰带里抽出一叠纸，抹了抹眼睛，抽了抽鼻子，说：「走吧，小姐；咱们耽搁了好些工夫了。得赶紧呢！」

===Original===

I said nothing more about not closing the *amadoes*, but several days later I had a carpenter put up a wide, openwork strip of carved iris﻿—the flower of health﻿—between the eaves and the top of the panels. At intervals were inserted iron bars run through the hollow tubes of bamboo. Thus we were safe in every way; for not enough poison air could filter through the health-giving blossoms of the carving to injure us, even in the opinion of our good, fanatical Taki.
The children surprised me by the readiness with which they accepted conditions in this strange land. Hanano, from babyhood, had been attracted by new things, and I concluded that our life of constant change had kept her from homesickness. And three-year-old Chiyo﻿—who had always been a contented little thing﻿—seemed so happy in the unbroken companionship of her sister that I did not realize the possibility of her having opinions and desires of her own. While we were visiting she expected strange things, but when we reached a place that I called “home” and she found her clothing arranged in drawers and her playthings put where she could get them, she began to miss many things.
“Mamma,” she said one day, coming up and leaning against my shoulder as I sat sewing, “Chiyo wants﻿—”
“What does Chiyo want?” I asked.
She took my hand and led me slowly through our six tiny rooms. White mats were on all the floors except the kitchen. In the parlour alcove hung a roll picture with a flower arrangement on the polished platform beneath. A small upright piano stood in one corner. Sliding doors of silk separated the parlour from my own and the children’s rooms, side by side, just beyond. In both, standing against the tan-coloured plaster wall, were whitewood chests of drawers with ornamental iron handles. My desk and Hanano’s, both low white tables with books and pen-stands on top, were so placed that, when the paper sliding doors were pushed back, we could see across the narrow porch into our pretty little garden with its well-trimmed shrubbery, its curved path of stepping-stones, and its small lake with nine darting goldfish.
The dining room, at right angles to our rooms, overlooked the garden, too. It was the sunniest room in the house.

===Chinese===

雨户的事我不再提了；可过了几天，我叫来一个木匠，在屋檐与雨户顶端之间，装上一条宽阔的镂空雕花板——雕的是鸢尾，健康之花。每隔一段，还穿进铁条，从竹管的空心里贯通而过。这样一来，我们便万全了；因为哪怕照我们那位善良而热狂的多喜的看法，能从那雕出的祛病花朵间渗进来的毒气，也不足为害了。
孩子们对这陌生国土的种种光景，接受得出奇地痛快。花野自婴儿时起便喜新好奇，我想，是我们那漂泊不定的生活，使她不知乡愁为何物。三岁的千代（Chiyo）——她从来是个知足的小东西——有姐姐寸步不离做伴，显得那样快活，我竟不曾想到，她也会有她自己的主意和渴望。做客的时候，她什么都是新鲜的；可等到了一个我称作「家」的地方，她发现自己的衣裳收进了抽屉，玩物搁在她够得着的地方，她便开始惦记起许多东西来。
「妈妈，」有一天她说着，走过来偎在我肩头，我正坐着做针线，「千代要——」
「千代要什么呀？」我问。
她拉起我的手，领着我慢慢穿过我们那六间小屋。除了厨房，间间地上都铺着白席。客厅的壁龛里挂着一幅立轴，下面光亮的搁板上供着一瓶插花。一角立着一架小小的直立钢琴。丝质的拉门把客厅同我的卧房和孩子们的卧房隔开——两间并排，就在客厅后头。两间屋里，靠着浅褐色的灰泥墙，立着白木的五斗橱，缀着铁艺的饰扣。我的书桌和花野的——都是低低的白木小桌，桌上摆着书和笔架——安放的位置，正好教人把纸拉门推开时，能越过窄窄的游廊，望进我们那座小巧可爱的园子：修剪齐整的灌木，曲折的踏石小径，还有一泓小池，九尾金鱼穿梭来去。
饭厅同我们的屋子成直角，也朝着园子。那是全宅最向阳的一间。

===Original===

The closets were hidden by sliding doors covered with tan-coloured tapestry, and the long, square-cornered firebox with drawers﻿—the invariable adjunct of every dining room in Japan﻿—was a handsome one of white birch. On one side was always a cushion, ready any moment for the mistress when she came to talk over house matters with the maid, called from the kitchen just behind another tan-coloured door which looked a part of the wall. The bathroom, Taki’s and Sudzu’s room, and the servants’ entrance, were just beyond. Our own “shoe-off place” and entrance hall were in front, opening toward the big wooden gates with the “camel’s-eye door” in one of them.
From room to room Chiyo led me, stopping in each and pointing aimlessly here and there. “Chiyo wants﻿—” she repeated, but her wants were so many that she had no words. The emptiness, which I loved, oppressed her. She longed for the big canopy beds of Mother’s home, for the deep-cushioned chairs, the large mirrors, the big square piano, the flowered carpets and the windows curtained with lace, the high ceilings, the wide rooms, the spaciousness! I looked at the wistful little face and my heart smote me. But when she pulled my sleeve and, burying her face in the folds of my dress, said piteously, “Oh, Mamma, take me home to Grandma and Papa’s picture! Please! Please!” I caught her in my arms and, sinking to the floor, hugged her close and, for the first time since I could remember, I sobbed aloud.
But this could not last. Where was my samurai blood? Where my childhood training? Had my years of unrestrained freedom in America weakened my character and taken away my courage? My honourable father would be shamed.
“Come, Little Daughter,” I said, choking and laughing together, “Chiyo has shown Mamma what we have not in our new house; now Mamma will show Chiyo what we have.”
So, gaily we went over the same road. In the parlour I pushed back the low silk doors beneath the moon window, and we saw two deep shelves in which were neatly arranged all of Hanano’s and Chiyo’s pretty books from America. I pointed to the wonderful panel over the doors﻿—a broad, thin slab of wood, strangely delicate and beautiful﻿—carved by unknown years of dashing waves into its odd, inimitable pattern.

===Chinese===

橱柜藏在浅褐色织锦的拉门后头；那口长长的、方角的带抽屉火盆——日本家家饭厅里不可少的一件——是白桦木的体面货。一侧常年放着一个坐垫，好教主母随时过来，同唤来商量家务的女仆说话——女仆从厨房里出来，厨房就藏在一扇浅褐色的门后，那门瞧着同墙是一体的。再过去，是浴室、多喜和阿铃的屋子，还有仆役进出的小门。我们自家的「脱鞋处」和门厅在前头，朝着那两扇大木门，其中一扇上开着「骆驼眼」小门。
千代领着我一间屋一间屋地走，每间都停下来，漫无目的地东指指、西点点。「千代要——」她一遍遍地说；可她想要的东西太多了，多得出不了口。这份我所深爱的空落落，却压得她喘不过气。她想念外婆家那几张带华盖的大床，想念那厚垫的软椅、大穿衣镜、大方形钢琴、织花的地毯、垂着花边帘的窗子、高高的天花板、宽宽的屋子——那份宽敞！我望着那小小的、怅惘的脸，心里一阵刺痛。待到她扯住我的衣袖，把脸埋进我裙褶里，苦苦地哀告：「哦，妈妈，带我回家看外婆和爸爸的照片吧！求求你！求求你！」——我一把将她搜进怀里，跌坐在地板上，紧紧抱着她；打从记事的时候起，我头一回放声哭了出来。
可这不能长久。我的武士血脉哪里去了？我童年的教养哪里去了？难道我在美国那些年不受拘束的自由，磨软了我的性子、夺走了我的胆量？我尊贵的父亲要蒙羞的。
「来吧，小女儿，」我说，又哽又笑，「千代让妈妈看见了新家里没有什么；如今妈妈要让千代看看，咱们有什么。」
于是我们欢欢喜喜地原路再走一遍。在客厅里，我推开月亮窗底下那几扇低低的丝门，只见两格深深的壁架，花野和千代从美国带来的漂亮书，齐齐整整排列在上面。我又指给她看门楣上方那块奇妙的横板——一大幅薄薄的木板，纤细得叫人称奇——是不知多少年的惊涛骇浪，把它雕成了那副无可仿效的奇纹。

===Original===

I showed her the post of the alcove: only the scaled and twisted trunk of a forest pine, yet so polished that it looked as if it were enclosed in crystal. We looked at the rich, dark wood of the alcove floor, “as smooth and shining as Grandma’s mirrors in the big parlour at home,” I told her, and she bent over to see the reflection of a grave little face, changing, as she looked, into one with a twisty smile. In another room I opened the tiny door of our unused shrine. Within the dainty carved interior stood her father’s picture, framed in America, which was to hang over the piano when the carpenter could come to put it up. I showed her the big closets where our bed cushions slept in the daytime, gathering, in their silken flowers, talk, music, and laughter to weave into pleasant dreams for her to find hidden in her pillow at night. I gently opened the wee mountain of ashes in the dining-room firebox so that she could see the softly glowing charcoal, always waiting with warmth and comfort for anyone who wanted a sip of tea. I had her peep into the tiny drawers﻿—one for small rice-cakes of pink and white, in case a child should come to visit, one for extra chopsticks, and one for a tiny can of tea with its broad wooden spoon near by. But the big, broad drawer at the bottom﻿—Oh, dear! Oh, dear!﻿—we didn’t need at all. That was made for some old-fashioned grandmother who sometimes, after she had told a fairy story to her little grandchild, would reach in for a long, slender pipe with a silver thimble for a bowl. After three whiffs she would tap it on the edge of the box﻿—just here﻿—three times, *tap-tap-tap*, and then put it away with its fragrant silken bag (sniff, sniff!﻿—poof, poof! Mamma doesn’t like!) to wait for another time of meditation or loneliness, or perhaps for an hour when another dear old grandmother might chance to call. Then there would be three more whiffs, or perhaps double three, while the two grandmothers sipped their tea and talked in gentle voices of olden time.
“And here is where Sudzu keeps the boats of the food fairies,” I said, “all waiting for their burden of good things to eat.”

===Chinese===

我又指给她看壁龛的柱子：那不过是一截森林古松的躯干，遍体鳞甲、虬曲盘错，却磨得莹润，竟像封在一层水晶里。我们细看壁龛底座那深色的木料，「又滑又亮，跟家里大客厅外婆的镜子一样，」我告诉她；她俯下身去，看一张一本正经的小脸映在里头，看着看着，那张脸扭出了一个歪歪的笑。在另一间屋里，我打开我们尚未启用的神龛的小门。那精致的雕花龛内，立着她父亲的照片，美国的框子；等木匠得空来挂上钢琴上方，就归在那里。我又指给她看几口大衣橱——我们白日里，铺盖便睡在里头——在那些绸缎的花朵里，收拢着闲谈、音乐和笑声，好织成香甜的梦，夜里藏进她的枕头，等她去发现。我轻轻扒开饭厅火盆里那一小座灰山，好教她看见那幽幽烧红的炭火——永远备着温暖与安慰，伺候任何一个想呷口茶的人。我叫她偷看那几只小抽屉——一只盛着粉白两色的小米饼，防着有小孩来串门；一只盛着备用的筷子；还有一只，盛着一小罐茶叶，旁边搁着一柄宽宽的木匙。可底下那只又宽又大的抽屉——哎呀，哎呀！——我们全用不着。那是给旧式的祖母们预备的：那种祖母，有时给小孙儿讲完了神仙故事，会伸手进去，摸出一支细长的烟管，烟锅是只银顶针。吸上三口，她便在匣沿上磕一磕烟管——就在这儿——磕三下，嗒、嗒、嗒，然后连着那只香喷喷的绸烟袋，一并收起（嗅嗅，嗅嗅！——啸、啸！妈妈不喜欢！），等下一次静坐独处的时候，或者哪一位亲爱的老祖母恰好来访的时辰。那时便又是三口，兴许是双份的三口，两位祖母一面呷着茶，一面拿温软的嗓音，说着古时候的事。
「这儿是阿铃给食物精灵们泊船的地方，」我说，「一只只都在这儿等着装载好吃食呢。」

===Original===

I pushed back one of the panels which didn’t look at all like a door, and we peeped into a closet of many shallow shelves, on which, in piles of five, were wooden bowls for soup, china bowls for rice, oval plates for fish, deep ones for pickles, and many plates and cups and dishes, each shaped for a special purpose and each decoration telling a story of old Japan. Below were our lacquer tables, each a foot square and a foot high; and piled up, a little distance away, were our cushions,

> “Just One and Two and Three,

For She and Her and Me!”

===Chinese===

我推开一扇全然不像门的板门，我们朝里张望：一间满是浅浅格架的小间，架上码着一摞摞五只一摞的木碗——喝汤的；瓷碗——盛饭的；椭圆的盘子——装鱼的；深碟——腌菜的；还有许多盘、杯、盏，各有各的用场，各色花纹各讲着一段古日本的故事。下面是我们的漆几，一张张见方一尺、高一尺；略略隔开码在一旁的，是我们的坐垫——
「一只，两只，三只，
给她，给她，还有我！」

===Original===

as Hanano sang when Sudzu brought them out for meals.
“And now the kitchen,” I went on. “This door doesn’t slide, but opens by turning a little bronze pine-cone. Step into these sandals, Chiyo; for no one goes into the kitchen with only foot mittens on﻿—or stockings. Here we are! One half the floor is of smooth, dark boards, you see, and the other half﻿—step down!﻿—of cement. There is the gas range, and close beside it a pottery firebox for the big swelling rice-kettle with its heavy wooden top. No bit of wastepaper or scrap of any kind must be thrown on *that* fire; only straw to start it and charcoal to continue it, for it is used just to cook rice﻿—the staff of life for Japan﻿—and we must treat it with respect. Here comes Taki; and now she will show us something, little Chiyo, that will make you want to run to the big box that smells all camphory, like the forest near Uncle Otani’s house, and get out the fur collar that Grandma gave you last Christmas Day. See!”
Taki stuck two fingers in two little holes in one of the narrow boards of the floor and lifted it; then other, and another. Next, up came a light, broad square of whitewood, and there, within easy reach of Taki’s hand, was a small cellar where was a block of ice, roughly cut in shelves, on which were set wooden plates of fish and vegetables, eggs and fruit.
“That is what becomes of the cold, cold bundle the man brings every morning in the straw saddle on his back,” I said. “And there is Taki’s wooden sink, standing high up from the cement part of the floor, just like a table with legs made of water-pipes.
“Now, turn to the right. Down the narrow little hall we go﻿—five steps of mine and eight of yours﻿—and here we are in the bathroom. The oval whitewood tub, with its two faucets above and little row of gaslights below, is so deep that even Mamma can kneel with the water up to her chin. Here are the three little shelves for our bran bag, cup, and toothbrush, each with a carved towel-hanger below; and over in the corner is a big bamboo basket for laundry and a coil of hose to water the garden. Oh, it’s a very interesting little house, Chiyo; just like a big playhouse, with Mamma at home all the time to play with you when Hanano has gone to school.”

===Chinese===

花野就是这样唱的——每当阿铃把坐垫抱出来开饭的时候。
「现在看厨房去，」我接着说，「这扇门不是拉开的，是拧这颗小铜松果开的。跨进这双草履来，千代；进厨房谁也不兴只穿布袜——更别说光着脚。到啦！你瞧，这半边地板是光滑的深色木板，那半边——跨下来！——是水泥。那是煤气灶，紧挨着一座陶火盆，架着那口鼓腹的大家伙——饭锅，配着厚厚的木盖。那张火上，一点废纸、一片碎屑也不许扔；引火只用稻草，续火只用木炭；因为它专司煮饭——日本人的命根子——我们须得敬着它。多喜来了；这会儿她要给咱们变个东西，小千代，保管叫你想奔到那只樟脑味儿的大箱子跟前去——像大谷叔父家旁边那座树林子一样香——把去年圣诞节外婆送你的毛皮领子翻出来。瞧！」
多喜把两根手指插进地板一块窄板上的两个小孔里，把它提了起来；然后又一块，又一块。接着掀上来一方轻巧的白木板；就在多喜伸手可及的地方，是一个小小的地窖，窖里卧着一块冰，冰上削出一层层搁板，搁着一盘盘的鱼、菜、蛋和水果。
「每天早上那人背着草鞍子驮来的、冰凉冰凉的一包，就是归到这儿来的，」我说。「那是多喜的木水槽，高高地立在水泥地上，活像一张桌子，桌腿是几根水管。
「现在，向右转。顺着这条窄窄的小走廊走下去——妈妈的五步，你的八步——我们到浴室了。这口白木的椭圆浴桶，上头两个龙头，底下一排小小的煤气灯，深得连妈妈跪下去，水都能没到下巴。这儿是三只小搁板，放我们的糠袋、杯子和牙刷，每块板底下刻着一只挂毛巾的小钩；角落里是一只盛脏衣裳的大竹筐，还有一盘浇园子的水龙带。哦，这是一座顶有意思的小房子，千代；就像一座大玩具屋，花野上学去的时候，妈妈整天在家陪你玩。」

## XXVI Tragic Trifles / 二十六 凄伤的琐事
===Original===

My finding a suitable school for Chiyo almost at once was a piece of good fortune. Not far from our house there lived a gifted educator who was interested in modern methods of teaching young children. He and his wife had in their home a small model kindergarten, to which I was given the privilege of sending my little girl. Chiyo could not speak Japanese, but fortunately there were in the class two children of an American missionary, who spoke the language well, so the little Japanese-born Americans became kind interpreters to the little American-born Japanese; thus forming an international combination that resulted, on one side at least, in a lifetime remembrance of grateful friendliness.
But Hanano’s education was a problem. In selecting a school for her the remembrance of my own happy school life in Tokyo naturally influenced me in favour of a mission school; but, after careful examination, I concluded that, although the atmosphere of the mission schools was unquestionably superior, they could not compete with the government schools in scholarship. Therefore I decided upon a public school, the principal of which was reputed to be one of the best in Tokyo, and which, fortunately, was not far from our home. Of this I knew Matsuo’s relatives would approve.
Hanano’s knowledge of the Japanese language was meagre, but of the history, literature, and traditions of Japan, she knew almost as much as other children her age, and so was too advanced for the primary class.
It was a puzzle for the authorities to know what to do with her; for rules in Japan are not flexible. Official life still moves in grooves, and in a minor officer, the old feudal pride in rigid faithfulness is frequently so extreme that to be jostled out of the established line is hopelessly disconcerting. Time and again I heard with a sinking heart that no place could be found for Hanano in any class, but﻿—I did not *dare* fail! Patiently I persisted, arguing that, since Japan claims her foreign-born children, and also has the rule of compulsory education, surely something could be done.
Well, I had a world of trouble and felt as if the child were being wound closer each day in a red-tape cocoon, but at last she was admitted into the third grade and I given permission to sit in the rear of the room, a silent spectator with a notebook.
I shall never forget those first days.

===Chinese===

我几乎一下子就给千代找到了合适的学校，实在是一桩运气。离我们家不远住着一位有才具的教育家，热心于幼童教育的新法。他夫妇俩在自己家里办了一所小小的模范幼稚园，我有幸获准把我家小姑娘送去。千代不会说日语，所幸班里有两个美国传教士的孩子，日语说得好；于是那两个生于日本的小美国人，便做了这位生于美国的小日本人的好心舌人。这样一个国际组合，至少在一方面，结下了一生感念的友情。
可花野的教育却是个难题。替她选学校时，我自己在东京那段快活的学堂生活，自然使我心向教会学校；可细细考察之后，我断定教会学校的风气固然无可争辩地优胜，功课上却赛不过官立学校。于是我选定了一所公立学校——校长号称东京最好的校长之一，而学校幸好离我家不远。我知道，松雄的亲眷们对这个选择也会赞成。
花野的日本话懂得不多；但日本的历史、文学、传统，她知道的几乎不输同龄的孩子，插初年级又太深了。
这可把主事的官们难住了；日本的规矩是不通融的。官场至今仍在旧辙里运行；小官吏身上那种封建传下来的、以死板恪守为荣的骄傲，往往极端到一旦被人请出既定成例，便惶惶然没了主意。
我一次又一次心口发沉地听说，哪个班都安不下花野；可是——我不敢失败！
唉，我吃了无穷的苦头，只觉得孩子一天一天被文牍的茧丝越裹越紧；可她终于获准入了三年级，我也得了许可，坐在教室后头，做一个带笔记本的哑巴旁听生。
最初那些日子，我一辈子忘不了。

===Original===

Hanano was naturally quick and observing and already familiar with third-grade stories; but the ideographs were wholly unknown to her, and she could understand very little of the teacher’s explanations. Again and again I would see her face light up with an expression of alert attention, which the next moment would change to a puzzled look and then gradually settle into one of blank hopelessness. Every evening our home was turned into a schoolroom, where I went over each lesson of the day, translating and explaining in English. At odd hours, even during mealtime, we played games in which words were limited to those in common use, and whenever Hanano heard Taki bargaining with vendors at the kitchen door, she was immediately at her elbow. But I think her greatest help from any one thing came from the playground at school. There she was a delightful curiosity.
