She took part in all games, running, gesticulating, and chattering in English, while the others ran, gesticulated, and chattered in Japanese, all having a good time, and Hanano piling up, by the dozen, unforgettable words which carried their own definitions too clearly to need interpretation.
I was faithful in my reports to Uncle Otani, and, on the whole, rather enjoyed the “investigation visits” of the relatives; but my being required to ask council advice before making a change, however slight, in my programme, was often very trying and useless. Formally to request an opinion regarding which of two studies to select for Hanano, when not a member of the council knew or cared to learn anything of her former school work, and every single member considered both of the suggested studies entirely unnecessary for a girl to waste her time over, was absurd. But I was conscientious to the minutest degree, and as time passed the visits from relatives became less frequent and more friendly; and my requests were mostly returned with orders to use my own judgment.
When Hanano reached the stage where she began to recognize characters on the street signs and to listen intelligently to conversation going on about her, I gave up my visits to the school and turned my attention to home duties. Here I found many problems. Some were seemingly too small to be noticed, and yet, like stinging gnat bites, extremely annoying. I had thought it would be well to keep the children in American clothing.

===Chinese===

花野天性敏捷，善观善察，三年级的课文故事原已烂熟；可那些方块字她全然不识，先生的讲解她十句里懂不上一句。
我一次次望见她的脸境地亮起来，一副警醒注意的神情；转眼又换成一脸困惑，末了渐渐凝成一片茫然绝望。
每天晚上，我们的家就变成教室，我把当天的功课一门门过一遍，用英语翻译讲解。
零碎工夫，连吃饭的时候，我们也做限用常用字的游戏；花野一听见多喜在厨房门口同小贩讲价，立刻就贴到她胳膊肘边去。
不过我想，若论哪一样给她的助力最大，还得数学校的操场。
在那里，她是个叫人喜爱的稀罕物。什么游戏她都入伙：她跑着、比划着、叽里咕啦说英语，别的人跑着、比划着、说日语，个个玩得痛快；花野则成打成打地攒下忘不掉的字眼——那些字眼自带注解，明白得用不着翻译。
我按时不误地给大谷叔父写信报告；亲戚们那些「考察来访」，我大体还觉得有趣。可有一桩常常叫人难堪又无用：我的日程无论怎样变动一下——哪怕最轻微的——都须先请示亲族会议。
为花野在两门功课里选哪一门而一本正经地请求裁示——而会议里没有一位知道、也没有一位愿意打听她从前的功课，每一位又都觉得提请斟酌的那两门，压根儿是女孩子白费工夫的东西——这实在荒唐。
可我一丝不苟，事事周到；日子久了，亲戚们的来访渐渐稀了，也渐渐亲热了；我的请示，多半批回来一句：请自酌量。
等到花野能认街牌上的字、能听懂左近人们的谈话了，我便不再去学校，掉转心思料理家事。
家事里问题也多得很。有些看似小得值不得理会，却像蜾虫叮咬，烦人得很。我原想让孩子继续穿美国衣裳。

===Original===

They had a goodly supply, and progressive families were beginning to advocate it for children, except for formal use. As the weather grew colder I put heavy underclothes and woollen stockings on them; for the schoolrooms were heated only with two charcoal fireboxes in each large room. But, notwithstanding my care, one day Chiyo came home with a cold. The next morning was chilly and damp. I had no heart to keep her from her greatest enjoyment; yet to risk her taking more cold was out of the question. What could I do? Suddenly I had a wicked inspiration. She had a coat of soft woollen goods which covered her dress completely. I put it on, buttoned it up close and, telling her not to take it off, sent her on her way.
Then I sat down to have it out with my conscience. In Japan, when one enters a house, the shoes, wrap, and hat are removed. It was as unpardonable for Chiyo to keep on her wrap in school as if it had been her hat; but I knew that, in the eyes of the teacher, the pretty red coat with its lace collar and cuffs would be only a foreign dress, no more suggesting a wrap than did her usual clothing. And to think that I had taken advantage of the ignorance of the teacher and done this deceitful thing! I thought of Kishibo-jin and wondered if in every mother’s heart is hidden an unborn demon.
Presently, with a sigh, I rose to my feet and prepared to go out. As I approached the mirror to arrange my hair I stopped with a half-ashamed laugh. For one instant a superstitious hesitation had held me back, as if I might see in the reflected face a hint of the deceit in my heart.
I went direct to the nearest shop and purchased material for a *hifu*﻿—a loose but proper and elegant house-garment, which in winter is padded with the cobwebby cut silk taken from empty cocoons. It is the lightest and warmest garment in Japan. Taki, Sudzu, and I sewed all day, and the next morning Chiyo went happily to school with her *hifu* over her American dress.
It was this incident that decided me to change the children from American to Japanese clothing.
There is another link, less tragic, in the chain of my memories of growing adaptability. When riding in jinrikishas it is the custom for the honoured person to go first. Therefore a child should follow a parent.

===Chinese===

她们的美国衣裳存了不少；新派人家也开始主张孩子穿洋装——只是正式场合除外。
天一冷，我给她们加了厚内衣和毛线袜；因为学校的教室，每间大屋只靠两只炭火盆取暖。
可百般小心，千代还是有一天带着伤风回家了。
第二天早晨又阴又潮。我狠不下心不让她去享受她最大的乐子；可再让她受凉，万万使不得。怎么办呢？忽然间我起了一个坏主意。
她有一件软呢大衣，能把她的小裙儿整个罩住。我给她穿上，扣得严严实实，嘱咐她不许脱，就送她上路了。
随后我坐下来，同我的良心算这笔账。
在日本，进屋要脱鞋、脱外衣、脱帽。千代在学校里穿着外套不脱，同戴着帽子一样不可饶恕；可我知道，在先生眼里，那件带蕾丝领口袖口的漂亮红衣裳，只算一件外国衣裳，同她平日的穿着一样，未必让人想到是件外套。
而我竟利用先生的无知，干下这等欺瞒的事！我想起鬼子母神，心想是不是每个母亲的心里，都藏着一个未曾出世的鬼。
过了一会，我叹口气站起身，预备出门。走近镜子拢头发时，我半是羞惭地笑了一笑，停住了。刚才有一瞬，一种迷信的踌躇拽住了我，仿佛怕从镜中那张脸上，照出我心里那点欺瞒的影子。
我径直去了最近的一家铺子，买了一件袷衣（hifu）的料子——那是一种宽松而端正雅致的居家衣裳，冬天絮上空茧里抽出的蛛网似的丝绵。那是日本最轻最暖的衣裳。
多喜、阿铃（Sudzu）和我缝了一整天；第二天早晨，千代便把袷衣罩在美国裙子上，欢欢喜喜上学去了。
正是这桩事，叫我拿定主意，把孩子们从美国衣裳换作日本衣裳。
在我这「渐渐入乡随俗」的记忆链上，还有一环，没有那么凄伤。坐人力车（jinrikisha）时，规矩是尊长先行；所以孩子应当跟在父母后头。

===Original===

But I never felt sure that some unexpected thing might not happen to my active little ones; so I always put them together in one jinrikisha just ahead of me. One day, as we were passing through a busy street, I saw Hanano looking back and waving frantically; almost standing up in her eagerness to have me see a small table and two chairs of bamboo in a shop window. Both children pleaded for me to buy them. It was nonsense to take them to our pretty home; for chair legs ruin the soft mats, and foreign furniture is wholly inartistic in a Japanese room. But the children looked at them so longingly that I made the purchase, ordering thin strips of wood to be fastened on the feet to make a flat foundation that would not injure our floor. They were to be delivered the next day.
Early the following morning I went shopping, returning home about noon. What was my astonishment when I entered the house to see the bamboo table in the centre of the parlour and on each side of it a chair, with Hanano seated on one and Chiyo on the other! They had no books, no toys. Sudzu said they had been there for an hour, occasionally changing places, but otherwise sitting still or talking in low voices.
“What are you doing, children,” I asked, “sitting here so quiet?”
“Oh, just enjoying!” replied Hanano.
After a moment Chiyo said: “Grandma’s chairs are soft, but this one has knobs on the edge. Let’s swap again, Hanano.”
Then there was the affair of the bedclothes. The pride of a Japanese housewife is to have not only dainty and pretty, but also appropriate, bed cushions. Mother had sent with Taki enough silk and linen for both children’s beds. The pattern for Hanano’s was, for her flower-name, the “Flowers of the Four Seasons,” in which bunches of many-coloured blossoms were scattered loosely over a background of shadowy pink. Chiyo’s﻿—for her name, which means “Long Life”﻿—was a flock of white storks flying across a blue sky with floating clouds. Taki and Sudzu had sewed steadily for several days making the cushions, so, on the night they were finished, and when Sudzu had made up the beds side by side, I told the girls that I would put the children to bed and they could go out to a street fair held on the temple grounds, not far away. In the midst of the undressing some friends came to call and I left the children to finish alone.
My friends stayed late.

===Chinese===

可我这两个活泼的小家伙，什么意外都保不定出；所以我总叫她们同乘一辆人力车，走在我的前头。
有一天，我们正穿过一条热闹的街，我看见花野回头拼命挥手，急得差不多站起来——只为让我看一家铺子橱窗里一张小竹桌和两把小竹椅。
两个孩子都求我买下来。把它们搬进我们那个雅致的家里，实在是胡闹：椅子腿要毁坏软软的席子，外国家具摆在日本屋子里，也全然不成体统。
可孩子们那样眼巴巴地望着，我还是买了，又吩咐在椅脚上钉薄木条，垫出平底的座子，免得伤了我们的地板。
说好第二天送到。
第二天一早我出门买东西，晌午前后才到家。一进屋，我惊得呆了：竹桌端端正正摆在客厅当中，一边一把椅子，花野坐一把，千代坐一把！
她们手边没有书，没有玩具。阿铃说，她们已经这样坐了一个钟头，偶尔换换位子，其余时候就静静坐着，或低低地说话。
「你们在干什么呀，孩子们，」我问，「坐得这样安静？」
「哦，就是享受享受！」花野答。
过了一会，千代说：「奶奶家的椅子是软的，这把边上砀人。咱们再换一换吧，花野。」
再有是铺盖的事。日本主妇的骄傲，不但在于铺盖的精致漂亮，还要各得其宜。
我的母亲随多喜捎来足够的丝绸麻布，做两个孩子的床褥。
花野那床，配她「花」的名字，绣的是「四季花草」：一小束一小束五彩的花，疏疏落落撒在暗粉红的底子上。
千代那床——她的名字意思是「长寿」——是一群白鹤，飞过浮着云的蓝天。
多喜和阿铃连缝了好几天才做成。完工那晚，阿铃把两张床并排铺好，我便对她们说，孩子由我来安顿，她们可以出门去逛庙会——就在不远的寺院场院上。
正脱衣裳的当口，来了几位客人，我便丢下孩子，让她们自己脱完睡下。
客人们坐到很晚。

===Original===

I heard Taki and Sudzu come in, and a short time later there was a disturbance in the children’s room. Hanano’s voice sounded clear and loud in English, “It isn’t fair! Stop! It isn’t fair!” Then came a low murmuring in Japanese﻿—sleepy complaints﻿—a soft scrambling﻿—a gentle, “Pardon my disturbing you. Honourable good night!”﻿—a sliding door, whisperings, and presently﻿—silence.
As soon as the guests had gone I hurried into the children’s room. Both were sleeping quietly. I waited for Sudzu to come in after locking the gate, and then I learned what had happened. Faithful Taki, on her return, had peeped into the children’s room to see that all was safe, and behold! the “Flower in a Strange Land” was asleep beneath the flying storks and the long-life lassie was peacefully reposing beneath the scattered blossoms of the four seasons. Taki’s orderly habits of a lifetime had sprung to the rescue of an upset world. Pulling off the covers with a jerk, she had lifted Hanano in her strong arms, and then, standing the startled child upright, had caught Chiyo and plumped her into Hanano’s bed, muttering constantly, “Ignorant children! Ignorant children!” Paying no attention to Hanano’s indignant protests that they had changed purposely, “just to swap,” she had tossed her back into bed, whirled up the covers, and then, politely bowing good night, had softly pushed the doors together and retired as gently as if she feared to awake a sleeping child.
“Taki is just like she used to be,” I thought as I lay down on my own bed with a laugh. “People who think Japanese women are always gentle ought to widen their acquaintance.”
But one thing about which I have never laughed was a peep I had into a hidden part of my children’s lives. Hanano always had been brave about bearing silently little troubles that could not be helped, and she seemed so busy and interested in her new life that I did not realize that deep in her heart was a longing for the old home. Our garden had two entrances, one through the house and one through a little brushwood wicket on the path that led from a wooden gate to the kitchen door. One day, just as I reached home, a sudden shower threatened to drench me. So, instead of going around to the big gateway, I slipped through the wooden gate, and ran across the stones of the garden to the porch.

===Chinese===

我听见多喜和阿铃回来了；不多时，孩子屋里起了一阵骚动。
花野的声音用英语又清又亮地响起来：「不公平！住手！不公平！」
接着是一阵低低的日语咕哝——睡意朦胧的抗议——一阵轻微的忙乱——一声温柔的「恕我搅扰。恭祝晚安！」——一扇拉门，几句耳语，随后——安静了。
客人一走，我赶紧进孩子屋去。两个都睡得香。我等阿铃锁了大门进来，才问出事情的原委。
忠心的多喜回来后，照例到孩子屋里张一张，看一切妥帖；你瞧！「异乡之花」睡在飞鹤底下，那长寿的小姑娘却安安稳稳躺在四季花草上头。
多喜一辈子规矩整洁的积习，立时跳出来匡正这个乱了套的世界。她一把掠开被子，用有力的胳膊抱起花野，把那吓憬了的孩子立在地下，又一把抱过千代，扑通放进花野的床里，嘴里一直咕哝着：「不懂事的孩子！不懂事的孩子！」
花野气呼呼地分辩，说她们是故意换的，「就是换着玩儿」，她理也不理，把她丢回床上，呼噜卷起被子；然后彬彬有礼地鞠躬道了晚安，轻轻拢上拉门，退出去时那份轻手轻脚，倒像怕惊醒一个睡熟的孩子。
「多喜还是老样子，」我躺到自己床上，笑着想。「以为日本女人永远温顺的人，真该多结交几个。」
可有一件事，我从来笑不出来——那是我对孩子心底隐秘的一瞥。
花野一向勇敢，没法子的小小委屈，她总默默受着；她又那样忙着、那样起劲地过她的新日子，我竟不曾觉察，她心底深处一直恋着旧日的家。
我们的园子有两个入口：一个从屋里进；另一个要穿过一扇小小的柴门，在从木门通往厨房的那条小径上。
有一天，我刚到家，一阵骤雨眼看要把我浇透。我便没有绕正门，径从那扇木门溜进去，踩着园里的踏石，一路跑到游廊跟前。

===Original===

Leaving my shoes on the step I was hurrying to my room when I heard the voices of the children.
“This shady place,” said Hanano, “is where Grandma’s chair always was, on the porch. And under this tree is where the hammock was where you took your nap and where Papa almost sat down on you that time. And this is the big stone steps where we always had firecrackers on Fourth of July. And this is the well. And this is the drawbridge. And this is the place where Clara went to feed the chickens. It’s all exactly right, Chiyo, for I drew it myself, and you must not forget again. Don’t tell Mamma, for she would be sorry, and she is our only treasure that we have left. All the rest are gone, Chiyo, and we can never have them again. So it can’t be helped, and we just have to stand it. But you mustn’t forget that all this﻿—forever﻿—is where our love is. And now, let us sing.”
They stood up, holding hands, and the childish voices rose in a clear, steady “My Country, ’tis of Thee!”
I cried softly as I moved about in the next room and thought of the transplanted morning glories. “Is it right,” I wondered, “to plant a little unasked flower in a garden of love and happiness, from which it must soon be wrenched away, only for another, and a dwarfed, start in strange, new surroundings? The garden had much to give of strength and inspiration, but is it worth the cost? Oh, is it worth the cost?”

===Chinese===

鞋脱在台阶上，我正急着回房，忽然听见孩子们说话的声音。
「这块荫凉地方，」花野说，「就是奶奶的椅子老待的地方，在游廊上。这棵树底下，是吊床——你睡午觉的地方，还有一回爸爸差点一屁股坐上你。这大石阶，是我们年年七月四日放爆竹的地方。这是井。这是吊桥。这是克拉拉去喂鸡的地方。全都一点不差，千代，因为是我亲手画的；你可不许再忘了。别告诉妈妈，她会难过的；她是我们剩下唯一的宝贝了。其余的都没有了，千代，我们再也要不回来了。没法子的事，我们只好受着。可你千万别忘了：这一切——永远——是我们的爱所在的地方。现在，我们来唱歌吧。」
她们站起来，手拉着手，清亮沉着的声音唱起了《我的祖国，这就是你》。
我在隔壁屋里轻轻走动，眼泪悄悄地落；我想起那些移栽的牵牛花。
「对不对呢，」我暗想，「把一株不请自来的小花，种进一座爱与幸福的园子，让她吸足了爱与幸福，却又要早早连根拔起，只为在陌生的天地里再发一回芽——一回受了挫的芽？那园子原有许多力量和奋励可以给她；可这代价付得值么？啊，这代价付得值么？」

## XXVII Honourable Grandmother / 二十七 祖母大人
===Original===

> “Honourable Grandmother is coming﻿—coming!

Honourable Grandmother is coming today!”

===Chinese===

「祖母大人要来啦——来啦！
祖母大人今天就要到啦！」

===Original===

happily sang Chiyo as her little foot mittens came pattering over the white mats, following me as I went through the rooms giving touches here and there to complete arrangements for our expected guest.
Foot mittens took the place of stockings now, and the free American dress had given way to a gay-flowered kimono with scarlet lining and graceful swinging sleeves.
“Japanese fashions are the prettiest for Japanese people,” I thought as I looked at Chiyo’s black hair, short in the back and cut square across the forehead. She had not been a pretty child in American dress. Japanese clothes were much more becoming, but oh, the opportunities for comfortable and healthful bodies the untrammelled children of America have! I sighed, yet I was so bound by outside influences that I could not regret having changed the children into Japanese dress before their grandmother saw them.
We had been very busy after the arrival of Mother’s letter saying that she was ready to come. The children and I moved in together, and I arranged a cosy little room for her, which I knew she would find more convenient and comfortable than any other in the house. I wanted everything to look homelike to her; so I had the swinging electric lights changed to three-foot-high floor lamps shaded by black lacquer frames with paper panels, like the candle-stands at the Nagaoka home. Our gas heaters were already in bronze braziers so ingeniously set up that they looked like charcoal burners. Mother would have accepted everything new with the smiling philosophy of a lifetime, but I did not want her to “accept” things; I wanted everything to look homelike so she could fit in happily without effort.
The empty shrine I had been using for books and the children’s hats. Even Taki had not objected to “high objects,” as she called them, being placed there; for Japanese people are taught to respect books as “intellectual results,” and hats as pertaining to the revered “crown of the body.” But, nevertheless, she was unreservedly pleased when I removed the things and began to prepare the carved wooden alcove for the small belongings that Mother would bring with her from the large shrine at home.
“Where shall we put the shrine that Honourable Grandmother will bring?” asked Hanano, thinking of the elaborate gilded and lacquered cabinet in Uncle Otani’s home.

===Chinese===

千代这样欢欢喜喜地唱着，一双小布袜踏着白席，啪嗒啪嗒地跟在我身后；我在各间屋里穿来穿去，东摸一把、西理一下，把我们恭候的贵客要用的东西一一安顿妥帖。
如今布袜代替了长袜，那一身自由自在的美国衣裳，也换成了印花鲜亮的和服——猩红的衬里，飘飘洒洒的大袖。
「日本人穿日本衣裳，才是顶好看的，」我望着千代的黑头发想——脑后剪短，额前齐齐的刘海。穿美国衣裳的时候，她算不得个好看的孩子。日本衣裳衬她得多；可是，唉，美国那些不受拘束的孩子们，享着怎样的舒展和健旺啊！我叹了口气；可是外头的势力把我束缚得那样紧，孩子们的外婆见到她们之前，我并不后悔把她们换上了日本衣裳。
母亲的信说到她动身的日子以后，我们就忙个不停。孩子们搬来同我一起住，我腾出一间舒舒服服的小屋给她——我知道，那是全宅里她住着顶顺手、顶熨帖的一间。我要样样东西在她眼里都像家。所以我把摇来晃去的电灯换成了三尺来高的落地灯——黑漆架子，糊着纸格，像长冈老家的烛台。我们的煤气取暖器，本就装在铜火盆里，安得那样巧妙，瞧着同炭火盆一模一样。母亲呢，凭她一辈子的豁达，什么新东西她都会笑眯眯地「领受」；可我不要她「领受」；我要样样东西都像家，好教她毫不费力、欢欢喜喜地安顿下来。
那座空着的神龛，我一直拿来搁书和孩子们的帽子。连多喜也不曾反对——她管那些叫「高位之物」——因为日本人从小受教，要敬书本，那是「智慧的成果」；也要敬帽子，那属于可敬的「身体之首」。话虽如此，等我撤去那些东西，开始拾掇那方雕花的木龛，好安放母亲将从老家大龛里带来的那几件随身法宝时，她也露出了毫无保留的欢喜。
「祖母大人要带来的神龛，摆在哪儿呢？」花野问；她心里想着的是大谷叔父家那座金漆辉煌的大家伙。

===Original===

“It’s as easy for Honourable Grandmother to wrap up all the really necessary things for her shrine as it would be for a Christian to carry a Bible and a prayer book,” I answered; “and we will have this little alcove all fresh and clean for them. Honourable Grandmother loves the things that have been sacred to her through all the sorrows and joys of her life.”
“Do Honourable Grandmother’s God and our God know each other up in heaven?” asked Chiyo.
I was leaning in the alcove to brush a bit of dust off the carving, and Hanano replied.
“Of course they do, Chiyo,” she said. “Jesus had just as hard a time as the August Buddha did to teach people that God wants them to be good and kind and splendid. Mamma always says that Honourable Grandmother and our dear American Grandma are good, just alike.”
While we were talking, there had been sounding a constant *pata-pata-pata* from the next room, where Sudzu, with her sleeves looped back and a blue-and-white towel folded over her freshly dressed hair, was vigorously cleaning the paper doors with a shoji duster﻿—a bunch of cut papers tied on the end of a short stick. The sound stopped abruptly and Sudzu appeared in the doorway.
Quickly removing the towel and pulling off the cord that held back her sleeves, she bowed to the floor.
“Taki San thinks that the bath water heated by gas will be too harsh for the delicate body of Honourable Retired Mistress,” she said. “Shall I go for a carpenter?”
I had forgotten the belief of country people that only charred wood must be used for bath fuel when one is frail or old. I hurried Sudzu out on her errand, and within two hours the gas coil had been exchanged for a small charcoal furnace, and our arrangements were complete.
That evening was a memorable one for the children. We all went to the station to meet Mother, except Taki. She remained behind so that the welcoming red rice and the fish, baked head and all, would be in hot readiness; and after we reached home, even before the bustle of welcome was over, she had the shrine belongings in place and the candles lighted. Then, with the gilded doors wide open and the pungent odour of incense filling the air, she brought in the little shrine table laden with food.

===Chinese===

「祖母大人要把神龛里真正要紧的东西都包起来随身带，就像基督徒揣一部《圣经》、一本祷告书那样容易，」我答道，「咱们把这小龛收拾得干干净净、清清爽爽地迎它们。祖母大人疼那些东西——她一辈子的悲欢里，它们件件都是神圣的。」
「祖母大人的上帝，跟我们的上帝，在天上认得彼此吗？」千代问。
我正探身在龛里掸雕花上的一点灰，花野替我答了。
「当然认得，千代，」她说，「耶稣当初教人学好、行善、做个堂堂正正的人，吃的苦头同尊贵的佛陀一样多。妈妈不是常说，祖母大人同我们亲爱的美国外婆一样好、一样一样的吗。」
我们说话的当儿，隔壁屋里一直响着啪嗒、啪嗒、啪嗒的声音：阿铃挽起袖子，新梳好的头发上搭着一条蓝白毛巾，正拿障子掸使劲掸纸门——那是一束剪碎的纸，扎在一根短棍头上。声音攸然而止，阿铃出现在门口。
她飞快地扯下毛巾，解下束袖的带子，伏地一拜。
「多喜姐说，煤气烧的洗澡水，性子太烈，怕伤了隐居的老夫人娇贵的身子，」她说，「要不要我去唤木匠来？」
我竟忘了乡人的讲究：上了年纪或身子娇弱的人，洗澡水须用烧焦的木柴来烧。我赶忙打发阿铃去办；不出两个钟头，煤气盘换成了小小的炭炉，诸事齐备了。
那天傍晚，对孩子们来说是个难忘的傍晚。除多喜以外，我们全体到车站接母亲去了。多喜留在家里，好教迎客的赤饭和连头带尾烤好的鱼热腾腾地候着；我们到家以后，欢迎的忙乱还没有消停，她已把神龛法物安放停当，蜡烛也点上了。然后，她敞开龛门，满屋看香扑鼻，端进来一张小小的供桌，供满了吃食。

===Original===

Our own tables came next, and once again I was sitting down to a meal with my mother beside me and the kindly spirits of the ancestors welcoming me and mine into cheerful companionship. Afterward we retired to the parlour and spent an hour in what Hanano called “getting-acquainted talk,” before Mother would confess to the weariness which her pale face already betrayed. Then we all gathered before the shrine, Taki and Sudzu sitting just within the doorway.
How familiar, and yet how strange! The chanting, the soft sound of the little bronze gong, Mother’s voice reading the sacred Buddhist scriptures that so often I had heard from the lips of the dear one who long ago had passed away﻿—oh, how quiet and safe it all seemed! The anxious loneliness of months was gone, and there crept into my heart a peace that had not been mine since the protected days when my little family were all together in the dear, dear home of our kind, beloved American mother.
“How alike are the two sides of the world!” I thought. “Both have many gods of little worth, but with one wise, loving, understanding Power over all, the time must surely come when we shall *all* understand.”
The weeks following were filled with new and unexpected lessons. I had had no thought but that family loyalty and natural affection were the only requisites necessary to draw together my mother and my children. But I soon discovered that, though neither loyalty nor affection was lacking, mutual interests were only possibilities of the future.
My attempts to combine the old and the new frequently resulted in my having to give up the combination and decide wholly in favour of one or the other. With material things this was only an inconvenience; but a puzzling problem, indeed, when it came to Mother’s old-fashioned ideas clashing with the advanced training of modern schools. Mother never criticized. She met all situations with a smile or some pleasant remark about the “new ways of the world”; but it was evident that she greatly distrusted the wisdom of spending so much time on boys’ studies and so little on flower-arranging, tea-serving, koto music, and other womanly accomplishments. And the gymnastic exercises which the children enthusiastically described, where whole classes of girls drilled on the school grounds, marching and singing with vigorous energy, were wholly contrary to her ideas of dignity.

===Chinese===

接着才摆上我们自家的食案；于是我又一次坐下来用饭，母亲在旁，列祖的慈灵含笑迎来我和我的孩子们，聚在热热闹闹的一处。饭后我们退到客厅，闲话了约莫一个钟头——花野管这叫「认亲谈话」——母亲才认了乏；她那张苍白的脸，原早已招了出来。随后我们齐集在神龛前，多喜和阿铃坐在门槛里边。
多么熟悉，又多么新奇！诵经声，小铜磬幽幽的一响，母亲诵读佛经的声音——从前我常常从那位早已故去的亲人唇边听来的经文——哦，这一切显得多么安稳、多么妥帖！几个月来焦灼的孤寂散了，一股宁静悄悄爬进我心里；自从我们的小家团团圆圆住在亲爱仁慈的美国妈妈那座亲亲爱的家里、那些受着庇护的日子以来，这样的宁静便不曾有过。
「世界的两面多么相像啊！」我想，「两面都有许多不足道的小神；可最高的那一位，智慧、慈爱、洞达一切——总有一天，我们*大家*都要明白的。」
随后那几个礼拜，充满了种种新奇的、意想不到的功课。我原以为，只要家人同心、天性有爱，我的母亲和我的孩子们自然便亲了。可我不久便发现：同心与爱固然一样不缺，「共同的趣味」却只是将来的指望。
我常想使新与旧合在一起，结果往往合不成，只能整个儿偏到一头去。东西物件，这不过是不方便；可母亲的老观念同新式学堂的新教育一碰上，便真成了难题。母亲从不挑剔指责。她逢事总是一笑，或是一句「世道的新风气」说得和和气气；可是，男孩子的功课花那么多工夫，插花、奉茶、弹筝和种种女子的才艺却花那么少，她显然大不以为然。孩子们兴冲冲描摹的体操——成班的女生在操场上列队操练，踏着步、唱着歌，个个劲头十足——更是同她体面的观念全然相悖。

===Original===

I tried to explain that these exercises were believed to be good for health and growth. I told her it was no longer considered bold and mannish for girls to sit straight and to carry the head upright when they walked; and that even Hanano’s habit of chatting happily about school matters while we were eating, which seemed to Mother the manners of a coolie, was in accordance with her training at school.
Chiyo’s gentle ways had appealed to Mother at once, but her sister’s quick, busy, energetic manner was a constant surprise and puzzle. Hanano was so active, so apt to speak without being spoken to, and so constantly doing what, according to strict etiquette, were abrupt and discourteous things, that I was continually on the alert to watch for and check her unexpected acts. It was not long before I became unhappily conscious that my only hours of freedom from anxiety lay between the time when Hanano tied up her school books and, jumping into her clogs at the door, ran off, gaily waving a goodbye, and the afternoon hour when the door would slide open and a cheery, “I have come back!” come echoing through the hall.
But this did not last. Gradually, I scarcely know when or how, the silent strain lessened. Hanano was growing more quiet in her talk, more gentle in her manners. Frequently I would see her settle herself beside Chiyo at Mother’s firebox to listen to stories or to receive help as she read aloud, and one day I found both children snuggled up close, one on each side, while Mother showed Hanano how to write the characters for “American Grandma.”
Chiyo had loved Mother from the beginning. The child’s affectionate advances were somewhat of a shock at first, but very soon the two were congenial companions. It was odd that religion should be one of the binding cords. The kindergarten was just beyond the temple, so Chiyo was familiar with the road, and as I did not like to have Mother go alone, Chiyo often went with her when Sudzu was busy. The child liked to sit in the great solemn place and listen to the chanting, and she liked to be given rice-cakes by the mild-faced priestess who served tea to Mother after the service. One day Mother said: “Chiyo, you are very kind to come with me to the temple. Next time I will go with you to your church.” So Chiyo took her to hear our minister, a good man who preached in Japanese.

===Chinese===

我试着解释，说这些运动于身体发育、于健康都是好的。我告诉她，如今女子坐得笔直、走路昂头，再不算轻狂、不像男人家了；连花野吃饭时高高兴兴谈论学校里的事——这在母亲看来是苦力的做派——也是学堂里教出来的规矩。
千代的温软性子，母亲一见便疼；姐姐那副麻利、忙碌、风风火火的做派，却叫她时时惊奇、时时纳闷。花野那样好动，那样不等问就开口，那样接连不断地做出照严格礼数说来唐突失礼的事，叫我时刻悬着心，提防她那防不胜防的一举一动。不久我便怏怏地发觉，我一天里唯一不操心的时辰，只有从花野捆好书本、在门口蹬上木屐、一路挥手蹦跳着跑走，到下午门口拉门一响、一声欢快的「我回来啦！」传遍门厅之间。
可这也过去了。渐渐地——我说不清是几时、是怎样——那无声的紧张松了下来。花野说话渐渐轻了，举止渐渐柔了。我常看见她挨着千代坐在母亲的火盆边，听故事，或是朗读时领教指点；有一天，我看见两个孩子一边一个偎得紧紧的，母亲正教花野写「美国外婆」几个字。
千代从一开头就爱母亲。孩子那副亲昵劲儿起初叫母亲吓了一跳，可没过多久，两个便成了投缘的伴儿。说也奇怪，把两人拴在一处的绳子，竟有一条是宗教。幼儿园就在寺院那边，路千代熟；我不放心母亲一个人走，阿铃忙的时候，便常由千代陪着去。孩子爱坐在那庄严的大殿里听诵经，也爱从那位和眉善目的女师父手里接米饼——法事过后，是她给母亲奉茶的。有一天母亲说：「千代，你陪我上寺院，真是有心。下一回，我陪你上你们教堂去。」于是千代带她去听我们的牧师讲道——一位好人，用日语讲道。

===Original===

After that they often went together, sometimes to the temple, where Chiyo stood with bowed head while her grandmother softly rubbed her rosary between her hands and murmured, “*Namu Amida Butsu!*” and sometimes to the Christian church, where Mother listened attentively to the sermon and bowed in reverence when the Minister prayed. Then hand in hand they would come home together, talking of what they had heard at one place or the other. One day as they entered the gate, I heard Mother say gently: “It may be that he said true things, Chiyo, but I must not go to a better place than where my honourable husband is. Even if he is in the dreadful Hell of Cold, it is my duty to be with him. The Christian faith is for the new generation, like you, little Chiyo, but I must follow the path of my ancestors.”
One afternoon, when I was sewing in my room, I heard Chiyo’s voice beyond the closed doors.
“Honourable Grandmother,” she said, “when are you going to die?”
I pushed back the sliding door. There was Mother with Chiyo snuggled up beside her on the same cushion. I was astonished, for in my day no child would have dared to be so familiar with an elder, but there she was, and both were looking down gravely at an array of tiny lacquer boxes spread out on the floor. A large box, into which the smaller ones fitted closely, was near by. How well I remembered that box! All through my childhood it was kept in a drawer of my mother’s toilet cabinet, and every once in a while she would take out the little boxes and sprinkle powdered incense into each one. This was what she was doing now.
“I wish I had those pretty boxes for my dolly,” said Chiyo.
“Oh, no, little Granddaughter,” Mother said, lifting one of the tiny boxes and shaking gently the curved bits that looked like shavings of pale shell. “These are my nail clippings that have been saved all my life.”
“Your fingernails﻿—and your toenails!” cried the child. “Oh, my! How funny!”
“Hush, little Granddaughter. I am afraid you have not been trained to respect the traditions of your ancestors. We have to save our nails and cutoff baby hair so that our bodies may be perfect when we start on the long journey. The time cannot be far away,” she said, gazing thoughtfully out into the garden.
Chiyo had been peering curiously into the boxes, but now her face suddenly sobered and she drew a little closer to her grandmother.

===Chinese===

打那以后，她们常常同去：有时上寺院，千代垂着头立在一旁，祖母在指间轻轻捻着念珠，低诵「南无阿弥陀佛」；有时上基督教堂，母亲恭恭敬敬听讲道，牧师祷告时，她也深深俯首。然后两个人手拉着手一路回家，讲着这边那边听来的话。有一天进门的时候，我听见母亲温温和和地说：「他说的兴许是真的，千代；可我不能到比我尊贵的丈夫更好的地方去。就算他在那可怕的寒冰地狱里，我也该陪着他。基督教的道理，是给你们新一代人的，小千代；我呢，得走我祖先的路。」
一天下午，我在自己屋里做针线，隔着关着的拉门听见千代的声音。
「祖母大人，」她说，「您什么时候死呀？」
我推开拉门。母亲坐在那里，千代偎在她身边，同坐一个坐垫。我吃了一惊——在我小时候，哪个孩子也不敢这样同长辈亲——可她确确实实坐在那里，两个人都低着头，一本正经地望着地板上摆开的一列小漆盒。旁边一只大盒，小盒一只只正好嵌进去。那盒子我记得多清楚啊！我整个童年，它都收在母亲梳妆匣的抽屉里；隔一阵子，她便把小盒一只只取出来，往每只里撒一撮香粉。此刻她做的正是这件事。
「这些漂亮盒子给我娃娃用就好了。」千代说。
「哦，不行，小外孙女，」母亲说着，掀起一只小盒，轻轻摇了摇那些弯弯的碎片——看着像淡色贝壳削下来的刨花。「这是我的指甲，存了一辈子的。」
「您的手指甲——还有脚趾甲！」孩子叫起来，「哎呀呀！多好玩呀！」
「嘘，小外孙女。我看哪，你还没受教，不懂得敬重祖先的规矩。我们得把指甲和小时剪下的胎发都存着，好教我们动身上远路的时候，身子是完完全全的。日子不会远了。」她说着，若有所思地望进园子里。
千代本来正探着头朝盒里张望，这时忽然正色起来，往祖母身边偎近了些。

===Original===

“My heart is troubled, Honourable Grandmother,” she said. “I thought it would be a long, long time. You said you had always, even when you were a little girl, put perfume in the boxes to keep them nice and all ready for your death.”
Mother lovingly stroked the little black head with her wrinkled hand.
“Yes, but it will not be long now. I have finished my life work, and the merciful Buddha is preparing my platform of lotus blossoms, I am very sure.”
“Does the merciful Buddha want you to take your old clipped nails with you when you go to the lotus platform?”
“No; he does not care about my body. He cares only for me.”
“Then why did you save your nails so carefully?”
Mother glanced toward the closed shrine.
“The holy shrine, little Chiyo, is only a box when it is empty,” she said, “and my body is only a borrowed shrine in which I live. But it is proper courtesy to leave a borrowed article in the best condition.”
Chiyo’s eyes looked very deep and solemn for a moment.
“That’s why we have to take a bath every day and always keep our teeth clean. Dear me! I never thought of that as being polite to God.”
I had been so anxious over the children’s shortcomings in etiquette and so happy over the slow but satisfactory outcome that I had never given a thought to the changes which my years in America must have made in myself. One afternoon, coming back from a hurried errand, I was walking rapidly up the road toward home when I saw Mother standing in the gateway watching me. I knew that she disapproved of my undignified haste, as indeed she should, for nothing is more ungraceful than a hurrying woman in Japanese dress.
She met me with her usual bow, then said with a gentle smile, “Etsu-bo, you are growing to be very like your honourable father.”
I laughed, but my cheeks were hot as I walked up the path beside her, accepting silently the needed reproof, for no Japanese woman likes to be told that her walk suggests that of a man. Occasional hints like this kept my manners from marching with my mind on the road to progress; and under the same quiet influence my two active American children gradually changed into two dignified Japanese girls. Within two years’ time both spoke Japanese without accent and both wore Japanese dress so well that to strangers they appeared to have lived always in Japan.

===Chinese===

「我心里难受，祖母大人，」她说，「我本想还早着、早着呢。您说过，您从小小姑娘的时候起，就往盒子里搁香料，把它们弄得香香的，时时刻刻备着您的后事。」
母亲用她那皱纹的手，疼爱地抚着那颗小黑头。
「是啊，可是不会太久了。我这一生的活计已经做完，慈悲的佛陀正在给我搭莲花的台座呢，我笃定得很。」
「慈悲的佛陀要您带着剪下的旧指甲，上莲花台去吗？」
「不；他不挂念我的身子。他只挂念我。」
「那您为什么把指甲存得那样仔细呢？」
母亲朝关着的神龛望了一眼。
「那座圣龛，小千代，空着的时候不过是一只盒子，」她说，「我的身子，也不过是借来栖身的一座龛。可借来的物件，还回去的时候要还得妥妥帖帖，才是礼数。」
千代的眼睛一时显得又深又庄重。
「怪不得我们得天天洗澡、天天刷牙。哎呀！我从没想过，这是对上帝讲礼貌。」
我一直惦着孩子们礼数上的欠缺，又为那缓慢却圆满的结果欢喜，倒从没想一想，我自己在美国那些年，身上该起了多少变化。一天下午，我出门勿勿办了件事回来，正沿着路快步往家走，看见母亲站在大门口望着我。我知道她不赞成我这不成体统的匆忙——她也实在该不赞成，因为再没有比一个穿着和服疾走的女人更不雅观的了。
她照常一躬迎我，然后带着温和的笑说：「津保，你越长越像你尊贵的父亲了。」
我笑了；可挨着她走上小径时，我的脸颊热辣辣的，默默受了这一番必要的责备——因为再没有一个日本女人，肯听人说自己走路像男人家。这样时不时的提点，拦住了我的举止，教它不至随着我的心一同奔上「进步」的大路；也是在这同一份静静的熏陶里，我那两个风风火火的美国孩子，渐渐变成了两个端庄的日本姑娘。不出两年，两个说起日语来都没了口音，和服也穿得地地道道，在生人眼里，就像生来就住在日本似的。

===Original===

“Just to be in the same house with Mother is excellent training for a girl,” I thought, congratulating myself that Hanano had adapted herself so well to her grandmother’s standards. Selfishly busy with my daily duties, and content that our home was so harmonious, I had forgotten that, when duty lies between the old and the young, Nature’s law points direct to youth. I was counting the gain only﻿—but what of the loss?
One day in the cherry-blossom season, Hanano was sitting at her desk near mine when a light breeze touched the branches of a cherry tree near the porch and some pale pink petals drifted across her desk. She picked one up and after holding it a moment, pressed it gently between her fingers, then threw it aside, and sat looking at the damp spot on her finger.
“What are you thinking, Hanano?” I asked.
She looked up startled, then slowly turned away.
“One time in America,” she said after a moment, “when many people were at our house﻿—I think it must have been an afternoon tea﻿—I got tired and went out on the lawn. I climbed to my castle, you remember, the seventh limb of the big apple tree. The blossoms were just falling and a petal fell right into my hand. It left a wet spot, just like this cherry petal did. Oh, Mamma, wouldn’t you give just *everything* to see Grandma again﻿—and the porch, and the trees, and﻿—”
The little black head went down on the desk, but before I could reach her it was up again, held high.
“It’s all right,” she said; “I love Japan﻿—now. But there used to be times when my breast was just full of red-hot fire, and I had to run fast﻿—fast. And once, when you were all away, I climbed the prickly pine by the porch﻿—just once. But I don’t want to any more. It’s all right. I love it here.”
I remembered, then, how sometimes she had scampered around and around the garden, her sleeves flying in the wind and her clogs clattering over the stepping-stones; and I, ignorant and unsympathetic mother that I was, had taken her to my room and talked to her about being gentle and quiet.
But that was a long time before. Gradually she had learned to talk a little lower, to laugh a little less, to walk a bit more noiselessly on the matting, and to sit silent and attentive with bowed head when her elders were speaking. Only the other day Mother had said: “Granddaughter shows great promise. She is growing gentle and graceful.”

===Chinese===

「女孩子能有母亲同住一宅，就是顶好的教养，」我想，一面暗自庆幸花野把外婆的规矩学得这样到家。我自私地忙着日常的事务，满意于家里的和顺，竟忘了：本分夹在老一代和少一代中间的时候，自然的法则直指着少年。我只数着得的那一头——失的那一头呢？
樱花时节的一天，花野坐在她挨着我的书桌前；一阵微风拂过游廊边一株樱树的枝子，几片淡粉的花瓣飘过她的桌面。她拾起一片，握了一会儿，用手指轻轻一捏，丢开，坐着端详指尖上那一小点湿痕。
「想什么呢，花野？」我问。
她一惊，抬起头，又缓缓转过脸去。
「有一回在美国，」过了一会儿她说，「家里来了许多人——我想准是一次午后茶会——我待腻了，就跑到草地上。我爬上我的城堡，妈妈记得的，大苹果树的第七根枝杈。花正落着，一片花瓣恰恰落在我手心里。它留下一个湿印子，就跟这樱花瓣一样。哦，妈妈，要是能再见外婆一面——还有游廊，还有那些树——你肯不肯拿*一切*去换？」
那颗小黑头伏倒在桌上；我还不及走到她跟前，它又抬了起来，抬得高高的。
「没关系的，」她说，「我爱日本——如今爱了。可从前有些时候，我胸口满满的，全是烧红的火，我得快跑——快跑。有一回，你们都不在，我爬上了游廊边那棵扎人的松树——就那一回。可我再也不想爬了。没关系的。我爱这儿。」
我这才想起来，她从前有时绕着园子一圈一圈地疯跑，衣袖迎风翻飞，木屐在踏石上橐橐作响；而我，又懵懂又没心肝的母亲，把她领进屋里，同她讲要文静、要稳重。
可那是很久以前了。渐渐地，她学会了说话低一点，笑少一点，走在席上轻一点，长辈说话时垂头端坐、肃静聆听。就在前几天，母亲还说：「孙女很有出息。越长越温柔，越长越端丽了。」

===Original===

As I sat and thought, I wondered if Hanano was ever really happy any more. She never seemed sorrowful, but she had changed. Her eyes were soft, not bright; her mouth drooped slightly and her bright, cheery way of speaking had slowed and softened. Gentle and graceful? Yes. But where was her quick readiness to spring up at my first word? Where her joyous eagerness to see, to learn, to do? My little American girl, so full of vivid interest in life, was gone.
With a feeling of helplessness I looked over at her desk and was comforted; for the touch of homesickness had passed away and she was studying busily.
An hour later, when I went unexpectedly to her room, I saw her kneeling beside an open drawer where her American clothes were kept. She had pulled out her old serge suit, and her face was buried in its folds. I crept away to the garden. I could not see, and I stumbled over a flower pot. It was a dwarf pine. The pushing roots had burst the pot, and my touch had caused it to fall apart, disclosing the roots cramped together in a twisted knot.
“It is just like poor Hanano!” I moaned. “They will bind it again tomorrow, and neither it, nor she, will *ever* be free!”

===Chinese===

我坐在那里想：花野如今还真正快乐过吗？她从不见愁容，可她变了。她的眼睛温软了，不再明亮；嘴角微微地垂着，那副清亮欢快的谈吐，也慢了、柔了。温柔端丽？是的。可我一开口她就一跃而起的那份利落呢？她那看、学、做样样争先的欢劲呢？我那个对生活处处闪着火花的美国小姑娘，没有了。
我怀着无能为力的心情朝她的书桌望去，心里一宽：那阵乡愁已经过去，她正埋头用功呢。
一个钟头以后，我无意间走到她屋里，看见她跪在一只拉开的抽屉旁——她那身美国衣裳就收在那里。她把那身旧的呸叽呢衣裳拽了出来，脸深深埋在衣褶里。我悄悄退到园子里。眼前看不清了，脚下绊着一只花盆。那是一盆矮松。撑胀的根把花盆胀破了；我这一碰，它整个散了开来，露出那盘根错节、紧紧蜷作一团、动弹不得的根。
「这正是可怜的花野！」我呜咽道，「明天他们又要把它捆起来；它，还有她，*永远*都自由不了啦！」

## XXVIII Sister’s Visit / 二十八 姐姐来访
===Original===

That summer Mother was far from well. Lately her occasional attacks of asthma had become more frequent and trying. Thinking that a visit from my elder sister, who had always lived near our old home, would give Mother the happiness, not only of seeing her daughter, but also of hearing the pleasant gossip of old neighbours and friends, I wrote asking her to come to Tokyo. In a few weeks she was with us and proved a veritable blessing to us all. She was a comfort to Mother, a wise adviser to me, and an encyclopedia of interesting family history to the children; for there was nothing Sister liked better than telling stories of our old home as it was when she was a child.
Almost every day that summer, about the time the sun was sinking behind the tiled roof of our neighbour’s tall house and the cool shadows were creeping across our garden, we would gather in the big room opening on the porch. One at a time we came, each fresh from a hot bath and clothed in the coolest of linen. Mother sat on her silk cushion, straight and dignified; but Sister, more informal, usually discarded a cushion, preferring, instead, the cool, clean mats. She was a beautiful woman. I can see her now, slipping quietly into her place, the suggestion of a wave in her shining widow-cut hair, and her sweet face seeming to be only waiting for an excuse to break into one of her gentle smiles. Between Sister and Mother were the children: Hanano’s fingers, always busy, shaping bits of gay silk into a set of beanbags or cutting out a paper doll for Chiyo, who, gazing with loving admiration at her sister, sat with her own dear, lazy little hands folded on her lap.
This was our hour to spend in talking of the small happenings of the day: school successes and trials, incidents connected with home affairs and stray items of neighbourhood gossip. But almost inevitably the conversation would eventually drift into a channel that called forth from someone the familiar, “Oh, isn’t that interesting! Tell us about it!” or “Yes, I remember. Do tell that to the children.”
One afternoon Mother mentioned that the priest had called that day to make arrangements for a certain temple service called “For the Nameless” that was held by our family every year.
“Why is it called ‘For the Nameless’?” asked Hanano. “It has such a lonesome sound.”
“It is a sad story,” replied Mother.

===Chinese===

那年夏天，母亲身子很不好。她偶发的气喘，近来发作得又勤又凶。我想，若是我那位一向住在老家近旁的姐姐能来一趟，母亲便不但见得着女儿，还能听听旧邻老友的闲话，必是快活的；于是写信请她来东京。不出几个星期她就到了，实在是我们全家的福星。她宽母亲的心，给我出明白主意，对孩子们，她就是一部有趣的家史的活宝典；因为天下事姐姐顶爱的，莫过于讲讲她小时候老家的光景。那年夏天，几乎每天，当太阳沉到邻家高楼瓦脊背后、清凉的影子悄悄爬过我们的园子时，我们便聚到那间朝游廊敞开的大屋里。我们一个一个地来，各人都刚泡过热水澡，穿着最凉快的麻布衣。母亲坐在她的绸坐垫上，腰身挺直，仪态庄重；姐姐却随便些，往往不要坐垫，宁肯就坐在清凉干净的席子上。她是个美丽的女人。我如今还看得见她悄悄入座的模样：寡居的短发梳得熨帖，隐隐带着波纹，乌亮乌亮；那张甜美的脸，仿佛只等一个由头，就要绽出她那温柔的笑来。姐姐和母亲当中，坐着孩子们：花野的手指头一刻不闲，把彩绸片儿缝成一套一套的豆袋儿，或是给千代剪纸人；千代呢，满眼倾慕地望着姐姐，把自己那双宝贝似的懒懒小手，交叠着搁在膝上。这一个钟点，我们用来谈当天的零碎事：学堂里的得意和烦恼，家事的种种，左邻右舍的片言只语。可说着说着，话头十有八九要漂进那样一条河道，惹得谁冒出一句熟惯的：「呀，这个有意思！快讲讲！」或者：「对，我记得。讲给孩子们听听吧。」一天下午，母亲说起，寺里的法师当天来过了，为的是安排一件我们家年年做的法事，唤作「祭无名氏」。「为什么叫『祭无名氏』呢？」花野问。「听上去怪冷清的。」「那是个伤心的故事。」母亲答道。

===Original===

“A story that began almost three hundred years ago and has not yet ended.”
“How could Kikuno’s story have anything to do with the little room at the end of the hall?” I asked abruptly, my mind going back to the half-forgotten memory of a door that was never opened. “It didn’t happen in that house.”
“No; but the hall room was built right over the haunted spot,” replied Sister. “Is it true, Honourable Mother, that after the mansion was burned someone planted chrysanthemum in the garden, and soft, mysterious lights were seen floating among the flowers?”
Hanano had dropped her sewing into her lap, and both children were gazing at Sister with eager, wide-open eyes.
“Your fate until dinner time is decided, Sister,” I laughed. “The children scent a story. Now you can tell them why you wouldn’t use the cushion decorated with chrysanthemums at the restaurant the other day.”
“I must seem foolish-minded to you, Etsu-bo, with your progressive ideas,” said Sister, with a half-ashamed smile, “but I have never outgrown the feeling that chrysanthemums are an omen of misfortune to our family.”
“I know,” I said, sympathetically. “I used to feel so, too. I didn’t really get over it until I went to America. The name Mary is as common there as Kiku is here, but I had associated it only with sacredness and dignity; for it is the holiest name for a woman in the world. Some people even pray to it. And when, one time, just after I went to America, I heard a shopwoman call roughly, ‘Mary, come here!’ and out ran a ragged child with a dirty face, I was astounded. And a neighbour of ours had an ignorant servant girl by that name. It was a shock at first; but I finally learned that association is a narrow thing. When we apply it broadly the original feeling does not fit.”
“People learn to forget when they travel,” said Sister quietly; “but as far back as I can remember, no chrysanthemum flower was ever brought into our house, no chrysanthemum decoration was ever used on our screens, our dishes, our dresses, or our fans; and, with all the pretty flower names in our family, that of Kiku﻿—chrysanthemum﻿—has never been borne by an Inagaki. Even a servant with that name was never allowed to work for us unless she was willing to be called something else while she lived in our house.”
“Why? Oh, do tell us about it!” pleaded both children.

===Chinese===

「一个差不多三百年前开了头、至今还没有完的故事。」「菊野（Kikuno）的故事，跟走廊尽头那间小屋有什么相干？」我冲口问道，心里浮起一段半已忘却的旧事——一扇从来不开的门。「那件事又不是出在这所宅子里。」「不是；可走廊那间屋，正正建在闹鬼的地界上。」姐姐答道。「母亲大人，老宅烧了之后，有人在园子里种下菊花，花丛里便见着一团团柔和的、幽幽的光——真的有这事么？」花野早把针线活掠在膝上，两个孩子都睁大了眼睛，眼巴巴望着姐姐。「姐姐，你到晚饭前算是有事做了，」我笑道。「孩子们闻见故事味儿了。正好，你也讲讲，那天在饭馆里，你为什么不肯用绣着菊花的坐垫。」「在你这样满脑子新思想的津保看来，我大概是迷信脑筋，」姐姐半是羞惭地笑着说，「可我到老也改不掉这个念头：菊花，于我们家是不祥之兆。」「我懂，」我深有同感地说。「我从前也是这样。直到去了美国才真的放下来。『玛丽』这个名字在那边，就同『菊』在这边一样平常；可我从前只把它同神圣庄严联在一处——那是世上女人最圣洁的名字，有人还向它祷告呢。刚到美国不久，有一回听见一个开铺子的女人粗声粗气喊：『玛丽，过来！』跑出来一个衣衫褴褛、满脸脏污的孩子，我惊得说不出话。我们的一个邻居，家里使唤的粗蠢丫头也叫这个名字。起初真是震动；可我后来明白了：联想原是顶狭隘的东西，一旦放宽了用，原先那点情味就不合身了。」「出门在外的人，学会把事情忘掉，」姐姐静静地说，「可从我记事起，我们家从不曾有一枝菊花进门；屏风上、器皿上、衣裳上、扇子上，从不曾用过一样菊花的纹样；我们家里好听的花名那样多，『菊』这个字，却从不曾落在稻垣家一个人头上。连名字里带菊的仆人，也不许进我们家做工——除非她住在家里的时候，肯改个别的称呼。」「为什么呀？哦，快讲给我们听！」两个孩子一齐恳求。

===Original===

So again I heard the story, familiar from childhood, but changing continually in its significance as I grew older, until it became fixed in my mind as the hero tale of a brave old samurai who represented the double virtue of a great and tender love combined with the hard, cold strength of loyalty to duty.
This ancestor of mine was lord of our family during the period when it was a government requirement that men of his class should have two handmaids. This was to guard against the possibility of there being no heir, that being an unspeakable calamity to people who believed that a childless family meant heavenly annihilation. Handmaids were always selected by the wife, from families of her own rank; and their position, although inferior in influence, was considered as honoured and lofty as that of the wife.
The second of my ancestor’s handmaids was named Kikuno. Her lord was old enough to be her father, but it must be true that he loved her, for our family records show that he loaded her relatives with gifts and with honours. Of course, we Japanese never say anything not nice about our ancestors, and it may be that family traditions are not always reliable, but they all praise this man, and I like to believe them true.
Every house of noble class, in those days, was divided into the home department, ruled by the mistress, where there were only women attendants, and the lord’s department, where every branch of work was done by men. For delicate and artistic duties, such as tea-serving and flower-arranging, graceful youths were chosen who dressed in gay garments with swinging sleeves like girls, and wore their hair in an artistic crown-queue with fluffy sides.
Among these attendants of my ancestor was a youth who was an especial favourite. He must have possessed both rank and culture, for he was the son of his lord’s highest retainer. Although the departments of the lord and the mistress were entirely separate, there was daily passing back and forth on formal errands, and also many gatherings for duty or for entertainment, in which both men and women took part. On these occasions the gentle Kikuno and the handsome youth were frequently thrown together. She was only seventeen. Her lord was twice her age, and his thoughts were of war and its grim duties.

===Chinese===

于是我又听了一遍这个故事——从童年就熟的，可随着我年岁渐长，它的意味一变再变，末了在我心里凝成了这样一篇英雄故事：一位刚勇的老武士，身上兼着两样德性——博大温慈的爱，和忠于本分的那种冷硬如铁的坚劲。我这位先祖，是我们一族的领主；那时节，朝廷有令，凡他这一阶级的男子，须置两名侧室。这是防备膝下无嗣——在信奉「无后之家即天绝」的人们看来，那是说不出口的大祸。侧室向来由正室拣选，必得出身相当的门户；她们的名分，虽然权势上不及正室，体面尊贵却视同夫人。我先祖的第二位侧室，名唤菊野。她的主君老得足以做她的父亲；可他必定是疼她的，因为族谱家录上明明白白，他给她的亲眷厚赠了财礼荣衔。当然，我们日本人对自家祖上是从来不说坏话的，家族传闻也未必句句可靠；可众口一词都称道这位先祖，我乐意信它们是真的。那时节，凡高贵门第的人家，都分内外两府：内府归主母执掌，当差的全是女子；主君的外府，各行差事皆由男子充任。至于斟茶、插花这类细巧风雅的职司，则选俊雅的少年充当——他们穿着彩衣，飘着同女子一样的长袖，头发梳成顶髻，两边蓬蓬松松，煞是好看。我先祖外府的侍从里，有一位少年，是格外得宠的。他必有身份也有教养，因为他是主君股肱重臣之子。外府内府虽然隔绝，每日因公务往来递送，还有些因差事或行乐的聚会，男女都在一处。这样的场合，温柔的菊野和那俊美的少年，便常常打照面。她才十七岁；她的主君比她年长一倍，心里想的是战阵和军旅的重任。

===Original===

The gentle, soft-voiced youth, whose talk was of poetry and flowers, won her heart; and it was the old story of Launcelot and Guinevere.
We have no reason to believe that any real wrong was in the heart of either; but a Japanese girl was taught from childhood to subdue self, and when she married﻿—and to become a handmaid was one type of marriage﻿—she was expected to live with no thought of self at all.
Rumours reached the ears of the master, but he waved them aside as absurd. One day, however, he walked into the great room adjoining the court and found the two talking in low voices, and﻿—an unpardonable breach of etiquette﻿—alone. This was, of course, a stain on the family name, which, according to the code of honour of that day, could be wiped out only with blood, or﻿—a disgrace a thousand times worse than death﻿—the exile of the culprits through the water gate, thus making them outcasts.
The old lord was merciful and allowed them honourable death by the sword. Both recognized the justice of their fate. Kikuno went away to prepare for death, and the young man, with slow and ceremonious dignity, removed his two swords and slipped his right arm from his outer dress, leaving only the white silk undergarment. Then he gave the sash a quick, loosening jerk, and with his short sword in his hand, quietly seated himself on the mat. I often pity the wronged lord as I think of him sitting there, erect and silent. I know his heart was full of grief as well as bitterness and indignation, but whatever the struggle within, he had to be true to the duty plainly marked out by the inexorable usage of the day.
Poor Kikuno went to her baby boy for a few last loving touches as he lay sleeping in his nurse’s arms, but she said goodbye to no one else. She washed the rouge from her lips, loosened her hair, tied it with the paper death-bow, and put on her white death-robe. Then she went back to the room where her lover and her lord were silently waiting.
Without the slightest deviation, the unchanging ceremony of Japanese etiquette was carried out. She kneeled and bowed deeply, first to her wronged lord and then to the beautiful girl-dressed youth beside him. Seating herself with her face to the west, she took her long sash of soft crêpe and tightly bound her folded knees.

===Chinese===

那嗓音温柔的少年，谈的是诗，是花，赢得了她的心；这原是兰斯洛特与桂妮薇儿的老故事。我们没有理由相信两人心里有什么真正的罪恶；只是日本女孩自幼受的教养是克己，既嫁了人——为人侧室也是婚嫁的一种——便须活得心里再没有自己。风声传到主君耳朵里，他一挥手斥为荒唐。可是有一天，他走进临院的那间大屋，正撞见两人低声说话——而且四下无人，这是不可饶恕的失仪。这自然辱没了家门；照当日荣誉的法度，家名之玷，唯有用血洗刷——再不然，便是把这一对罪人从水门逐出家门，沦为弃民，那是比死难堪一千倍的奇耻。老主君慈悲，允他们以刀自裁，全一个体面的死。两人也都认了他们命分里这一点公道。菊野退下去装殓待死；那少年从容不迫、一丝不苟地卸下双刀，右臂褪出外袍，只留一身白绸贴身衣，把腰带使劲一抖松开，短刀握在手里，安安静静在席上坐定。我每每怜悯那位受了欺的主君，想他直挺挺默默坐在那里的模样。我知道他心里除了愤恨，也满是悲恸；可无论心里怎样翻腾，他必须忠于那个时代铁一般的成例给他明明白白划下的本分。可怜的菊野，到乳母怀里睡得正香的小儿子身旁，恋恋地抚了又抚，便再没有向任何人道别。她洗去唇上的胭脂，解散头发，束上送终的纸结，换上白色的寿衣；然后回到那间屋——她的情人和她的主君，正默默等在那里。日本礼法那一成不变的仪节，一步不曾错。她跪下来，深深行礼——先向被她辜负的主君，再向他身旁那个女装般秀美的少年。她朝西跪坐，解下长长的绉纱腰带，把交叠的双膝紧紧缠住。

===Original===

For one moment she placed together her hands, clasping a crystal rosary; then slipping the rosary over one wrist, she lifted her dagger to press the point to her throat. Her lord was a stern and a just man, but he must have loved the woman very tenderly, for he did a wonderful thing. Leaning quickly forward, he took away her dagger and placed in her hand his own short sword. It was a Masamune, a precious family heirloom, and sacred because a gift to his grandfather from the great Ieyasu.
Well, they both died: the youth, bravely, like a samurai; but poor Kikuno threw out one hand as she fell, which struck the plaster wall and left a lasting stain.
The man’s body was sent to his family with the polite message that his death had taken place suddenly. Everyone understood, and, like the youth himself, recognized the justice of his fate. He was buried at midnight, and ever afterward both the temple and his family gave him only silent death anniversaries. But the woman was buried with great honour﻿—suitable to the mother of the young lord﻿—and a large sum was given to charity in her name. Then the lord forbade any of his descendants ever to cultivate the chrysanthemum flower or to allow the name, Kiku, in the household. The baby, whose frail mother had robbed him of his birthright, was sent away﻿—for no stain must descend to the next generation﻿—and a later-born little one carried on the family name.
The bloodstained room was closed, and until the burning of the mansion about two hundred years later was never opened. When my father rebuilt his ruined home many of the relatives urged him to leave an open space above the site of that room, but he refused, saying that the kindly spirit of living friends had taught him to believe in the kindly spirit of the dead. My father was a very progressive man for that day.
But the servants never forgot. They said the new room had on its plaster wall the same faint, dark stain of a wide-open hand that was on the wall of the old; and so many ghostly stories were told, that finally, for purely practical reasons, my mother was obliged to close this room also.
The little son of Kikuno became a priest who, in later life, built a small temple on Cedar Mountain. It was so placed that its shadow falls over a lonely nameless grave guarded by a statue of the goddess of Mercy.
But the memory of love and pity cannot die.

===Chinese===

有一瞬，她双手合十，捧着一串水晶念珠；随后念珠套上手腕，她举起怀剑，把锋尖抵住咽喉。她的主君是个严峻刚正的人，可他必定极温柔地疼爱过这个女人，因为他做了一件惊人的事：他倏地俯身向前，夺下她的怀剑，把自己那柄短刀放进她手里。那是一柄正宗（Masamune），传家的至宝，更是圣物——当年是伟大的家康（Ieyasu）赐给他祖父的。末了，两人都死了。少年死得英勇，像个武士；可怜的菊野倒下时伸出一只手，拍在灰泥墙上，留下一个永不磨灭的印痕。男子的遗体送回他家里，附一句客客气气的话：说他暴病而终。人人都心里明白，也同那少年本人一样，认了这命里的一点公道。他下葬在半夜；从此以后，寺院和他家里，都只在忌日默默祭他，不出一声。那女子却以极大的哀荣下葬——合乎少主生母的身份——还以她的名义捐了一大笔香资行善。随后主君立下禁令：后代子孙永远不得种菊花，家里永远不得用『菊』这个名字。那个婴儿——他柔弱的母亲断送了他的嫡位——被送了出去（污点万不可传到下一代去）；后来另生的一个小的，承续了家名。那间染了血的屋子封闭了；直到约两百年后老宅焚毁，从未开启。我父亲重建残破的家宅时，许多亲戚劝他，那间屋的旧址上头，索性留一块空地；他不肯，说活人朋友的善灵，教他信死者的善灵。在那年月，我父亲要算极开通的人了。可仆人们始终没忘。他们说新屋的灰泥墙上，也有同旧屋一样的一痕淡淡的黑手印，五指张开；鬼话越传越多，到后来，只为图个清静实用，我母亲也不得不把那间屋重新封了。菊野的小儿子后来做了僧人，晚年时，在杉山上盖了一座小寺。寺的位置，恰教寺影罩着一座孤零零的无名坟，坟前立着一尊观音——慈悲女神的石像。可爱与哀怜的记忆，是不会死的。

===Original===

For almost three hundred years my stern old ancestor has lain among his people in his extravagant bed of vermilion and charcoal; and for almost three hundred years the descendants of the name whose honour he upheld have, in respect for his unexpressed heart wish, held each year a sacred service in memory of “The Nameless.”

===Chinese===

将近三百年来，我那严峻的老祖先，一直安卧在他的子民中间，卧在朱砂和木炭铺成的、过分隆重的墓床里；也将近三百年来，他为之守护了荣誉的那个姓氏的子孙，为敬重他那不曾出口的心愿，年年设坛追荐，祭那「无名氏」。

## XXIX A Lady of Old Japan / 二十九 古日本的一位贵妇
===Original===

One afternoon Sister and I were sewing in my room when Hanano came in. It was warm weather and the paper doors had been lifted off so that the entire fronts of the rooms facing the garden were open. We could look across and see Mother sitting beside the dining-room firebox, holding her long, slender pipe in her hand and gazing out into the garden as if her thoughts were far away.
“Mother is happy in this home,” said Sister. “Her face has the calm, peaceful look of the August Buddha.”
“I wonder,” said Hanano thoughtfully, “if Honourable Grandmother was ever really, strongly, *terribly* excited in all her life.”
Sister looked at Hanano with a strange smile.
“I never saw her *seem* excited,” she said slowly. “It was a terrible time when we left the old home, but Mother was calm and steady. She commanded like a general on the battlefield.”
“Oh, tell me!” cried Hanano, sitting up very straight. “Tell me all about it.”
“Perhaps it would be well, Sister,” I said. “Hanano is old enough to know. Tell her all of Mother’s life that you can remember.”
So she told how Mother, when only thirteen years of age, was lifted into her wedding palanquin and, accompanied by a long procession of attendants, headed by spearmen and followed by her father’s guards, journeyed to her new home. Father was First Counsellor of the daimiate, and his bride came to a mansion so spacious that in all the years she lived in it there were rooms in which she never set her foot. She saw little of her husband, for his duties as ruler obliged him to make frequent journeys to the capital, and the young wife filled her time in writing poems on slender cards of gold and silver, or playing dolls with her attendants; for, after all, she was only a child.
In time, a son and two daughters were born; but the little girls, with nurses to take every care from their mother, were a good deal like beautiful playthings to her; and her son, the heir who was to carry on the family name, had so many attendants with various duties that she saw him only at stated intervals. He was like a precious jewel for which she had strong affection, but still stronger was the feeling of pride. So in the big, peaceful mansion the girl-wife passed the pleasant, uneventful years.
Then changes came, for clouds of war were gathering slowly over the land.

===Chinese===

一天下午，姐姐和我在我屋里做针线，花野走了进来。天气和暖，纸门都已卸下，朝着园子的几间屋子，整个前脸敞开着。我们望过园子，看见母亲坐在饭厅的火盆边，手里握着她那支细长的烟管，凝望着园子出神，仿佛心思飘得很远。
「母亲在这个家里是快乐的，」姐姐说，「她脸上有一副安详平和的神色，同尊贵的佛陀一样。」
「我倒想知道，」花野若有所思地说，「祖母大人这一辈子，可曾真正地、强烈地、*天翻地覆地*激动过一回。」
姐姐带着一种古怪的笑容望着花野。
「我从没见她*像是*激动过，」她慢慢地说，「离开老家的时候，那是何等的年月；可母亲镇定沉着，像战场上督阵的将军。」
「哦，讲给我听！」花野喊起来，坐得笔直，「原原本本讲给我听。」
「讲讲也好，姐姐，」我说，「花野到了该知道的年纪了。把你记得的母亲的一生，都讲给她听吧。」
于是她讲起来：母亲才十三岁上，就被抱进了出嫁的彩轿，在长长的仪仗簇拥之下——前头是持枪的卫士，后头是她父亲的家臣——一路行到她的新家。父亲是藩国的首席重臣，他的新娘嫁进的是那样深广的一座大宅，以致她住了一辈子，里面还有她从不曾踏足过的屋子。她难得见丈夫一面；他身为一方之主，公务所系，须频频上京。年轻的主母便拿光阴消磨在金银薄笺上写诗，或是同侍女们玩偶人戏——毕竟，她自己也还是个孩子。
后来，生了一子二女；可两个小女儿，事事有乳母照料，母亲插不上手，倒很像是两件漂亮的玩物；儿子是承继家名的嗣子，侍候他的仆役各司其职、人数众多，母亲只在定例的时辰见得着他。他像一颗珍宝，她对他有很深的爱；可比爱更深的，是骄傲。就这样，在那座安安静静的大宅里，这位少妇主母，把平顺无事的年年岁岁，过得舒舒服服。
后来变故来了：战云，正慢慢在大地上聚拢。

===Original===

Her husband gradually told her of many important things, and one day he left home on a mission that filled her heart with dread. She was not far out of her teens, but she knew the duties of a samurai’s wife, and with suddenly awakened womanhood she called her son’s tutor and they disguised in shabby clothing her small son, whose life as heir would be forfeit if his father came to harm, and sent him, in the care of faithful Minoto, to the protection of our ancestral temple on the mountain. Then she waited, while every day the clouds grew more threatening. One dark, rainy night there came a warrior to her home bearing the tidings that Father was a prisoner and on his way to the capital. Near the midnight toll of the temple bell he would pass the road at the foot of the mountain, and she would be permitted an interview.
She looked at the messenger steadily. If there should be treachery what would become of her son?
“Are you a samurai?” she asked.
Solemnly the man put his hand to the hilt of his sword.
“I am a samurai,” he answered.
“Whether friend or enemy,” she said, “if you are a samurai, I will trust you.”
Though she believed him, those were dangerous days, and so she washed her hair and put on her death-robe, covering it with an ordinary dress. Then, slipping her dagger into her sash and bidding her faithful servant Yoshita to be loyal to his young master, whatever happened, she told the messenger she was ready.
Through the rain and darkness they went﻿—the warrior, his wet armour shining in the lantern-light, followed by Mother in her hidden death-robe. They passed through empty streets and along narrow paths of lonely ricefields until finally they came to the road which curved around the foot of the mountain. There they waited.
Presently lights came swaying through the darkness and they could hear the dull, soft thuds of trotting carriers, coming nearer and nearer, then to a stop. A palanquin covered with a rope net was rested on the ground, a warrior on each side. The carriers stood back. Mother looked up and saw Father’s pale face gazing at her through the small square window. The crossed spears of the warriors were between them. There was a moment’s silence, then Father spoke.
“My wife, I trust you with my sword.”
That was all. Both knew that listening ears were eager for word of the son. Mother only bowed, but Father knew that she understood.

===Chinese===

丈夫渐渐把许多要紧事说给她听。有一天，他奉命离家，此行叫她心里充满恐惧。她才二十出头，可她懂得武士之妻的本分。她身上陡然醒来了妇人的胆气，唤来儿子的师傅，把年幼的嗣子装扮成穷家孩子——父亲倘有不测，嗣子的性命便难保——托付给忠心耿耿的美浓户（Minoto），送到山上我们家的祖庙里避祸。然后她等候着，眼见战云一日日压得更沉。一个漆黑的雨夜，一名武士登门报信：父亲被囚，正被押解进京。寺钟敲过午夜不久，一行人将经过山脚下的那条路；准她见上一面。
她定定地望着那报信的人。万一其中有诈，她的儿子可怎么办？
「你是武士吗？」她问。
那人神情庄重，把手按上刀柄。
「我是武士。」他答道。
「不论敌友，」她说，「你既是武士，我便信你。」
话虽如此——她也信了他——可那毕竟是凶险的年月。于是她洗了头发，穿上赴死的白衣，外面罩一件寻常衣裳。又把短刀插进腰带，嘱咐忠仆芳田（Yoshita）：无论出什么事，都要效忠他年幼的主子。然后她告诉报信人：她准备好了。
两人冒雨踏黑而行——那武士湿漉漉的铠甲在灯笼光里闪亮，母亲穿着暗藏寿衣的衣裳，跟在后头。他们穿过空荡荡的街市，沿着冷清清的稻田小径，终于到了那条绕着山脚去的路上。在那里候着。
不多时，灯火摇摇晃晃地穿破黑暗而来；听得见轿夫小跑的、沉闷又轻软的脚步声，越来越近，越来越近，停下了。一顶罩着绳网的轿子落了地，左右各立着一名武士。轿夫退开。母亲抬眼，看见父亲苍白的脸，正从小小的方窗里望着她。武士们交叉的长枪，横在两人中间。片刻沉默，然后父亲开了口。
「我的妻，我把我这口刀托付给你。」
只这一句。两人都知道，多少竖起的耳朵，正候着儿子的下落。母亲只是深深一拜；可父亲知道，她懂了。

===Original===

The reed screen was dropped before the face of the prisoner, the warriors shouldered their spears, the carriers lifted the poles of the palanquin to their shoulders, and the little procession passed on into the darkness. The guide she had trusted raised his bowed head and turned toward the ricefields, and poor Mother followed, carrying with her the knowledge of a sacred trust; for those few words from Father’s lips meant: “Death is before me. I trust to you the son who will continue the name of Inagaki and thus insure the heavenly salvation of hundreds of ancestors.”
Again poor Mother bore the heavy burden of anxious uncertainty, until one autumn night when a messenger brought word that the plain was full of soldiers marching toward Nagaoka. For that she had been waiting; so, calm and fearless, she commanded that the entire house be arranged as for honoured guests. The most treasured roll pictures were hung, the rarest ornaments placed on tokonomas, then the retainers and servants were ordered to leave by a rear gateway and to scatter in various directions.
Sister was only a child of seven, but she remembered every detail of that awful night. She and little Sister were awakened by frightened nurses and hurried into dress and sash﻿—for even in their haste and horror the sash, emblem of virtue to every Japanese girl, could not be forgotten by the trusted servant of a samurai family﻿—and taken part way up the mountain to wait in the darkness for Mother, coming more slowly with Honourable Grandmother and two menservants.
Sister smiled faintly as she told how Honourable Grandmother and Mother looked as they came up the narrow path, disguised as farmers. Honourable Grandmother’s straw coat kept pulling apart and showing her purple dress, which was of a kind worn only by a retired mistress of her rank, and which she had stubbornly refused to have removed. And she would *not* walk with her toes turned out as peasants do.
Leaving Honourable Grandmother with them on the mountainside, Mother went back to the mansion with Yoshita. They could see the two, carrying torches of twisted paper, as they passed from point to point, Yoshita piling straw and Mother lighting with her own hands the fires to destroy her home.

===Chinese===

轿窗前放下了苇帘。武士们扛枪上肩，轿夫抬起轿杠，这一小队人马继续前行，没入黑暗。她信过的那名向导抬起低着的头，转身朝稻田走去；可怜的母亲跟在后头，怀里揣着一份神圣的嘱托；因为父亲唇间那寥寥数语，说的是：「死亡在我面前。我把承继稻垣家名的儿子托付给你——他保全着数百祖先的往生之路。」
可怜的母亲又担起那沉重难熬的悬心，直到一个秋夜，有信使来报：原野上满是兵马，正朝长冈进发。这一刻她等了许久；于是她镇静无惧，吩咐把整座宅子布置得如同迎贵客一般：最珍贵的立轴一幅幅挂起，最稀罕的摆设一件件安放到壁龛里；然后命令家臣仆役从后门退出，四散各方。
姐姐那时才七岁，可那个可怕的夜晚，一桩桩一件件，她记得清清楚楚。她和小妹被惊惶的乳母唤醒，急急穿衣束带——哪怕在那样的仓皇恐怖里，腰带——每个日本女孩贞节的表记——也不曾漏掉，武士人家的忠仆是漏不掉的——被带着上了半山，在黑暗里等候母亲；母亲同祖母大人和两名男仆，走得慢，落在后头。
姐姐讲起祖母大人和母亲走上窄径时的模样，微微一笑。两人都扮作庄稼人。祖母大人身上那件蓑衣总往两边分开，露出里头的紫裙——那颜色，只有她那样身份的隐居主母才穿得，她执拗着，死活不肯脱下。还有，她*就是*不肯像村妇那样撇着脚尖走路。
母亲把祖母大人留在山腰上同孩子们一处，便带着芳田回宅子去了。她们望见那两个人，举着纸捻的火把，一处一处地走；芳田堆草，母亲亲手点火，烧她自己的家。

===Original===

Honourable Grandmother sat perfectly quiet, gazing straight before her, but the servants knelt on the ground swaying back and forth, sobbing and wailing, as servants will. Then Mother, with dishevelled hair and smoke-stained face, came toiling up the path, and by the pale light of early dawn the two little girls were dressed in servants’ clothes from the bundle on Yoshita’s back, and the nurses were told to take them in different directions to places of safety. Servants were trustworthy in those days. To each was given a dagger with orders to use it in case capture was inevitable. Those crested daggers are still held as treasures in the families of the faithful nurses.
Sister said it was a long time before she saw Mother again. Her nurse took her to a farmer’s family where she dressed and lived as they did, and her nurse worked in the ricefield with the farmer’s wife. Every night, after her bath, she was rubbed with a brown juice squeezed from wild persimmons﻿—for castle people are lighter than peasants﻿—and was told to talk like the children she played with. She was treated like the others in every way except that always she was served first. “I know now,” explained Sister, “that the farmer suspected who I was, but we were in one of the districts where Father had bestowed upon the headman the privilege of owning two swords, and so we were not betrayed. Little Sister was in a similar place of safety.”
In the meantime, Honourable Grandmother and Mother, in the care of Yoshita, all wearing the dress and wide, drooping hats of peasants, had been wandering from place to place, sometimes living in the mountains, sometimes in a farmer’s family, and sometimes for a few weeks finding refuge in a temple. More than two years this dreadful time lasted; always hiding, always hunted; for though Father was a prisoner and his cause lost, conquest was not complete until the enemy had extinguished forever the family and name.
“At last,” Sister went on, “Mother came to the farmhouse where I was. She looked so thin, so brown, and so wild that I didn’t know her, and cried out. That night Minoto brought Brother. He told us that the priest, in order to save the child’s life, had given him up, and for several months he had been a prisoner with Father.

===Chinese===

祖母大人端坐不动，直直望着前方；仆人们跪在地上，前摇后晃，哭的哭，嚎的嚎——仆人总是这样的。随后，头发散乱、满脸烟灰的母亲，一步一步吃力地走上山径；趁拂晓惨淡的天光，两个小姑娘换上芳田背上包袱里仆人的衣裳；乳母们奉命各自领一个，往不同方向的安稳去处。那个年头，仆人是信得过的。每人发一把短刀，吩咐说：万一被擒、万难脱身，便用它。那几柄带家纹的短刀，至今还当传家宝，供在忠心乳母的儿孙家里。
姐姐说，过了很久她才又见到母亲。她的乳母把她带进一户农家，吃穿住都同那家一样；乳母跟着农妇下田。每天晚上，她洗过澡，要抹一身野柿子榨出的褐色汁水——城里人到底比庄稼人白净——还要学得同玩耍的孩子们说一样的话。样样她都同旁人一样，只有一件：吃饭总是她先。「如今我明白了，」姐姐解释道，「那农家疑心我的身份；只是我们那一带，父亲曾恩准过村长佩双刀的特权，所以没有人出卖我们。小妹也在相仿的一处安身。」
其间，祖母大人和母亲，由芳田照应着，都作农夫打扮，戴宽宽的垂檐笠帽，辗转流徙：有时住在山里，有时住进农家，有时在寺里躲上几个礼拜。这样可怕的时日，拖了两年多；一直躲着，一直被追捕；因为父亲虽已被囚，大势虽已去，可要等敌方把这个家族和这个姓氏斩草除根，才算得征服。
「后来，」姐姐接着说，「母亲到了我寄身的那家农舍。她又瘦、又黑、又憔悴，我竟认不出她，吓得叫了起来。当天夜里，美浓户把哥哥带来了。他告诉我们：师父为了救孩子一命，不得不把他交了出去；有几个月，他同父亲关在一处，做了囚徒。」

===Original===

Both had been very near the honourable death, but a message that the war was ended and all political prisoners were pardoned had saved them. Brother seemed to have almost forgotten me and would not talk much, but I heard him tell Mother that, one day, when soldiers were seen coming up the mountain, the priest had put him in a book chest and, covering him with rolls of sacred writings, had left the cover off and seated himself beside it as if arranging papers. Brother said that he heard rough footsteps and falling furniture, and when all was quiet and he was lifted out, he saw that spears had been thrust through the closed chests standing in the row with the one where he was hidden.”
The next day Mother had gathered her family together and Yoshita found a place where they could live. Then Father came, and in a modest way life began all over again.
“So you see, Hanano,” said Sister, “your grandmother’s life has not always been full of peace.”
“It was a wonderful life,” said Hanano in a tone of awe, “wonderful﻿—and terrible. But Honourable Grandmother *did* things! Oh, she *did* things!”
I looked at the lithe young body, held so straight, at the uplifted head and the tightly clasped hands. She was very like Mother. One generation removed from the ancient pride and rigid training; one generation ahead of the coming freedom; living, alas! in the sad present﻿—puzzled, misunderstood, and alone!
Sister remained with us throughout the autumn and into the winter. I shall always be doubly thankful for her visit, for those weeks were Mother’s last with us, and they were happy ones. The long talks when she and Sister lived over the old days were like those of friends rather than mother and daughter; for there was only fourteen years between them and Sister was as old-fashioned in many ways as Mother. And when the sorrowful time came, Sister’s presence was an especial comfort to me, for she was familiar with all the old customs and could direct with a tenderness that no other could have shown.
On our sad journey to the temple, as we followed the death *kago* swaying on the shoulders of the white-robed coolies, my thoughts went back to another day long before, when I, a child of eleven, walked in a procession of mourning friends, my little hands clasped tight about the tablet bearing my father’s name.

===Chinese===

两人都几乎到了那「光荣的死」跟前；幸而传来消息，说战事已终，政治犯一概赦免，两人才捡回性命。哥哥像是一时把我忘尽了，也不大说话；可我听见他告诉母亲：有一天，望见兵士上山来，师父把他藏进一只书箱，盖上经卷，却敞着箱盖，自己坐在箱边，装作整理文书。哥哥说，他听见粗暴的脚步声、翻倒的家具声；等一切静了，被人抱出来，才看见同他那只箱子并排放着的一列箱子，都叫长枪扎穿了。
第二天，母亲便把一家人聚齐，芳田寻了一处安身的地方。随后父亲也来了；一家人敛抑度日，从头再起。
「所以你瞧，花野，」姐姐说，「你外婆的一生，并不总是太平岁月。」
「那是了不起的一生，」花野的声音里带着敬畏，「了不起——又可怕。可是祖母大人*做过*大事！哦，她*做过*大事啊！」
我望着那副挺得笔直的轻盈身量，昂起的头，紧扣的双手。她真像母亲。离古老的骄傲和森严的教养，隔了一代；离将临的自由，也早了一代；却偏偏活在眼前这愁苦的时代——惶惑着，被人误解着，孤零零的！
姐姐同我们住了一秋，又住进冬天。她的来访，我永远双倍地感激；因为那几个礼拜，是母亲同我们在一起的最后的日子，而那是一段快乐的日子。她同姐姐追话当年，一谈半日，倒像一对朋友，不像母女；因为母女俩只差十四岁，而姐姐在许多地方，同母亲一样是旧派人。及至哀伤的时辰来到，姐姐在侧，于我是格外的安慰；旧礼数她样样烂熟，处处指点，那份体贴，旁人谁也给不了。
在那趟去寺院的伤心路上，我们跟在载着遗体的驾笼后头，看它在一身白衣的杠夫肩头摇荡；我的心思飘回很久以前的另一天——那天，我这个十一岁的孩子，走在吊丧亲友的行列里，一双小手紧紧捧着父亲名号的牌位。

===Original===

Over the narrow paths of the ricefields we wound after the chanting priests, while from the high, tossing baskets carried on long poles by their attendants showered hundreds of tiny pieces of the five-coloured sacred paper. They filled the air with clouds of soft colours, floating and mingling as they drifted downward to settle gently on the straw hats and white robes of the mourners.
Now, everything was different. Even the honours we show our dead must bow to the world’s changes, and the services for Mother were the simplest possible to be in accord with her former rank. But she had requested that, in addition to the rites for herself, there should be held the ceremony for “The Nameless.”
My noble, loyal mother! True to her wifehood and to her husband’s family, even as she was entering the door of death she had remembered poor Kikuno, for whom no prayer was ever offered except in this lonely service. And since Brother, the head of the family, was a Christian, she knew it would never again be observed.
All through the calm and peaceful intoning, beneath which sounded the rhythmic throb of the wooden drum, my mind was on my gentle mother’s life of unswerving duty to her highest belief, and I wondered what power had kept her so strong and true. Then, dully, I became aware that the soft music was melting into a weird and mournful chanting that carried my thought to the hopeless soul who had lost the way to Heaven because of her great sin. And so, once more, the descendants of the name she had dishonoured, sat, lowly bowed, while the priests chanted the prayer that help be given to guide the wanderer on her lonely path.
When we came to the pause in the music where the high priest chants the arrival of the dead at the gates of Heaven to present the plea for mercy, the priests raised their cymbals above their heads, and, bringing them slowly together, clashed a long, quivering accompaniment to the soft, muffled beat of the wooden drum. Before my misty eyes the swinging sleeves made a blur of purple, scarlet, and gold, and, listening to the wailing and pleading prayer that had for almost three hundred years winged its way through the curling incense, I wondered if the long-remembering God of Vengeance would not, if only in pity for Mother’s unselfish faithfulness, grant this last plea for the erring one of long ago.

===Chinese===

我们跟在诵经的僧人后头，沿稻田的窄径蜿蜒而行；僧人的随从扛着长竿，竿头挑着高高的、颠动的筐篮，里面撒下成百上千片五彩的纸——那是敬神的纸。软软的颜色漫天飞舞，飘飘扬扬，交错着、旋舞着，轻轻落在戴斗笠、穿白衣的送葬人头上身上。
如今，样样都不同了。连我们敬奉亡人的礼数，也得向世道的变化低头；母亲的丧仪，是同她旧日身份相称的最简的规格。只是她自己留了话：除给她行的法事之外，还要为「无名氏」行那一道仪轨。
我高贵忠贞的母亲啊！直到跨进死亡之门的时辰，她还守着为妻之道、守着丈夫的家族，还惦念着可怜的菊野——除了这一场孤零零的法事，再没有人给她诵过一句经。她也知道，哥哥既是一家之主，又是个基督徒，这道仪轨，从此不会再行了。
诵经声安详平和，底下衬着木鱼一板一眼的笃笃声；这声音里，我心里想的，全是我温柔的母亲一生对她至高信仰坚定不移的奉行，我想不明白，是什么力量撑得她这样坚强、这样忠贞。随后，蒙蒙眬眬地，我觉出那柔缓的经乐，正化入一段凄厉哀婉的诵唱——它把我的思绪，引向那个因大罪迷失了登天之路、再无指望的灵魂。于是，又一次，被她玷污了的那姓氏的子孙，低低俯首而坐；众僧诵经，祈求援引，好指引那蹒跽独行的迷途者。
唱到乐声的间歇——由高僧诵唱亡人抵达天门、代为乞怜的一段——众僧把铜钹举过头顶，缓缓合拢，铙的一声长颤，应和着木鱼幽幽的闷响。在我迷蒙的眼前，舞动的衣袖化作一片紫的、红的、金的模糊光影；我听着这哀告乞怜的经文——三百年来，它一直循着裊裊的香烟上达天听——心里想：那位长于记恨的复仇之神，纵然只怜惜母亲这一片无私的忠贞，可肯不肯，应允这最后一愿，宽宥那很久以前失足的人？

===Original===

At the temple door I made my last bow to my mother’s dear body, and, with a heavy ache in my heart, stood watching the swaying *kago* with its curving roof and gilded lotus blossoms as it disappeared at a turn in the road leading to the cremation grounds. Then we returned to the lonely home, and for forty-nine days the candles burned and the incense curled its fragrant way through the carvings of the little whitewood shrine. On the last night I knelt in my mother’s old place and breathed a Christian prayer to the God who understands. Then I slowly closed the gilded doors upon my prayer, believing sincerely that my mother’s journey had ended in peace; and that, wherever she was or whatever she might be doing, she was faithfully taking her part in God’s great plan.
My minister was sorely troubled that I should have observed these last Buddhist rites﻿—unnecessary after my mother had passed beyond the knowledge or the hurt of their neglect. I told him that, had I died even one day after I became a Christian, my mother would have been faithful, to the minutest detail, in giving me the Christian burial that she believed would satisfy my heart; and that I was my mother’s daughter. Influence? Yes. The influence of loyalty, sympathy, understanding; all of which are characteristics of Our Father﻿—hers and mine.

===Chinese===

在寺门口，我朝母亲可爱的遗体行了最后一拜；然后怀着胸口沉沉的痛，立在那里，望着那顶驾笼——弯弯的笼顶，饰着金莲——摇摇荡荡，转过通往火葬场的路口，不见了。随后我们回到冷清清的家。四十九天里，烛火长明，香烟裊裊，从那小白木神龛的雕花间逸出。最后一夜，我跪在母亲从前跪的位置上，向那位洞达一切的上帝，做了一次基督徒的祷告。然后，我缓缓合上龛门，把我的祷告合在里面；我诚心相信，母亲的旅程已安然抵埠；也相信，无论她如今在哪里、在做什么，她都在忠实地尽她的一份，成全上帝的大计划。
我们的牧师很是难过，怪我不该行这最后的佛家丧仪——母亲既已故去，行与不行，她既无从知晓，也无可伤害。我对他说：哪怕我做了基督徒的第二天便死去，母亲也会一丝不苟，为我行她相信能安我心怀的基督教葬礼；我呢，是我母亲的女儿。感化么？是的。忠贞的感化，体恤的感化，洞达的感化——这一切，都是我们天父的品性——她的天父，也是我的。

## XXX The White Cow / 三十 白牛
===Original===

When Hanano was fifteen, the family council brought up the subject I had been most dreading. According to Japanese custom, when there are only daughters in a family, a son is adopted, who takes the family name and marries the eldest daughter. Thus the name is perpetuated. The question of the selection of a son for me, I had dealt with in as tactful a manner as possible, but after having refused two or three offers, I saw that I was expected to give a positive decision soon.
It is never wise for a Japanese woman, if she wishes to retain a position of influence and dignity, to say much on any subject. Actions, not words, are her most successful means of expression; but the time came when I saw that I must speak. With a letter of wise suggestions from my ever-faithful American mother in my hand, I went before the council and asked to be allowed to take the children back to my former home for a few years more of study. This request caused excited discussions; but I now had friends in the council, both of Matsuo’s family and of my own, and my past faithful adherence to their wishes brought a glorious reward. Again my petition was granted, and, with my heart weighted with gratitude and my soul singing with joy, I began my preparations to return to America.
With Chiyo, our going was a question of whether to be glad or sad. Leaving her little friends and her loved school, to go back to a vagueness in which only “Grandma” stood out vividly was a serious thing. But Hanano’s joy was profound. She was quiet, but busy every moment, going about with light, quick steps and singing softly all the while; and I never glanced at her that I did not meet a bright smile. Many times during the weeks of preparation, as I watched her happy face, the thought came to me that if﻿—if﻿—such a cruel thing could happen as that she would never reach the land of her heart’s love, I should always be grateful, anyway, for the quiet, overflowing joy of this season of hope. Nothing could ever take away that happy memory.
The busy weeks flew by, and at last there came a morning when the children, who had been turning down their fingers to count the days, gleefully announced that only two widespread hands were left. Ten days! We were almost ready, but however well one may plan, there always seem to be some unfinished things that are pushed away until the last crowded days.

===Chinese===

花野十五岁那年，亲族会议提起了一桩我最怕的话题。照日本的习俗，家里若只有女儿，便抱养一个儿子，改姓本家，娶长女为妻；家名就此传续。替我择婿立嗣这件事，我一直极尽委婉地周旋着；可两三回辞让之后，我看出来，他们指望我快给个痛快的答复了。日本女人若想保住自己有分量、有体面的位置，在任何事上多话都是不聪明的。她的言语，以行而不以言为上；可如今时候到了，我明白我必须开口。我手里拿着我那位永远忠实的美国妈妈的信——满纸明智的指点——到亲族会议跟前，请求准我带孩子们回我从前的家，再求几年学。这请求激起了一场热闹的争论；可如今会议里已有我的朋友——松雄家的，稻垣家的，都有——而我往日对他们意愿的恭谨遵行，也得了丰厚的报偿。我的呈请又蒙了恩准。我心头压满感激，灵魂欢唱，着手张罗回美国去了。对千代来说，走，是欢喜还是愁，很难说。撇下小朋友和心爱的学堂，回到一片模糊的旧影里——那影子里只有「奶奶」还鲜亮亮地立着——是件了不得的事。花野却是欢喜透了的。她不多话，却一刻不闲，脚步轻轻快快，嘴里一直低低唱着；我每回望她，迎面总是一脸明亮的笑。张罗行装的那几个星期里，多少次，望着她那张快活的脸，我暗想：万一——万一——真有那样残忍的事，她终究到不了那片心底热爱的国土，那么无论如何，这一段满怀盼望、静静满溢的欢喜，我也永远感激。谁也夺不走那一段幸福的记忆。忙碌的几个星期飞也似的过去，末了有一天早晨，一直扳着指头数日子的孩子们欢天喜地地宣布：只剩两只摊开的手了。十天！我们差不多齐备了；可无论筹划得多周全，总有些收不了尾的零碎，一推再推，堆到最后那几天拥挤的日子里。

===Original===

The children had never been to Nagaoka. Many times I had planned to go, but life was full for us and something had always interfered. But I could not allow them to leave Japan without a visit to the place where their grandmother lay beside her husband among the graves of our ancestors; so early one spring morning we started.
How different was this trip from the one of years before which I took with my brother when on my way to school in Tokyo! Instead of a journey of several days, spent, sometimes perched upon a high wooden saddle, sometimes tucked snugly into a swinging *kago* and sometimes rolled and jolted along the rough path in a jinrikisha, this was only fourteen hours of comfortable riding on a brisk little narrow-gauge train, that wound its puffing way up the mountains, through twenty-six tunnels that represented some of the world’s finest engineering. Between these dashes of darkness were welcome glimpses of sunny hillsides terraced with ricefields, and a narrow, winding road that I remembered well. Just at twilight we found ourselves on the station platform of a busy town having a background of hills bristling with the skeleton towers of multitudinous oil wells. I had been told of these changes, but my slow mind had failed to realize how entirely *my* Nagaoka was a dream of the past.
I was glad that the children’s first sight of the town was in cherry-blossom time; for, even to me, the buildings looked smaller and the streets narrower than I had pictured them in my stories. Everything might have proved a disappointment to them had it not been for the glow and freshness that peeped over the plaster walls, glorified the temple yard, and showed from the tinted branches of the trees lining every street. There was a faint breeze on our first morning, and as our slow jinrikishas jogged along the strangely unfamiliar road to Chokoji the air was filled with fragrance from the pink, shell-like petals that were continually dropping, or lying in drifts on the sloping roofs of the snow-sheds which hung over the sidewalks.
“How we love these fruitless, beautiful trees﻿—emblem of our dying knighthood!” I thought with a sigh; and then I looked toward the hill where the castle used to stand, and an amused gleam of satisfaction came to me.

===Chinese===

孩子们从没到过长冈。多少回我打算去，可我们的日子总是满的，总有什么岔了开来。但我不能让她们不拜她们的外祖母安息之地就离开日本——她躺在丈夫身旁，卧在我们历代祖先的墓地里。于是一个初春的清晨，我们动身了。这一趟，同多年前我赴东京上学、跟哥哥同行的那一趟，多么不同啊！当年是一程好几天的路：有时高高地跨在木鞍上，有时稳稳地窝在摇摇晃晃的驾笼里，有时又在坎坷的小路上坐人力车，一路颠得滚来滚去；如今只需乘上一条轻快的小窄轨火车，舒舒服服14个钟头便到——它噗噗地喷着气，一路盘山而上，钻过26座隧道——那隧道，算得世界上一等一的工程。一道道黑暗之间，不时闪出叫人心怀欢畅的片断：向阳的山坡，层层稻田，还有一条窄窄弯弯的路——那条路我熟得很。暮色初合时，我们已站在一座热闹市镇的月台上；镇后一带山冈，密密地竖满了油井的井架，像森森的骨架。这些变迁我都听人说过；可我这迟钝的心，竟不曾真正明白：我的长冈，早已整个是前尘一梦了。孩子们初见这镇子正赶上樱花时节，我暗自庆幸；因为连我看来，这里的房子也比我故事里描画的小了，街道也窄了。若不是那一片娇艳新鲜——它从灰泥墙头探出来，把寺院场院照得辉煌，又从条条街上染了色的花枝间透出来——样样都难免叫她们失望呢。头一天早晨微微有风。我们的人力车慢慢悠悠，在一条陌生得叫人疑惑的路上往长兴寺去；空气里满是粉色贝壳似的花瓣的香气——花瓣不住地落，或成堆积在人行道上空雪棚的斜顶上。「我们多爱这不结实而美丽的树啊——它是我们那正在消逝的武士道的象征！」我叹着想。随后我朝古城堡旧日矜立的山头望去，一丝忍俊不禁的快意浮上心头。

===Original===

The old spirit of protection still dwelt amidst the ruins, for on the foundation rocks rose a huge fire-tower with its high platform and warning gong.
The old house was no more. I had hoped that Brother would decide to return, in time, and spend his old age in the home of his youth; for the gentle little wife that he had taken in late middle life had lived only long enough to bring an heir to the Inagaki family and then had drifted out of life as gently as she had lived in it during her scant score of quiet years. But all Brother’s interests were in a distant city amidst the progressive whir of factories and modern life, and he would listen to no plans beyond the education of his son.
So the gods of Utility and Commerce had taken charge, and all that were left of our worthless treasures were removed to Sister’s godown. Then Jiya and Ishi had gone to distant homes; and now, in place of the great rambling house with its sagging thatch and tender memories, stood the ugly foreign buildings of “The Normal School for Girls.” The old chestnut tree beneath which was Shiro’s grave, and the archery field, where so often I had seen Father and Mr. Toda, each with his right sleeve slipped from a bare shoulder, in a strenuous, but laughing, game of competition, was lost in a wide gravelled campus, where modern schoolgirls marched and drilled in pleated skirts and foreign shoes. Strange indeed it seemed﻿—and full of heartache for me! I realized that these changes pointed toward a future of usefulness and hope, and I would not have retarded them for the world; but all the quiet pleasures and picturesque life of the past had been merged into a present that looked cheap and sordid. It was hard for me, during my few days in the old town, to keep my memories of beautiful old customs and ideals from completely overshadowing the new, progressive path that I was striving to follow.
