When our duty of love and honour to the dear ones was over, we went with Sister, who had come to Nagaoka to meet us, to her home on a mountain a few hours’ jinrikisha ride distant. It was an odd little village where she lived. So narrow was the ledge upon which it stretched its one-street length that, from the valley below, it looked as if a toy town of plaster walls and thatched roofs had been pinned up against the green side of the mountain.

===Chinese===

那护卫的老精神还在废墟间守着：基石上立起了一座高高的望火楼，带瞭望台，吊警锣。老宅是没有了。我原指望哥哥终有一天肯回来，在他少年时代的家里养老；因为他中年续娶的那位温柔的小媳妇，只活得替稻垣家添了一个嗣子，便像她短短二十年安静岁月里过活时那样，轻轻地飘出了人世。可哥哥的心思全在一座遥远的城里，在那工厂与摩登生活的进步喧响中；除了儿子的教育，什么打算他也听不进。于是实利与商卖两位神接管了一切；我们家那些不值钱的宝贝，剩下的都搬进了姐姐家的土仓。随后阿爷和阿石也各去了远方的儿女家；如今，那座铺展的大宅——塌陷的茅顶、温柔的旧忆——原址上立着的，是「女子师范学校」丑陋的洋楼。那株老栗树——小白坟头的老栗树——和射箭场，也不见了；我多少回看见父亲和户田先生，各人褪下右袖露出一边肩膀，在那里又较劲又笑着地比箭——如今一并没入一片碎石铺地的大操场，摩登的女学生穿着褶裙、蹬着皮鞋，在那里列队操练。这光景实在古怪——也实在叫我心痛！我明白这些变迁通向一个有用而有希望的未来，我万万不愿阻拦它们；可旧日一切静好的乐趣、画意的生活，都并进了一个显得寒磣而俗气的现在。在老城的那几天，我极难叫我记忆里那些美好的旧风旧理想，不把我正奋力要走的那条新的、进步的路，整个罩没。对亲爱逝者的爱与敬礼一一行完，我们便随姐姐——她赶到长冈来接我们——到她山里的家去，人力车要走几个钟头。她住的是个古怪的小村。那道山肩窄窄的，村子便顺着一条街的长度铺在上面；从山谷底下望上去，就像一座灰泥墙、茅草顶的玩具小城，钉在青山的一侧。

===Original===

We left the valley, each with tandem pullers and a pusher behind. It was a steep climb up a winding path from which reached out, on either side, long, even lines of scrubby trees. Occasionally the men would stop and, bracing themselves, would rest the shafts against their hips and wipe their hot faces.
“It’s a breathless climb,” said one, as he smiled and pointed down into the valley, “but it’s worth the labour just to see yon terraces of green against the great brown rocks, and the sunny blue of the sky reflected brokenly in the rippling stream below.”
“*Hai*,” said another, “so it is. The city fellows see naught but level streets and dusty roofs peeping above walls or fences of wood. I pity them.”
Then on they went﻿—panting but content.
“What are all these low, twisted bushes with the gray trunks and so many little fresh buds?” asked Hanano, in one of these pauses.
“Mulberry trees,” replied Sister. “This is a silk-culture district, and the mountain is covered with cocoon villages. Almost every house here has wooden frames filled with trays of silkworms, and on a quiet day you can hear the rustle of their feeding as you walk along the street.”
That sounded interesting indeed to the children and as we went on, they shouted questions and exclamations to each other about silkworms and their mulberry-leaf diet, until the long climb ended in a short, steep pull and an abrupt turn into a broad street of low, wide-eaved houses. At the farther end stood the large house of the village﻿—Sister’s home. Its brownish-yellow thatch rose above a wall of rounded stones topped with a wooden fence, so like the one surrounding my old home in Nagaoka that the sight brought a shadow-ache of homesickness to my heart.
With cordial country manners, the servants had come out to the big wooden gateway, and as our jinrikishas rolled between the two lines of bowing figures, I caught murmurs of the familiar, old-fashioned greeting, “*O kaeri asobase!*”﻿—“Your return is welcome!”
The quiet house seemed very restful after our long, jolting ride, and the hot bath which is always ready in old-fashioned Japan for the expected visitor refreshed us wonderfully.

===Chinese===

我们离开山谷，每辆车两个车夫在前头拉，一个在后头推。山路陡陡地盘旋而上，路两旁伸出一行行齐整的矮树。偶尔车夫们停下，扎稳了马步，把车杠支在胯上，抹一把脸上的热汗。「这一趟爬得人喘不上气，」一个车夫笑着说，一面朝谷底指去，「可单为看一看这些也值了——看那大褐岩上一层层绿田，看晴天的蓝天碎碎地映在底下流转的溪水里。」「嗨，」另一个说，「就是这话。城里人只看得见平平板板的街，还有墙头篱笆上冒出来的灰扑扑的屋顶。我可怜他们。」说罢又走——气喘吁吁，心满意足。「这些矮矮的、盘盘曲曲的树，灰杆子，顶着那么多嫩芽，都是什么呀？」歇脚的当口，花野问。「桑树，」姐姐答道。「这一带是养蚕的地方，满山都是蚕茧村。这里几乎家家都有木架，架上一屉一屉的蚕；清净的日子里，你沿街走着，都听得见蚕吃桑叶的沙沙声。」这在孩子们听来实在有趣；一路走着，她们你一句我一句，嚷嚷着问蚕、嚷嚷着叹桑叶，直到长长的一程爬完，末了再一陡、一拐，转进一条宽街，两旁是低矮宽檐的人家。街那头，立着村里的大宅——姐姐的家。褐黄的茅顶高过一圈圆石垒的墙，墙头罩着木栅，同我长冈老家四围的那道那样相像，一望之下，我心底泛起一阵薄薄的、想家的酸痛。仆人们带着乡下人热诚的礼数，早迎到大木门外；人力车从两行躬身的人影中间碾过时，我听见一片熟稔的旧式请安：「O kaeri asobase！」——「您回来啦！」（欢迎归来！）长途颠簸之后，这安静的宅子叫人浑身松快；老派的日本，给将到的客人备下的热汤浴永远是现成的——泡过之后，我们个个焕然一新。

===Original===

The children and I had just returned to the living room, where, settling ourselves comfortably on soft cushions, we were gazing across the porch straight out into the blue sky, for the valley and the world were far below us, when two maids appeared bringing in the dainty little tables for luncheon.
“You’ll have to do without meat up here,” said Sister apologetically, as she came hurrying in. “We have only chickens and vegetables from my farm, and fish from the mountain streams. We cannot get meat or bread.”
“That matters nothing,” I replied. “The children are fond of fish and rice; and you know that I always liked everything green that grows. Don’t you remember the ‘white cow’?”
Sister laughed; and Hanano, always on the alert for a story, asked, “What is it about a white cow?”
So, as we ate, Sister told a story of my childhood which dated back so far that my knowledge of it was only what others told me.
“Your mother was not a very strong child,” she began, “yet she was never really sick, either. At that time many of the Nagaoka people, when they were puzzled and helpless about a really serious matter, used to consult the priestess of a small Shinto shrine just outside the town; and Honourable Grandmother asked Father to send for the holy woman. For two days before she came, Etsu-bo was not allowed to eat whale-meat soup, or onion, or any food with an odour; and she was carefully instructed to be extremely good, both in behaviour and in thought.
“Early on the important morning Ishi sprinkled her with cold water. Then she dressed her in her crest dress and took her to Honourable Grandmother’s room. All the family were there, and several women relatives. I remember how Etsu-bo looked as she toddled in, holding on to Mother’s hand. She bowed to everyone, and Mother seated her on the mat beside Honourable Grandmother, just a little in front of the rest of us. The tokonoma was covered with straw matting and decorated with all the sacred Shinto emblems. Of course the priestess was in the most honoured seat of all. She was dressed in pure white, and her black hair was hanging down her back, tied behind the shoulders with a band of rice-straw, from which dangled strips of white, zigzag-cut Shinto paper.

===Chinese===

孩子们和我刚回到起居间，在软垫上安顿舒坦，隔着游廊直望出去便是蓝天——因为山谷和人世都在我们脚底下老远——这时两个侍女进来，摆上精致的小方桌，预备午饭。「在山上，只好委屈你们不吃肉了，」姐姐急急走进来，含歉说道。「我们只有我田里出的鸡和菜，山溪里的鱼。肉和面包是弄不到的。」「这算得了什么，」我答道。「孩子们爱吃鱼和白饭；你也知道，凡地里长的青的，我样样爱。你不记得那头『白牛』了？」姐姐笑了；花野——她从来竖着耳朵等故事——忙问：「白牛是怎么回事？」于是我们边吃边听，姐姐讲了一个我幼年的故事——那故事年代久远，久得我自己对它也只剩下旁人的转述了。「你们这位妈妈小时候，身子算不得结实，」她开口道，「却也从不曾真生过病。那时候，长冈人逢到真正了不得的难事，没了主意，常去请教镇子外头一座小神社的女巫；祖母大人便求父亲打发人去请那位神女。神女来的前两天，津保不许喝鲸鱼汤，不许吃葱，不许沾一切有气味的东西；还细细教她务必乖到十足——行为上乖，心思上也乖。」「那个要紧日子的清早，阿石给她身上洒了凉水，替她穿上带家纹的礼服，领到祖母大人房里。全家都在，还有几位女亲眷。我记得津保牵着妈妈的手摇摇摆摆走进去的模样。她向人人行礼，妈妈让她坐在祖母大人身旁的席上，比我们都靠前一点点。壁龛里铺了稻草席，陈设着神道的一切神圣徽记。神女自然坐在最尊的座位上。她一身纯白，黑发披在背后，肩后用一根稻草绳束住，绳上垂着一条条白色的、剪成锯齿形的神纸。」

===Original===

When Mother and Etsu-bo were seated, the holy priestess prostrated herself two or three times; then she lifted from the tokonoma a whitewood rod that had on the end a bunch of long streamers of holy paper. She waved it above Etsu-bo’s head, murmuring some religious ritual. We all sat very quiet with bowed heads. After a moment of silence the priestess announced that she had just learned from the gods that Etsu-bo, in a previous existence, had been a small white cow used in drawing lumber for the building of a Shinto shrine on the top of a mountain. The message said that the little creature had toiled up the rocky path so patiently and faithfully day after day, and had lent its strength so willingly for the holy duty, that the gods had hastened the slow steps of transmigration and allowed the soul of the white cow to enter at once into the present life as a human being.
“Do you mean that my mamma was that white cow?” asked Hanano, with wide-open, astonished eyes; while Chiyo stopped eating and looked at me with alarm.
“Father didn’t believe the priestess,” added Sister, smiling; “nevertheless, to please Honourable Grandmother, he made a generous gift to the shrine. But he always said it was not so much a gift of gratitude to the gods as it was a token of satisfaction that he could now account for Etsu-bo’s exceeding fondness for all green vegetables and her little liking for fish. Now, whether it was really true or not, doesn’t matter any more than any other fairy story; but it’s lucky for you children that your honourable mother is a faithful and a patient puller, for she has climbed over a rocky path of obstacles and at last is ready to pull you all the way over to America.”
And she nodded merrily at the children as she served me another generous helping of bamboo shoots and greens.
A few days later one of Sister’s neighbours, whose son was a successful oil merchant in Tokyo, came to see us. Meeting her recalled to both Hanano and me a very amusing incident connected with a call from the son’s wife soon after we had gone to Tokyo to live. She was a lady of the new-rich aristocracy﻿—progressive, wealthy, and altogether “*highkara*”﻿—a recently coined word which implied the very essence of what was stylish and up-to-date.

===Chinese===

「等妈妈和津保坐定，神女俯身拜了两三拜；随后从壁龛里请起一根白木神杖，杖头垂着一束长长的神纸。她在津保头顶上摇着，口中念念有词。我们都垂着头，静静坐着。默了片刻，神女宣布：她刚从神明那里得知，津保的前身，是一头小白牛，曾在山顶营造神社时拉运木料。」「神谕说，那小小的生灵，日复一日，那样耐烦、那样尽心地爬那石头山路，那样甘愿出力尽那神圣的职分，神明便加快了轮回迟缓的脚步，允那白牛的灵魂即刻投胎，做了一回人。」「你是说，我妈妈就是那头白牛？」花野睁圆了眼睛问；千代也停了筷子，惊慌地望着我。「父亲倒是不信那位神女，」姐姐笑着接道，「不过为讨祖母大人欢喜，他还是给神社捐了厚礼。只是他总说，这与其说是谢神的礼，不如说是他放了心——如今总算弄明白了，津保为什么那样出奇地爱吃青菜，还稍微爱一点点鱼。至于这到底真不真，同旁的仙怪故事一样，不必较真；只是你们这两个孩子有福气，你们这位母亲大人，天生是头忠心耐劳的拉车的牛：她已经拉过了一路的石头坎，如今正要把你们一路拉到美国去呢。」说着，她朝孩子们快活地点点头，一面又给我添了满满一筷子的笋和青菜。过了几天，姐姐的一位邻居来看我们；她儿子在东京做油生意，很发了财。一见到她，我和花野都想起一件顶好笑的旧事——那和我们初到东京住定后，她儿媳妇来拜客的一桩事有关。那位夫人是新富的贵族——又开通，又有钱，整个儿一个「哈依卡拉（highkara）」——这是个新造的词，把时髦摩登的精髓全装在里头了。

===Original===

She was beautifully attired, in Japanese dress of course, for even the most progressive women had not reached the stage where European dress was worn on elegant occasions.
After a long, ceremonious bow and the usual complimentary inquiries regarding the health of family and relatives, and also a few tactful remarks in praise of the flowers arranged on the tokonoma, she leaned forward and unwrapped a square of beautiful crêpe exquisitely dyed and embroidered. It is an age-old Japanese custom, when calling upon a friend, to take a gift, and my guest lifted out and presented, modestly but with evident pride, a large imported paper box on which was printed in fancy English letters:

> A Foreign Delicacy Possessing the Fragrance of Flowers!
> Used by Ladies and Gentlemen

in the

Cultured Society of Europe and America

===Chinese===

她穿得漂亮极了——自然是和服；因为便是最开通的女人，也还没开通到在体面场合穿洋装的份上。长长的、彬彬有礼的一躬之后，照例把阖家上下的安康问了一遍，又得体地夸了几句壁龛里的插花；然后她欠身向前，解开一方绫纱——染绣得精致极了。拜友携礼，是日本千年的老规矩。我这位客人捧出一只大号的进口纸盒来相赠——态度谦和，得意却掩不住——盒子上花体英文印着：

> A Foreign Delicacy Possessing the Fragrance of Flowers!
> Used by Ladies and Gentlemen
> in the
> Cultured Society of Europe and America

> （一种飘着花香的异国珍味！欧美上流社会绅士淑女之选用）

===Original===

It was a large, wholesale package of ordinary chewing gum. The elaborate, ceremonious manner of my guest, every movement being in accordance with the strictest etiquette, made the unexpected appearance of that plebeian package a most incongruous and amusing thing. Yet this was a perfectly natural gift for her to make. It was not easy to choose a suitable present for a person who had lived for several years in America, and who was believed to be foreign in her tastes; so my guest had gone to a store where foreign things were sold and, with considerable care, had selected this as being an especially appropriate gift for me.
Hanano and Chiyo had been in the room when the box was presented. Chiyo looked with grave interest upon the foreign lettering. Of course she could not read it, but Hanano’s first careless glance, as we all bowed slightly in acknowledgment of the kindness, was followed instantly by another quick look; then, with a strange contortion of countenance, she bowed a deep “Excuse me” and slipped quickly from the room.
As soon as the caller had gone she hurried in to me.
“Oh, Mamma,” she cried, gleefully, “just to think that Nakayama Sama should select that gift for *you*! What would she think if she could only know how you scolded me that time in America when I came home from school chewing a piece of gum? And how you made me wash my mouth and told me that if I were in Japan Ishi would say that I looked like the Buddhist pictures of a starving soul in the Hell of Hunger!”
Sister was very much interested in this story.
“It seems a peculiar custom,” she said, “but it is not so harmful as the one from which originated our blackening the teeth.”
“What did start that, Sister?” I asked. “Several people in America asked me, and I could only tell them that ridiculous old story of the homely wife who stained her teeth by mistake, and it made her so beautiful that she won devotion from her husband and envy from all other wives.”
“There are many stories as absurd as that, about all our old customs,” said Sister. “When I made my first visit home with black teeth, I heard Father and Mr. Toda talking about our ancestors once having had a fashion of chewing something.

===Chinese===

那是一大包批发的、普普通通的口香糖。客人那一套繁文缛节、一举一动都合着最森严的礼数，越发衬得这平民包装的突然现身，不伦不类得可笑。可这份礼在她，实在是再自然不过。要给一位在美国住了好些年、据说口味也洋化了的夫人挑一件合适的礼物，本不容易；于是我的客人进了洋货店，仔仔细细，挑中了这一样，认定送我再合适不过。捧出盒子时，花野和千代都在屋里。千代对着那外国字，郑重其事地端详；她自然读不懂。花野呢——我们大家都微微躬身谢那番好意时，她先不过随意一瞥，随即又飞快看了一眼；跟着脸上做出一副说不出的怪相，深深一躬道声「失陪」，一溜烟出了屋子。客人一走，她急忙奔到我房里。「哎呀，妈妈，」她欢天喜地地喊，「想想吧，中山夫人（Nakayama Sama）竟会挑那样一件礼物送给您！她要是知道就好了——在美国那回，我嚼着口香糖从学校回来，您是怎么骂我的！您还逼我漱口，说我要是在日本，阿石准会说我活像佛画里饿鬼地狱里那个饿瘪了的魂灵！」姐姐听得津津有味。「这风俗看着古怪，」她说，「可还没有咱们染黑牙的那个来历邪性呢。」「那到底是怎么起的头呢，姐姐？」我问。「好些美国人问过我，我只会讲那个可笑的老故事：说有个丑媳妇误染了牙齿，反倒变得那样美，赢得了丈夫的宠爱，还招来天下媳妇的嫉妒。」「咱们的旧风俗，件件都有这样荒唐的传说，」姐姐说。「我头一回染了黑牙回门的时候，听见父亲同户田先生说起，咱们的祖先早年有一种嚼什么玩意儿的习气。

===Original===

But the story that Honourable Grandmother told me was this:
“Long ago, when everybody had white teeth, there lived a young wife whose jealous husband accused her of smiling to show her beautiful teeth. That day when cutting eggplant for dinner she took some thin peelings and put them over her teeth. The husband returned and, seeing how beautifully the purple colour contrasted with his wife’s olive skin and scarlet lips, angrily asked why she had decorated herself. She told him she had tried to cover her teeth so that they would not show. Recognizing her modest worth, the husband was jealous no longer. Thus, more attractive than ever, she became a model to imitate, and in time, the added beauty of blackened teeth came to be the emblem of a trusted and dutiful wife. That is the story that Honourable Grandmother was told when she married.”
What Sister had heard Father and Mr. Toda talking about was probably the theory which is considered the most reasonable explanation of our custom of blackening the teeth. It is an historical fact that the first conquerors of Japan, who no doubt came originally from the hot shores of Central Asia, planted betel orchards in the warm islands of South Japan where they first landed; but on account of difference in soil and climate it was almost impossible to make the trees grow. So, in a few years the habit of betel chewing became necessarily confined to those who represented wealth and rank. An ancient Imperial coach used by an Emperor who reigned more than a thousand years ago, and which is now in the Art Museum of Tokyo, was roofed with a thatch made of the husks of betel nuts. This speaks of the rarity of the betel trees at that time, for of course the Imperial cart was the most costly vehicle in the land.
When the time came that only people of the highest class had betel-stained teeth, imitations became the fashion and substitutes were found. During the Middle Ages, long after the nuts were extinct in Japan, both men and women of high rank blackened their teeth with a powder made from a wild nut from the mountains. The Imperial courtiers kept up this custom to 1868. At that time even Meiji Tenno, the Emperor of the Restoration, had blackened teeth. The samurai never stained their teeth. They took pride in scorning any fashion that spoke more of luxury and ease than of strength and power of arms.

===Chinese===

可祖母大人给我讲的，是这个：
「很久以前，人人都还是白牙的时候，有个年轻的媳妇，丈夫善妒，怪她一笑就露出漂亮的牙齿来炫人。那天她切茄子备晚饭，便揭了几片薄薄的茄皮，贴在牙上。丈夫回来，见那紫色同媳妇的蜜色皮肤、朱红嘴唇衬得那样好看，怒问她为什么打扮。她说她是想把牙盖上，不叫它露出来。丈夫这才识得她的贤淑，从此不再妒了。这一来，她比先前更动人，人人争相效法；久而久之，染黑了的牙齿添出的美，便成了可信、贤惠的媳妇的标志。祖母大人出嫁时，听的就是这个故事。」
姐姐听见父亲和户田先生谈的，多半是那个如今公认最讲得通的、解释咱们染黑牙风俗的说法。史有明文：最先征服日本的那批人——多半是从中亚炎热的海岸过来的——在他们初登的南方温暖诸岛上，种过蕠叶园；只是水土不服，那树几乎栽不活。于是不出几年，嚼槟榔的习惯，便只好局限在代表财富与身份的人们中间了。东京艺术博物馆里如今存着一驾御辇，是一千多年前在位的一位天皇用过的，车顶竟是用槟榔壳编的茅草盖的。这足见当时槟榔树何等珍稀——御辇嘛，自然要是天下最贵重的车驾。到了中世，槟榔果在日本绝迹已久，上流人家的男女便用一种山里野果磨成的粉，把牙齿染黑。宫廷的贵胄把这风俗保持到1868年。那时节，连明治天皇——维新的天子——都染着黑牙呢。武士却从来不染牙。凡是不见筋骨刀兵、只见逸乐安闲的风尚，他们都引以为耻——这正是他们的骄傲。

===Original===

After the Restoration this emblem of vanity faded before the advancing light of Western life; but, suggestive as it was of artistic beauty and high-class leisure, it remained with the women, and all classes adopted it as the marriage emblem. From then on, they blackened their teeth on their wedding day and kept them black ever after.
The fashion is not an ugly one. When blackened every morning, the teeth look like polished ebony, and the gleam of shining black behind coral lips brings out the clear olive of the skin and looks as beautiful to Japanese eyes as did, to the eyes of a European, the dot of black courtplaster on the ivory skin of a maiden in the days of colonial America. The custom is now dying out, but it is still seen everywhere in rural districts. Even in large cities, almost all old ladies of very high rank and of very humble station still cling to the custom. The middle class of Japan always leads the way in progress.

===Chinese===

维新之后，这虚荣的标志在西方文明步步进逼的光前，渐渐褪了色；可它既带着艺术之美和上等闲雅的意味，便留在了女人身上，各阶层都把它取作婚姻的标志。从此，女人出嫁那天染黑牙齿，此后终生不褪。
这风尚其实不难看。每天早晨染过的牙，像乌木磨得锃亮；珊瑚色的唇后透出黑亮的光，衬得蜜色的皮肤越发莹洁。在日本人眼里，这同殖民地年代美国人的眼里，少女象牙色皮肤上贴的那一粒黑绸膏药点一样美。
这风俗如今正在消亡，可在乡下仍处处可见。便在大城市里，极高贵门第的老夫人和极寒微人家的老奶奶，也多半还守着它。日本的进步，向来是中产阶层领头。

## XXXI Worthless Treasures / 三十一 无用的珍宝
===Original===

We spent a happy week with Sister in the little silkworm village, and our visit was almost ended when one day she took us into her big godown, where the things brought from Nagaoka had been stored. The greater part of our ancient treasures were now only worthless burdens, but there were some things that I wanted the children to see; for, in the old days, they had been both useful and beautiful, and, to me, were still full of precious memories.
We passed through the heavy door, a foot thick of fireproof plaster, and entered a large room all four sides filled with shelves, most of them crowded full to the edge. There were rows and rows of high narrow boxes containing a whole library of soft-backed books. There were rows of still larger boxes holding small eating tables, and still others filled with dishes, trays, and all the reserve belongings of a prosperous household. There were long, slender boxes of roll pictures and many ornaments﻿—bronze vases, incense burners, and carvings of wood and ivory﻿—all neatly tied up in squares of cotton or silk, and placed within convenient reach, ready for the frequent changes necessary in a Japanese house.
Part of the floor was taken up with chests of drawers arranged in rows, back to back; and in corners stood tall candlesticks, screens, and various large articles of household use.
“Just look!” cried Hanano, gazing about her in astonishment. “I never saw so many things, at once, in all my life!”
“It’s like a store,” said Chiyo, “only everything is put away so nicely, and yet it’s all mixed up, too!”
“Don’t be critical of my housekeeping,” laughed Sister. “A well-filled godown is said to be the best museum of household belongings that is to be found in all Japan; and it ought to be, for it is the place where we keep everything that is not in immediate use. Things are put in and pulled out every day. I never knew of a godown that *looked* in order.”
But Sister’s godown really was in disorder, for in some half-filled shelves and in a wide space beyond the wooden steps leading to the floor above were gathered a lot of objects from our Nagaoka godowns, for which suitable places had not yet been found.

===Chinese===

我们在姐姐那座养蚕的小村里，快快活活地过了一礼拜；探访将尽的时候，有一天，她把我们领进她那座大土仓——从长冈搬来的东西，都收存在那里。我们的祖传之物，如今大半成了无用的累赘；可有些东西，我要让孩子们看一看；因为在旧日，它们既实用，又美观；而在我心里，它们至今盛满珍贵的记忆。
我们穿过厚重的仓门——一尺厚的防火灰泥——进了一间大屋，四面墙全是架子，大半都堆得齐着架沿。一排排又高又窄的箱子里，装着整整一库软皮书册。还有一排排更大的箱子，盛着小小的食案；另有一些，装满碟盏托盘，同一家兴旺人家的各样备用什物。细长的画轴箱、许多摆设——铜瓶、香炉、木雕牙雕——都方方正正包在棉布或绸帕里，放在顺手的地方，随时听用；日本人家里的陈设时常更换，这些是少不了的。
一部分地面上，背靠背排着一行行衣橱；墙角立着高烛台、屏风，同各色大件的家用物件。
「你们瞧！」花野四下里惊讶地张望着，喊道，「我长这么大，从来没一口气见过这么多东西！」
「像个铺子，」千代说，「只是样样都收得整整齐齐，可又全都混在一块儿！」
「不许挑剔我的持家，」姐姐笑了。「人家说，一座堆得满满当当的土仓，是全日本最好的家用什物博物馆；本来也该是——但凡眼前用不着的，我们都收在那儿。东西天天放进去、取出来，我可从来没见过哪座土仓*瞧着*是整齐的。」
可姐姐这座土仓实在是乱的：几格没堆满的架子上，还有通到上面一层去的木梯旁边一大片空地上，堆着一堆从长冈土仓里来的物件——还没给它们找到合适的去处。

===Original===

Among some high lantern stands wrapped in oil-paper, and a pile of boxes containing war banners, I saw the big, cumbersome palanquin that Father had used on his official trips to the capital in the years before the name was changed from Edo to Tokyo. The lacquer was dulled, the metal ornaments tarnished, and the brocade cushions faded; but Hanano thought it wondrously elegant. She crept inside, settled herself comfortably on the thick cushion, rested her elbow on the lacquer arm rest, and peeped into the toilet box in the silk pocket in front. Then she glanced at the misty reflection of her face in the metal hanging mirror and declared that Honourable Grandfather’s travelling coach was convenient and comfortable enough for a trip all the way to America.
As she climbed out I pushed at the padded top, but the hinges were rusted. It used to lift and swing back. Many a time, when on a hurried trip, Father had dressed while his carriers were trotting fast and Jiya running by his side to help him now and then.
“Here’s another palanquin﻿—prettier than yours, Hanano,” chirped Chiyo, from behind the stairs; “only this one hasn’t any doors.”
“*Maa! Maa!*” laughed Sister, going over to her. “This is not to ride in, little Chiyo. It’s for a swim!” and she lifted her into an enormous bathtub of red lacquer which from my earliest recollection had stood in a corner of our godown. We used it for holding the cocoons, until the maids were ready to put them on the spindle to twist the silk threads off the poor, little, cooked inhabitants. The tub was marred on the edge, but not chipped anywhere, for the lacquer was of olden time. It still held the deep softness of velvet, and the band of braided bamboo showed beneath the polished surface like water weeds in a clear stream. It must have been very old, for it had been brought into our family as a part of the wedding dowry of my three-times-great-grandmother, the daughter of Yodo daimyo, Inaba-no-kami.
“Climb out, Chiyo! Climb out and come here!” called Hanano. “I’ve found a wooden stovepipe hat﻿—only,” she added, peering into it, “it has a funny inside.”
She was standing in a shadowy corner where a number of miscellaneous articles were gathered on a crowded shelf, and had just lifted the tall cover from a shallow bucket of whitewood, the bottom having in its centre, rising sharp and strong, a short hardwood spike.

===Chinese===

在几座油纸包着的高灯笼架中间，在一摞盛军旗的箱子旁边，我看见父亲那顶又大又笨的坐轿——江户还没改叫东京的那些年，他进京公干，坐的就是它。漆色暗了，金属饰件失了光，锦缎的座褥也褪了色；可花野觉得它雅致极了。她钻进去，舒舒服服地在厚座褥上坐定，胳膊肘支在漆的扶手上，朝前头绸袋里的梳妆匣里张望；又对着悬着的铜镜里自己模糊的影子照了照，宣称祖父大人这辆旅行车，又便当又舒服，一路坐到美国去都尽够了。
她往外爬的时候，我推了推那带衬垫的轿顶，铰链已经锈死了。从前它是可以掀开、向后摇起的。多少回赶路的时候，轿夫们疾步小跑，阿爷在一旁跟着，时时搭手，父亲就在轿里更衣。
「这儿还有一顶轿子——比你的漂亮，花野，」千代在楼梯后头尖声喊，「只是这顶没有门。」
「哎呀呀！哎呀呀！」姐姐笑着走过去。「这不是坐的，小千代。这是游泳用的！」说着，把她抱进一只红漆的大浴桶——打我记事的时候起，它就立在我们家土仓的一个角落里。从前我们拿它盛蚕茧，等女仆们把茧子放上缫车，从那些可怜的、蒸熟了的小东西身上抽出丝来。桶沿有了磨痕，却处处不见磕碰，因为是古时的漆工。它至今还存着一层天鹅绒似的软润；编竹的箍圈沉在光润的漆面底下，像清溪里的水草。它一定非常古老了：它是随我曾曾曾祖母的嫁妆进的家门——她是淀城大名、因幡守（Inaba-no-kami）的女儿。
「快出来，千代！出来，到这儿来！」花野喊。「我找着一顶木头高顶帽——只是，」她朝帽里蹟了蹟，补了一句，「里头有点古怪。」
她正站在一个背阴的角落，架子上挤挤挨挨堆着各色杂件；她刚把一只高高的盖子从一只白木浅桶上揭开——桶底正当中，硬硬地、尖尖地，竖着一截短短的硬木刺。

===Original===

It was Father’s head-bucket that always had been kept in the closed shelf-closet above our parlour tokonoma.
“Let us go upstairs now,” I said quickly. “Sister, won’t you show the children your wedding cap of silk floss? They have never seen an old-fashioned wedding, where the bride’s cap comes down to the chin.”
I hurried them up the narrow stairs to the room above. I did not want to explain to the children the use of the head-bucket. Their modern, practical education held nothing that would enable them to understand the deep sentiment of honour which has inspired many an ancient samurai, who, when guilty of some unlawful act, has chosen to die an honourable death by his own hand, rather than bring upon his family the disgrace of a public execution. In such a case the head-bucket, one of which every samurai house possessed, was used to carry to court the proof that the law had been obeyed. After being identified by the authorities, the head was returned, with respectful ceremonies, to the family; and the dead samurai, his crime now fully expiated, was buried with honour.
Of course, the gruesome mission of our head-bucket had never been fulfilled. Its only duty had been the occasional holding of a coil of hemp when Honourable Grandmother or Ishi was twisting it ready to spin. It was as convenient for that purpose as a flax-box. Indeed, the two looked so much alike that no bride was ever allowed to have a flax-box, although in those days all other spinning implements were considered essential to every wedding dowry.
The upstairs room of Sister’s godown was lighted by narrow, iron-barred windows set deep in the thick plaster wall. The shutters, which were really heavy plaster doors, were open, and a pleasant breeze was blowing through the room, making it cool and airy. Against the walls were chests of drawers and great wooden boxes having metal bands, on some of which I saw the Inagaki crest. I could readily guess what Sister’s chests contained, for her large house was well stocked with all the requirements of a country home.

===Chinese===

那是父亲的首桶，一直收在我们客厅壁龛上首那口闭着的柜格里。
「咱们这就上楼吧，」我急忙说。「姐姐，把你的丝绵婚礼帽给孩子们看看，好不好？旧式的婚礼她们还没见识过——新娘的帽子，是一直罩到下巴的。」
我催着她们上了窄楼梯，进了上面那间屋子。我不想过孩子们知道首桶的用处。她们那套新式的、讲实用的教育里，没有一样东西，能教她们懂得那种荣誉的深情——多少古代的武士，正是怀着这份深情，在犯了不法之事后，宁肯自裁，求一个体面的死，也不肯叫家族蒙受公开处刑的耻辱。逢到那样的情形，便用首桶——武士人家家家都有一只——把「王法已遵」的凭据送到官府。经官验明之后，那颗头颅便依着恭敬的仪节，发还家人；死去的武士，罪愆已全额抵偿，体体面面地下葬。
当然，我们家这只首桶，那桩怕人的差使从不曾当过。它唯一的用处，是偶尔搁一搁祖母大人或阿石捻好待纺的麻线。当麻线箱用，它再顺手不过。说实在的，这两样东西像极了，所以做新娘的从来不许陪嫁麻线箱——尽管在那些年月，别的纺绩家什，样样都算嫁妆里少不得的。
姐姐土仓楼上那间，靠厚厚的灰泥墙深处开的窄窗取光，窗上安着铁条。窗板其实是厚重的灰泥门，敞着；一阵好风穿堂而过，屋里又凉快又通透。靠墙立着衣橱和包着铁箍的大木箱，有几只箱上，我看见稻垣家的家纹。姐姐的箱子里装着什么，我一猜便着：她那座大宅，乡下人家该有的一样不缺。

===Original===

There were padded-silk comforts, round pillows for men and little lacquer box-pillows for women, large mosquito nets made to swing by short cords from the corners of the ceiling, thus enclosing the entire room, and cushions of every kind﻿—soft, thick ones of heavy silk for winter; thin ones of woven grass for summer, braided bamboo for the porch, woven rope for the kitchen, some round, some square, some plain, and some elaborately dyed in patterns﻿—for cushions were our chairs, and every house had to have a supply always on hand.
“This holds my ‘treasure dresses,’ ” said Sister, waving her hand toward a low chest of drawers. “The clothes that I wear I keep downstairs within easy reach; but some of these have been in the family for more than two hundred years.”
She took out an elaborately embroidered trained garment with a scarlet lining and heavily padded hem﻿—a dress of ceremony, worn, even in ancient times, only on state occasions. It looked fresh and almost new, for Japanese women are careful housekeepers, and probably this gown had been shaken out and examined on every airing-day since it was first used by the ancestor of long ago.
“It looks just like the splendid dresses we saw in that play at the Tokyo theatre, doesn’t it?” said Hanano.
And indeed it did. For only on the stage were these gorgeous costumes to be seen in modern life.
The next drawer held Sister’s wedding dresses﻿—seven of them. There was the soft, white linen, emblem of death to her own home, the scarlet silk, emblem of birth into her husband’s family, and the five other elaborately embroidered gowns bearing her husband’s crest and the marriage emblems of pine, bamboo, and plum.
“Here is the wedding cap you asked to see,” said Sister, presently, unfolding something that looked like a great satiny mushroom. It was of exquisite pressed silk floss and made to fit rather close over the head and shoulders. It looked like a thick, shining veil.
“Oh, isn’t it pretty?” cried Chiyo, delighted. “Put it on, Hanano, and let’s see how you look!”
I gave a half-frightened gasp, and was glad when Hanano, with a slow smile, shook her head. I don’t know why the child refused. Perhaps the soft whiteness of the snowy floss suggested in some vague way the white mourning clothes we had worn at Mother’s funeral.

===Chinese===

有丝绵的厚被，男人们用的圆枕，女人们用的小漆盒枕，罩住整间屋的大蚊帐——用短短的绳从天花板四角吊起；还有各式各样的坐垫——冬天用厚厚的重丝软垫；夏天用细编的草垫，游廊用编竹的，厨房用绳编的；有圆的，有方的，有素净的，也有染出繁复花样的——因为坐垫就是我们的椅子，家家都得常年备着一堆。
「这一橱装的是我的『礼服衣裳』，」姐姐朝一口矮衣橱摆了摆手。「我身上穿的，都放在楼下顺手的地方；可这里头有几件，在咱家传了两百多年了。」
她取出一件绣工繁复、带着长裾的衣裳——猩红的衬里，厚厚的垫边——一件礼仪之服，便在古时，也只在隆重的场合才穿。它簇新挺括，像没上过身；日本女人持家仔细，打很久以前那位老祖宗头一回穿它起，每逢晾晒的日子，这件衣裳想必都抖开来看过。
「它同我们在东京戏园里那出戏里的华丽衣裳一模一样，对不对？」花野说。
确实一模一样。在现代生活里，这样华美的服装，只有台上才见得着了。
下一格抽屉里，是姐姐的婚礼衣裳——一共七件。有软软的白麻——辞别娘家、归于死别的表记；有猩红的绸——生于夫家的表记；还有五件绣工繁复的袍子，缀着丈夫的家纹，和松、竹、梅的婚嫁纹样。
「这就是你们要看的婚礼帽，」姐姐说着，抖开一件东西——像一朵巨大的缎子蘑菇。那是上好的压制丝绵做的，罩下来，严严地扣住头和肩，看着像一厚层发亮的面纱。
「哦，多好看呀！」千代欢喜地叫，「戴上试试，花野，看看你什么样！」
我半惊半喘地吸了口气；幸而花野慢吞吞地笑着，摇了摇头。我不知道这孩子为什么不肯。也许那雪白丝绵的软白，隐隐叫她想起我们在母亲葬礼上穿的丧服。

===Original===

While there was no definite superstition regarding the wearing of wedding garments after the ceremony, still, it was never done. They were laid away﻿—to wait. Both Honourable Grandmother and my mother wore the wedding dress beneath the death-robe when they were ready for the last journey.
The very next chest﻿—just as marriage and death go hand in hand as the two most important ceremonies in Japanese life﻿—held articles for the funeral. This chest was one of those from my home and was about half filled with a disordered array of ceremonious uniforms for the men who carried the tall lanterns, the bamboo dove cage, and the heavy death *kago*. These were all made of linen, since no silk was ever used at a funeral. There were also pleated skirts and stiff shoulder garments for retainers with no family crest, white-banded servant kimonos, boxes of knee bands, pilgrim sandals, and countless small articles essential for the various attendants in the elaborate procession. I could remember when that chest contained everything requisite for a samurai funeral except the wide straw hats that shade the sorrowing faces from the Sun goddess. Those had to be fresh and new for each occasion. The house of every high official always had these things in readiness, for death often comes without warning, and Japanese rules for ceremonious occasions were strict and unvarying.
“There!” said Sister, as she closed the lid of the chest and pushed the metal bar through the triple clasp, “the usefulness of these things belongs with their glory﻿—to the past. Sometimes I cut up a garment to get linen binding for a worn-out mat, and occasionally, when a workman breaks his sandal cord, I present him with a pair of sandals from this chest; but the things go slowly﻿—slowly.”
“But *this*,” she added, gently tapping a drawer in a fresh whitewood chest, “belongs to the future. It will be used some day.”
“What is it?” I asked.
“My death-robe.”
“Oh, Sister,” I said earnestly, “please show it to the children. They saw Mother’s, of course, but I had no chance to explain the meaning.”
She opened the drawer and lifted out her shroud. We all sat very quiet, for as it was folded it looked exactly like the one we had placed on Mother. It was made of soft white linen, and instead of a sash, had a narrow band like that of a baby’s first dress, for the belief was that we enter the next world as an infant.

===Chinese===

婚服穿过之后再穿，虽没有什么明文的忌讳，可从来没有人那样做。它们被收起来——等着。祖母大人和我母亲，收拾停当踏上最后一程的时候，贴身穿着的，都是当年的婚服。
再过去一口橱——正如婚与死在日本人一生里并肩而行，是两桩顶要紧的大礼——装的是丧仪的东西。这口橱是我娘家的，半橱散乱地堆着各种仪制的制服，给那些执高灯笼的、执竹鸽笼的、抬沉重的灵驾笼的男人们穿的。都是麻布的——丧事里从不用丝。还有给无家纹家臣穿的褶裙和硬肩衣，缀白带的仆人和服，一盒盒扎膝的布带，行脚的草鞋，同许许多多零星小物——都是那一长列仪仗里各色人等少不了的。我记得这口橱从前应有尽有，办一场武士的丧事，只差遮太阳女神、护住哀容的宽草帽——那个须得每次现做、用新的。凡高等官宦人家，这些东西永远备着；因为死亡常常不打招呼就来，而日本人的仪制，又严又板，从无变通。
「好啦！」姐姐合上橱门，把铁闩推过三道锁鼻。「这些东西的用处，连同它们的荣光——都归于过去了。有时我铰一件衣裳，取麻布条给磨破的席子滚边；偶尔哪个做工的磨断了草鞋绳，我便从这橱里送他一双草鞋。可这些东西，走得慢——慢慢地往外走呢。」
「可*这一格*，」她接着说，轻轻拍着一口新白木橱的抽屉，「是留给将来的。总有一天用得着。」
「是什么？」我问。
「我的寿衣。」
「哦，姐姐，」我恳切地说，「给孩子们看看吧。母亲的她们见过；可我没能讲给她们这里头的意思。」
她拉开抽屉，取出她的裹尸衣来。我们都静静地坐着；它那样叠着，同我们放在母亲身上的那件，一模一样。软软的白麻，不用腰带，却缀着一条窄窄的带子，同婴儿的初衣一样——因为人们相信，我们进入来世的时候，是要做婴儿的。

===Original===

The robe was almost covered with texts from the Buddhist scriptures, which had been written by famous priests at various times. A blank strip in front showed that it was not yet finished. Beside the robe lay a small white bag intended to be placed around the neck. It would contain, when all was ready for Sister’s last journey, a tiny package of her baby hair, shaved off at the christening ceremonies when she was eight days old, the dried navel cord, her cut widow-hair, a six-*rin* coin to pay the ferryman, a death rosary of white wooden beads, and a sacred tablet called “The Heavenly Pass.”
While Sister was re-folding the robe she glanced up at the grave faces of the children and broke into a merry laugh.
“Why so sad, thou solemn-faced ones?” she cried. “Would it not be a disgrace should I receive a telegram to go home and have no suitable dress for the journey?”
“Yes, children,” I added, “it is as natural and commonplace for everyone in Japan to be ready for the last journey as it is in America to have a trunk in the house.”
“Come over this way,” said Sister, leading us to the other side of the room. “Here is something that belongs to you, Etsu-bo. You had better take charge of it.”
She pulled out a narrow drawer. Within, wrapped in purple crêpe on which was the Inagaki crest, lay a slender parcel about a foot long. My heart gave a bound. It was one of our three family treasures﻿—the *saihai* used by Tokugawa Ieyasu, and presented by him to my ancestor on the battlefield of Sekigahara.
Reverently I lifted the precious thing to my forehead. Then, bidding the children sit with bowed heads, I slowly unwrapped the square of crêpe, disclosing a short, thick rod of lacquered wood, having on one end a silk cord for a wrist loop and on the other a bronze chain-clasp that held a bunch of soft, tough paper cut in strips.
We all sat very quiet while Sister told the children of the brave ancestor who, in a time of peril, saved the life of his great overlord; and how Ieyasu, in gracious remembrance, presented him with his own bloodstained coat, his wonderful Masamune sword, and this rod which he used in guiding his followers on the battlefield. “And,” concluded Sister, “all three are still kept in the Inagaki family as sacred treasures.”
“It looks like just a plain wooden stick, doesn’t it?” whispered Chiyo to Hanano.
“So it is,” said Sister.

===Chinese===

那件衣裳上，几乎写满了佛经的经文，是历代高僧陆续写上去的。胸前空着一条，写着还没写完。衣裳旁边搁一只小小的白布袋，预备挂在颈上。等到姐姐最后一程的一切齐备，那袋子里将装着：一小包她的胎发——她生下八天、行命名礼的时候剃下来的；干了的脐带；寡居后剪下的头发；一枚付给渡子的六*厘*（rin）钱；一串白木珠的丧用念珠；还有一面唤作「天门通行」的神圣牌位。
姐姐重新叠着那件衣裳，一抬眼，看见孩子们肃穆的小脸，忽然嗤的一声笑出来。
「怎么这样愁眉苦脸的，你们这两张小苦瓜脸？」她喊道，「万一打来电报叫我回家，我倒没有一身合式的行装，那才叫丢人呢！」
「是啊，孩子们，」我附和道，「在日本，人人都为最后一程备着行装，就像在美国家家屋里都搁着一只旅行箱一样，自然得很，平常得很。」
「到这边来，」姐姐说着，领我们到屋子的另一头。「这儿有一样东西，是你的，津保。顶好由你自己收着。」
她拉开一格窄抽屉。里面，紫色的绉纱里包着一件细长的物件，约莫一尺来长，绉纱上印着稻垣家的家纹。我的心猛地一跳。那是我们家三件传家宝之一——德川家康用过的采配（saihai），是他在关原战场上，亲手赐给我家祖先的。
我恭恭敬敬把这件宝物举到额前。然后，叫孩子们垂头坐着，我缓缓解开那方绉纱，露出一段短短的、粗粗的漆木棒：一头拴着丝绳的腕环，另一头是一枚铜链卡，扣住一束又软又韧的、剪成细条的纸。
我们都静静坐着，听姐姐给孩子们讲那位勇敢的祖先：危难之际，救了他家伟大的主公一命；家康感念在心，把自己那件染血的外袍、他那柄稀世的正宗（Masamune）宝刀，连同这根在战场上指引他部下的采配，一并赐给了他。「这三件，」姐姐收尾道，「至今都当神圣的宝物，收在稻垣家里。」
「它瞧着就是一根光溜溜的木棍，对不对？」千代悄悄对花野说。
「可不就是，」姐姐说。

===Original===

“As plain as the most simple director rod used by any ancient general; for Ieyasu lived in the age when was written, ‘An ornamental scabbard signifies a dull blade.’ ”
“The pieces of paper are so yellow and ragged,” said Hanano. “Did they use to be white?”
“Yes,” I answered. “They are yellow because they are so old. And the reason the papers are ragged is because so many pieces have been torn off for people to eat.”
“*To eat!*” exclaimed both children, horrified.
I couldn’t help smiling as I explained that many people used to believe that because the *saihai* had been held in the hand of Ieyasu, the paper strips possessed the magic power of healing. I have heard my mother say that sick people often came from long distances just to beg for a bit of the paper to roll into a pellet and swallow as a cure. Father always laughed, but he told Mother to give the paper, saying that it was less harmful than most medicine, and that belief alone frequently cures.
We were starting to go downstairs when I stopped beside a large whitewood box having the overlapping lid and the curved feet of a temple book chest. It stood on a platform raised a little above the floor. I had seen this box in my childhood, but never except on airing-days, and always it had the sacred Shinto rope around it. With some hesitation I called Sister to come back.
“I am very bold,” I said, “but would you mind if I ask you to open the *kiri*-wood box? Our feelings have changed since the old days, and I would so like for the children﻿—”
“Etsu-bo, you ask to gaze upon sacred things﻿—” Sister began hastily; then, stopping abruptly, she shrugged her shoulders. “After all, women’s eyes have already looked upon it,” she added a little bitterly; “the new order of things has done much to take the spirit of reverence from us all.”
Then we, she at one end and I at the other, lifted off the lid just as Jiya and Yoshita in their ceremonial dresses used to do, long ago. I felt a little awestruck as we leaned over and looked within. Some of the sacred relics had been removed. The coat and sword of Ieyasu were in charge of another branch of the family, and Brother had taken the books of the Inagaki genealogy; but, before us, lying shroud-like in its pressed stillness, was a garment, once white, but now yellowed by time. A pointed cap and an ancient unfolding fan of thin wood lay on top.

===Chinese===

「同任何一位古代将军用的最朴素的指挥棒一样朴素；因为家康生在那样一个年代，那时人写：『剑鞘尚文饰，剑刃必钝。』」
「这些纸片都黄了、破了，」花野说，「它们从前是白的吗？」
「是白的，」我答。「黄，是因为年头太久了。破，是因为一条一条撕下来给人吃掉的太多了。」
「*吃掉*！」两个孩子骇然齐声叫起来。
我忍不住笑了，一面解释：从前许多人相信，这采配既然在德川家康手里握过，那些纸条便带着治病的法力。我听母亲说过，病人常常从很远的地方赶来，只求一小片纸，搓成一个小丸，吞下去治病。父亲总是笑，可他吩咐母亲把纸给人家；他说这总比大半的药要无害，何况单是「信」这个字，就常常治病。
我们正要下楼，我在一口大白木箱旁边停住了脚——箱盖叠沿，弯脚，像寺院里的经箱。它架在一只略高于地面的台座上。这口箱子我小时候见过，可只在晾晒的日子见，而且永远缠着神道的注连绳。我踌躇了一下，唤姐姐回来。
「我斗胆了，」我说，「可不可以请你开一开这口桐木（kiri）箱？如今的人心不比旧日了，我很想让孩子们——」
「津保，你要开眼看圣物——」姐姐急忙开口；又猛地顿住，耸了耸肩。「说到底，女人的眼睛已经看过了，」她略带苦涩地补了一句，「新时势把人人心里那点敬畏，剥掉得不少了。」
于是我们俩，她一头，我一头，像多年以前阿爷和芳田穿着礼服所做的那样，抬下了箱盖。我们俯身朝里望的时候，我心里微微生出几分敬畏。有几件圣物已经移走了：家康的外袍和宝刀，归了家族的另一支；哥哥取走了稻垣家的谱牒书册。可在我们眼前，静静平卧着，像一袭尸衣似的，是一件衣裳——从前是白的，如今叫岁月熏黄了。上面搁着一顶尖帽，和一柄古时的薄木折扇。

===Original===

It was the sacred robe which was used when the daimyo, or his representative, officiated as high priest in the temple dedicated to his ancestors and was believed to possess heavenly power. My grandmother had told me that once, when it was worn by my great-grandfather, a miracle had been performed beneath the shadow of its widespread sleeve.
We gazed only a moment, then the box was silently closed. Neither Sister nor I spoke of it again, but I knew that she felt, as I did, that we had been a little daring in lifting the lid of this box, which, in ancient days, was always kept in the holy room, even the entrance hall of which was never profaned by woman’s foot. I had grown away from my childhood faith in these things, but not entirely away from the influence of memory; and thoughts, beautiful and solemn, were crowding my mind when there came a sudden “bang!” from one of the heavy, swinging windows. They were always closed from the outside by a servant with a long pole, and evidently were being shut this time by someone who did not know that we were still there.
“*Maa! Maa!* It is late. Make haste, I inhospitably beg you,” laughed Sister; and we all scrambled down the narrow stairs and out of the door, hearing the windows bang one after another behind us, shutting the godown, with all its treasures, into darkness.

===Chinese===

那是一件神圣的法袍——从前，大名或他的代表，在供奉祖先的寺社里以大祭司的身份主祭时，便穿它；人们相信它带着上天的力量。我的祖母告诉过我：有一回，我的曾祖父穿上它，在它张开的衣袖荫下，行过一件神迹。
我们只望了一霎，箱子便无声地合上了。姐姐和我都没有再提起它；可我知道，她同我一样觉得：这一回开这口箱子的盖，我们是有点孟浪了。在古时，这口箱子永远供在圣洁的屋子里——那屋子的门厅，连女人的脚也不许踏污。我早已远离了童年时对这些东西的信仰，却还不曾全然走出记忆的力量；种种又美又庄严的念头正涌上心头，忽然「哐！」的一声，一扇沉重的吊窗撞上了。这些窗子，向来由仆人在外头用长杆关合；这一回关窗的，显然不知道我们还在里头。
「哎呀呀！哎呀呀！天晚了。快走快走，恕我不留客啦。」姐姐笑道；我们全都手脚并用，爬下窄楼梯，出了仓门，只听身后的窗子一扇接一扇地哐哐撞上，把整座土仓，连同它全部的珍宝，关进了黑暗里。

## XXXII The Black Ships / 三十二 黑船
===Original===

The night before we sailed my Tokyo uncle called, bringing with him a package of “friendship ribbons” for the children﻿—those frail, dainty, quivery strips that bind the hands of friends between deck and dock at the moment of starting﻿—and parting.
“I’ll hold a pink one for Toshiko and a blue one for Kuni San,” cried Chiyo, as the bright-coloured rolls tumbled out of the package, “and a white one for my teacher and a purple one for﻿—for *you*, Uncle Tosa! Two of the most beautiful for you, of any colour you choose!”
“I’ll hold a whole bunch of red and white ones for all Japan!” said Hanano. “Love, much love, and goodbye; for I’ll never come back. I love everybody here, but I’m going to stay forever with Grandma in ‘Home, Sweet Home,’ ” and she softly hummed the tune as she slipped away, her face full of light. Ah, how little she dreamed that in years to come she would return﻿—more than once﻿—and always with a heart full of double loyalty: half for the land of her birth and half for the land of love, where were husband, children, and home.
Hanano and Chiyo had gone to bed, and I was attending to the last scattered duties of the packing when Sudzu lifted a folded shawl to lay on top of the tray before closing a trunk.
“This is rather loose,” she said. “A cushion would exactly fit; but how ridiculous it would be to carry to a great country like America just an ordinary cushion that we sit on.”
She did not know that in the bottom of my trunk of greatest value was something which, until I had seen it in Sister’s godown, I had never dreamed could be anywhere except beside the familiar firebox in the room of Honourable Grandmother. It was a square, flat cushion of blue brocade, old and somewhat faded.
I was alone when I wrapped it for its long journey, and, as my hands passed over the silken flowers, my mind went back﻿—back to the day when a little black-haired girl in wooden clogs clattered through the big gateway and, hurrying her polite bows of greeting to the family, spread out before her grandmother, who was seated on this very cushion, a large, flat book.
“Honourable Grandmother,” she said, pointing to a coloured map of the world, “I am much, much troubled. I have just learned that our beloved land is only a few tiny islands in the great world.”
The grandmother adjusted her big horn spectacles and for a few minutes carefully studied the map.

===Chinese===

启航前夜，我的东京叔父来了，给孩子们带来一包「惜别彩带」——那是些纤细、俏皮、微微发颤的纸带，船起锚的时刻，一头系在船上朋友的手里，一头系在岸上朋友的手里——连着，又别着。
「我要拉一条粉红的给敏子（Toshiko），一条蓝的给阿国（Kuni San），」彩纸卷从纸包里滚出来时，千代嚷道，「再要一条白的给我的老师，一条紫的给——给您，土佐叔父（Uncle Tosa）！给您两条顶漂亮的，颜色随您挑！」
「我要拉一大把红白相间的，给全日本！」花野说。「爱，许多的爱，还有再见；因为我再也不回来了。这里的人我个个爱，可我要永远同奶奶一起，住在《家，甜蜜的家》里。」她一面溜出去，一面轻轻哼着那支曲子，满脸放光。
啊，她哪里梦得到，多年之后她还会回来——不止一回——而且每次心里都装着双份的忠诚：一半给生她的国土，一半给爱的国土——那一片国土上，有她的丈夫、儿女和家。
花野和千代睡下了，我还在料理装箱收尾的零零碎碎；阿铃拎起一件叠好的披肩，要搁在衣匣上层，好合上匣盖。
「这儿还松得很，」她说。「塞一只坐垫正好；可是想想多可笑——美国那样的大国，还千里迢迢带一只我们天天坐的寻常坐垫去。」
她不知道，我那只顶贵重的衣箱箱底，藏着一样东西——若不是在姐姐的土仓里亲眼见过，我做梦也想不到它会在祖母大人房里那只熟惯的火盆之外的任何地方。
那是一只四四方方的蓝锦缎坐垫，旧了，颜色也有些褪。
替它打包远行时，只有我一个人；我的手抚过缎面上丝织的花，心思飘回去——飘回那一天：一个穿木屐的黑发小姑娘嗒嗒地跑进大门，来不及把向全家请安的礼一一躬到，便在祖母面前摊开一本又大又薄的书——祖母坐的，正是这只坐垫。
「祖母大人，」她指着一张彩色世界地图说，「我烦恼得很，很得很。我刚知道，我们心爱的国土，在偌大的世界上，不过是几个小小的岛。」
祖母扶了扶她那副牛角框大眼镜，对着地图细细端详了好几分钟。

===Original===

Then with slow dignity she closed the book.
“It is quite natural, little Etsu-bo, for them to make Japan look small on this map,” she said. “It was made by the people of the black ships. Japan is made large on the Japanese maps of the world.”
“Who are the people of the black ships?” asked the little girl.
“They are the red barbarians who came uninvited to our sacred land. They came in big, black ships that moved without sails.”
“I know. Ishi sings it to me”; and her shrill little voice chanted:

> “They came from a land of darkness,

Giants with hooked nose like mountain imp;

Giants with rough hair, loose and red;

They stole a promise from our sacred master

And danced with joy as they sailed away

To the distant land of darkness.

===Chinese===

然后，她慢慢端严地合上了书。
「小小的津保，这地图把日本画得小，是顶自然的，」她说。「它是黑船来的人画的。在日本人画的世界地图上，日本画得大。」
「黑船来的人是谁呀？」小姑娘问。
「是那些红毛蛮夷，不请自来，闯进我们神圣的国土。他们乘着又大又黑的船来，那船没有帆，自己会走。」
「我知道。阿石唱给我听过」；她尖尖的小嗓音便唱起来：

> 「他们从黑暗之国来，

巨人，钩鼻梁，活像山魈；

巨人，一头红毛，蓬蓬松松；

他们从我们神圣的主公那里偷走了一个约许，

便欢天喜地舞蹈着，

驶回那遥远的黑暗之国。

===Original===

“I wonder why they were called ‘black ships.’ Do you know, Honourable Grandmother?”
“Because far out on the waters they looked like clouds of black smoke rolling nearer and nearer, and they had long, black guns that roared. The red barbarians cared nothing for beauty. They laughed at the Japanese boats, whose sails were made of rich brocade and their oars of carved wood, inlaid with coral and mother-of-pearl. They talked like tradesmen and did not want to learn the hearts of the children of the gods.”
The grandmother stopped and slowly shook her head.
“And after that?” asked the eager little voice. “And after that, Honourable Grandmother?”
“The black ships and the rude barbarians sailed away,” she concluded, with a deep sigh. “But they sailed back many times. They are always sailing. And now the people of our sacred land also talk like tradesmen and no longer are peaceful and content.”
“Will they never be peaceful and content again?” asked the little girl, with anxious eyes. “The honourable teacher said that sailing ships bring lands nearer to each other.”
“Listen!” said the grandmother, holding herself very straight. “Little Granddaughter, unless the red barbarians and the children of the gods learn each other’s hearts, the ships may sail and sail, but the two lands will never be nearer.”
Years passed, and Etsu-bo, the little girl who had listened to the story of the black ships and the red barbarians, herself went sailing on a black ship that moved without sails, to a new home in the distant land of the red barbarians. There she learned that hearts are the same on both sides of the world; but this is a secret that is hidden from the people of the East, and hidden from the people of the West. That makes another chapter to my grandmother’s tale﻿—another chapter, but not the last. The red barbarians and the children of the gods have not yet learned each other’s hearts; to them the secret is still unknown, but the ships are sailing﻿—sailing﻿—

===Chinese===

「我纳闷，为什么管他们叫『黑船』。您知道么，祖母大人？」
「因为在茫茫大水的那头，他们望过去像一团团黑烟，滚滚地越滚越近；他们还有长长的黑炮，吼声震天。红毛蛮夷是不把美放在心上的。他们笑话日本的船——那帆是富丽的锦缎做的，桨是雕花的木头，镶着珊瑚和螺钿。他们说起话来像买卖人，也不肯用心去懂神之子民的心。」
祖母停了停，缓缓摇头。
「后来呢？」那急切的小声音问。「后来呢，祖母大人？」
「黑船和无礼的蛮夷驶走了，」她深深叹一口气，收尾道。「可他们驶回来了一回又一回。他们总在航行。如今呢，我们神圣国土上的人，说话也像买卖人了，再不得安宁，再不得知足了。」
「他们再也不能安宁知足了么？」小姑娘眼巴巴地问。「老师说，帆船会把国土和国土带得相近。」
「你听着！」祖母说，腰身挺得笔直。「小孙女，红毛蛮夷和神之子民若不肯彼此用心相懂，船尽管一程一程地开，两片国土，是永远不会相近的。」
许多年过去，津保——那个听黑船与红毛蛮夷的故事的小姑娘——自己也登上了一条没有帆、自己会走的黑船，驶向红毛蛮夷遥远国土上的一个新家。在那里她懂得了：世界两边的心，原是一样的；只是这个秘密，东方的人看不见，西方的人也看不见。这就成了我祖母的故事的又一章——又一章，却不是末一章。红毛蛮夷和神之子民，还没有学会彼此的心；那个秘密，于他们依旧不晓。可船在航行——航行——
